=== [0:35] 사위질 ===
meaning: Getting set up.
tone: Casual, self-talk, preparatory.
--- word ---
term: 사위질
type: Noun (non-standard)
breakdown: 
meaning: Getting set up
notes: This is not a standard Korean word and is likely a mis-transcription or a playful, made-up term. '사위' means 'son-in-law' and '-질' is a suffix for actions, often with a negative connotation. In the context of the live stream, she is adjusting her camera, so it's interpreted as 'getting set up' or 'fiddling around'.

=== [0:39] 사위질을 하려고 해 ===
meaning: I'm trying to get set up.
tone: Informal, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 사위질을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사위질 + 을 (Object Particle)
meaning: Getting set up (as the object)
notes: 을 is the object particle, marking the preceding noun as the object of the verb. It is used after a noun ending in a consonant. The non-standard nature of '사위질' makes this phrase unusual.
--- word ---
term: 하려고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + -려고
meaning: in order to do / trying to do
notes: This is part of the 'V-려고 하다' grammar pattern, which expresses the speaker's intention or plan to do something. It attaches to a verb stem.
--- word ---
term: 해
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -아/어
meaning: I do / am doing
notes: 해 is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) present tense form of the verb 하다 (to do). It's a contraction of 하여 and is standard in casual conversation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-려고 하다
meaning: Expresses intention or a plan to do something; 'to intend to,' 'to plan to,' 'to be going to.'
example: 영화를 보려고 해요. (I'm planning to watch a movie.)
level: Beginner

=== [0:47] 저 짧게 할 수 있어? ===
meaning: Can I do this for a short time?
tone: Informal, questioning, self-directed.
--- word ---
term: 저
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: I
notes: 저 is the humble form of 'I', typically used in formal speech (존댓말, jondaetmal). However, it's sometimes used in informal speech (반말, banmal) like this to sound a bit softer or more thoughtful, even when the verb endings are informal.
--- word ---
term: 짧게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 짧다 + -게
meaning: shortly, briefly
notes: The suffix -게 is attached to an adjective stem (here, 짧다 - to be short) to change it into an adverb, describing how an action is performed.
--- word ---
term: 할
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 하다 + -ㄹ
meaning: to do
notes: This is part of the '-(으)ㄹ 수 있다' pattern. The -(으)ㄹ ending modifies the following noun '수' (ability), indicating a potential action.
--- word ---
term: 수
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: way, ability
notes: 수 is a dependent noun that means 'ability' or 'possibility'. It is almost always used in the grammar pattern '-(으)ㄹ 수 있다/없다' (can/cannot).
--- word ---
term: 있어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -어?
meaning: is there? / can I?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) question form of 있다 (to exist/have). In this context, it completes the 'can do' pattern.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ 수 있다/없다
meaning: Expresses ability or possibility ('can' or 'cannot').
example: 한국어를 할 수 있어요. (I can speak Korean.)
level: Beginner

=== [0:49] 30분 ===
meaning: 30 minutes.
tone: Informative, direct answer.
--- word ---
term: 30분
type: Noun (time)
breakdown: 30 (Sino-Korean number) + 분 (minute counter)
meaning: 30 minutes
notes: In Korean, minutes (분) are counted using Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼...), while hours (시간) are counted using Native Korean numbers (한, 두, 세...). This is a common point of confusion for learners.

=== [0:51] 짐을 빨리 싸서 이렇게 해야 돼가지고 ===
meaning: Because I have to pack my bags quickly and do this...
tone: Explanatory, informal, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 짐을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 짐 + -을
meaning: luggage, bags
notes: 짐 means 'luggage' or 'belongings'. The particle -을 marks it as the object of the verb 싸다 (to pack).
--- word ---
term: 빨리
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: quickly, fast
notes: A very common adverb derived from the adjective 빠르다 (to be fast).
--- word ---
term: 싸서
type: Verb
breakdown: 싸다 + -아서
meaning: pack and so...
notes: The connective ending -아서/어서 indicates a sequence of events (pack, and then...) or a reason (because I packed...). Here, it implies sequence.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: like this, in this way
notes: A contraction of 이리하게. It refers to doing an action in a way that is being demonstrated or was just mentioned.
--- word ---
term: 해야
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -아야
meaning: have to do
notes: This is part of the grammar pattern -아/어야 하다/되다, which expresses obligation or necessity ('must', 'have to').
--- word ---
term: 돼가지고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + -아 가지고
meaning: because it is...
notes: -아/어 가지고 is a colloquial connective ending common in spoken Korean. It can mean 'because' or indicate a sequence of actions. The sentence trails off, which is natural in conversation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어야 하다/되다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity ('must,' 'have to').
example: 숙제를 해야 돼요. (I have to do my homework.)
level: Beginner

=== [0:57] 이거 무슨 머리이게? ===
meaning: Guess what hairstyle this is?
tone: Playful, teasing, engaging with the audience.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것 (contraction)
meaning: this
notes: 이거 is the colloquial, spoken form of 이것 (this thing). It's used to refer to something near the speaker.
--- word ---
term: 무슨
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: what, what kind of
notes: 무슨 is used before a noun to ask 'what kind of [noun]?'. It differs from the pronoun 뭐 ('what'), which stands alone. For example: '무슨 영화?' (What kind of movie?).
--- word ---
term: 머리이게?
type: Noun + Verb ending
breakdown: 머리 + 이다 + -게?
meaning: hairstyle is it? (Guess!)
notes: The -게? ending creates a playful, quiz-like question, inviting the listener to guess the answer. It's used in informal speech (반말, banmal).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)게?
meaning: A playful, quiz-like question ending used in informal speech to ask 'Guess what [Noun] is?'.
example: 내가 뭘 샀게? (Guess what I bought?)
level: Intermediate

=== [1:03] 별이 다섯 개 ===
meaning: Five stars.
tone: Humorous, referential, self-descriptive.
--- word ---
term: 별이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 별 + -이
meaning: stars (as the subject)
notes: 별 means 'star'. The particle -이 marks it as the subject of the sentence. It is used after a noun ending in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 다섯
type: Number (Native Korean)
breakdown: 
meaning: five
notes: Native Korean numbers (하나, 둘, 셋...) are used for general counting of items, age, and hours.
--- word ---
term: 개
type: Counter
breakdown: 
meaning: item(s)
notes: 개 is the most common, general-purpose counter for objects. The phrase '별이 다섯 개' is a famous catchphrase from an old Korean mattress commercial, which Hwasa uses here to humorously describe her spiky hairstyle.

=== [1:17] 이거 무슨 머리인지 알아봤죠 볼 사람? ===
meaning: Anyone watching recognize what kind of hairstyle this is?
tone: Casual, interactive, playful. Directly asking fans a question.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것
meaning: this thing
notes: A common contraction of 이것 (igeot), meaning 'this thing'. In spoken Korean, contractions like 이거 (this), 그거 (that), and 저거 (that over there) are used far more frequently than their full forms.
--- word ---
term: 무슨
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: what kind of
notes: Used before a noun to ask about its nature or type. It's different from 뭐 (mwo), which means 'what' and stands alone as a pronoun (e.g., 이게 뭐예요? - What is this?).
--- word ---
term: 머리인지
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 머리 + 이다 + -(으)ㄴ지
meaning: what hairstyle it is
notes: This is an embedded question clause. The -(으)ㄴ지 ending attaches to verbs or adjectives to mean 'whether/what/who...'. It's often followed by verbs like 알다 (to know) or 모르다 (to not know).
--- word ---
term: 알아봤죠
type: Verb
breakdown: 알아보다 + -았- + -죠
meaning: you recognized, right?
notes: 알아보다 means 'to recognize' or 'to look into'. The -죠 ending is a contraction of -지요 and is used to seek agreement or confirmation from the listener, similar to '...right?' or '...isn't it?' in English.
--- word ---
term: 볼
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 보다 + -ㄹ
meaning: (the person) who is watching
notes: The -ㄹ/을 ending turns a verb into a future-tense adjective that modifies the following noun. Here, 보다 (to watch) becomes 볼 to modify 사람 (person), meaning 'the person who will watch' or, in this context, 'the person who is currently watching'.
--- word ---
term: 사람?
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: anyone?
notes: Literally 'person'. In this context, it's a shortened question, implying 'Is there any person (who recognized it)?'. This is a very common and natural way to pose questions in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -인지 알다/모르다
meaning: Used to embed a 'what is [Noun]?' question inside a larger sentence. It means 'to know/not know what [Noun] is'.
example: 그 사람이 누구인지 알아요? (Do you know who that person is?)
level: Beginner

term: 맞았어

breakdown: 맞다 + -았- + -어
meaning: was correct
notes: The informal, past-tense conjugation of 맞다 (matda), 'to be correct'. It's a very common expression used to confirm something someone said, like 'That's right!' or 'You got it!'.

=== [1:39] 운전 중에 문자 치면 안 돼 ===
meaning: Don't text while driving.
tone: Informal, cautionary, caring. A friendly warning to a fan.
--- word ---
term: 운전
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: driving
notes: A Sino-Korean noun meaning 'driving'. It can be turned into a verb by adding 하다: 운전하다 (unjeonhada), 'to drive'.
--- word ---
term: 중에
type: Postpositional Phrase
breakdown: 중 + -에
meaning: in the middle of / during
notes: A common phrase attached to nouns to indicate that something is happening 'during' or 'in the middle of' that noun. For example, 수업 중에 (sueop junge) means 'during class'.
--- word ---
term: 문자
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: text message
notes: Refers to a text message (SMS). The verb 'to text' is often expressed as 문자를 보내다 (munjareul bonaeda, 'to send a text') or 문자를 치다 (munjareul chida, 'to type a text'), as used here.
--- word ---
term: 치면
type: Verb + Ending
breakdown: 치다 + -(으)면
meaning: if you type
notes: 치다 (chida) means 'to hit' or 'to type'. The -(으)면 (-(eu)myeon) ending is a common conditional that means 'if' or 'when'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: The most common way to negate a verb or adjective in Korean. It is placed directly before the verb/adjective. For example, 가다 (gada, 'to go') becomes 안 가다 (an gada, 'to not go').
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 + -어
meaning: it is okay
notes: The informal conjugation of 되다 (doeda), which has many meanings, including 'to become' or 'to be okay/permissible'. The phrase 안 돼 (an dwae) is a very common way to say 'No!', 'Don't!', or 'It's not allowed'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -(으)면 안 되다
meaning: Expresses prohibition: 'one must not...' or 'it is not okay to...'. It's a standard and widely used pattern.
example: 여기서 담배 피우면 안 돼요. (You shouldn't smoke here.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:53] 이분들의 의견을 또 이렇게 참고를 해봤습니다 ===
meaning: I took these people's opinions into consideration like this again.
tone: Mock-formal, explanatory, humorous. Using a formal ending in a casual setting.
--- word ---
term: 이분들의
type: Pronoun + Noun + Particles
breakdown: 이 + 분 + -들 + -의
meaning: of these people
notes: 분 (bun) is the honorific counter for people, more polite than 명 (myeong) or 사람 (saram). -들 (-deul) is the plural marker, and -의 (-ui) is the possessive particle.
--- word ---
term: 의견을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 의견 + -을
meaning: opinion (as object)
notes: 의견 (uigyeon) means 'opinion'. The particle -을 (-eul) marks it as the direct object of the verb.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again
notes: A common adverb meaning 'again' or 'also'. It can be placed in various positions in the sentence, but usually comes before the verb.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: like this / in this way
notes: Formed from the adjective 이렇다 (ireota, 'to be like this') and the adverbial ending -게 (-ge). Similarly, 그렇다 -> 그렇게 (like that) and 저렇다 -> 저렇게 (like that over there).
--- word ---
term: 참고를
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 참고 + -를
meaning: reference (as object)
notes: 참고 (chamgo) means 'reference' or 'consideration'. It is often used with the verb 하다 (hada) to mean 'to refer to' or 'to take into account'. The object particle -를 is sometimes omitted in speech (참고 해봤습니다).
--- word ---
term: 해봤습니다
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -아/어 보다 + -았- + -습니다
meaning: I tried doing / I did
notes: This combines the verb 하다 (to do) with the auxiliary verb -아/어 보다 (to try doing). The ending -습니다 (-seumnida) is a formal polite ending. Using it in a casual live stream is a stylistic choice, often for humorous or dramatic effect.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -아/어 보다
meaning: An auxiliary construction that adds the meaning of 'to try doing' or 'to experience' something. It softens the main verb.
example: 김치를 먹어 봤어요. (I have tried kimchi before.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -습니다 / -ㅂ니다
meaning: The formal polite declarative ending, used in formal settings like news broadcasts, presentations, or when speaking to superiors. Hwasa uses it here humorously in a very informal context.
example: 감사합니다. (Thank you.)
level: Beginner

=== [1:57] 쉬는 날이야? ===
meaning: Is it a day off?
tone: Informal, inquisitive. A direct question to the fans.
--- word ---
term: 쉬는
type: Adjective
breakdown: 쉬다 + ~는
meaning: resting
notes: This is the present tense adjectival form of the verb 쉬다 (to rest). The ~는 ending attaches to a verb stem to turn it into an adjective that can modify a noun, like 'the resting day' or 'day for resting'.
--- word ---
term: 날이야?
type: Noun + Copula + Question Ending
breakdown: 날 + 이다 + ~야?
meaning: is it a day?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) question form. 날 (day) ends in a consonant, so it takes ~이야?. If the noun ended in a vowel, it would be ~야? (e.g., 휴가야? - Is it a vacation?). The polite equivalent is 날이에요?.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 + Noun
meaning: Present tense verb modifier. Describes a noun with an action that is currently happening or is a general fact.
example: 지금 읽는 책이 재미있어요. (The book I'm reading now is fun.)
level: Beginner

=== [2:00] 어쩠어? ===
meaning: How was it?
tone: Informal, colloquial, friendly.
--- word ---
term: 어쩠어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 어찌하다 + ~았/었~ + ~어?
meaning: How was it? / What did you do?
notes: This is a colloquial contraction of 어찌하였어?. It's a very casual way of asking 'what happened?' or 'how was your day?'. A more standard but still informal alternative would be 어땠어? (from 어떻다 - to be how).

=== [2:10] 아니 무화과가 없는 거야 이모지가 ===
meaning: No, there's no fig emoji.
tone: Informal, explanatory, slightly exasperated.
--- word ---
term: 아니
type: Interjection/Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: no / wait
notes: Used as an interjection to negate a previous statement or express surprise. It's different from 아니요 (polite 'no') and 아니다 (to not be). Here, it's used to introduce a correction or explanation.
--- word ---
term: 무화과가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 무화과 + ~가
meaning: a fig
notes: 무화과 (muhwagwa) literally means 'flowerless fruit'. The particle ~가 marks it as the subject of the verb 없다 (to not exist). ~가 is used after nouns ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 없는
type: Adjective
breakdown: 없다 + ~는
meaning: that doesn't exist
notes: The adjectival form of 없다 (to not exist/not have). It modifies the following noun, 거 (a contraction of 것, meaning 'thing').
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 것 + 이야
meaning: it is the thing that...
notes: 거야 is a contraction of 것이야. The ~는 거야 ending is extremely common in spoken Korean to state a fact or provide an explanation with a sense of emphasis or discovery. It feels more conversational than a simple statement like 없어요.
--- word ---
term: 이모지가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 이모지 + ~가
meaning: the emoji
notes: A loanword from English 'emoji'. In spoken Korean, it's common to place the subject or topic at the end of the sentence for emphasis, as done here. The standard order would be '아니 이모지에 무화과가 없는 거야'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~는 거야
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to explain a situation or state a fact, often with a nuance of 'The thing is...' or 'It's that...'.
example: 지금 집에 가는 거야. (The thing is, I'm going home now.)
level: Intermediate

=== [2:13] 그래서 뭔가 어금이 비슷한 모과로 해봤어 ===
meaning: So I tried using a quince, which has a similar feel/sound.
tone: Informal, explanatory, detailing a thought process.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / therefore
notes: A fundamental conjunction that connects two sentences, where the first is the cause or reason for the second.
--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: Adverb/Pronoun
breakdown: 무엇인가
meaning: something / somehow / kind of
notes: A contraction of 무엇인가 (something). It's often used as a filler word in conversation to mean 'like', 'kind of', or 'for some reason', making the statement sound less direct or more thoughtful.
--- word ---
term: 어감이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 어감 + ~이
meaning: the feel/nuance (of a word)
notes: 어감 refers to the subtle feeling, sound, or connotation of a word. The particle ~이 marks it as the subject of the verb 비슷하다 (to be similar).
--- word ---
term: 비슷한
type: Adjective
breakdown: 비슷하다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: similar
notes: The adjectival form of 비슷하다 (to be similar). The ~ㄴ ending is attached to adjective stems ending in a vowel to allow them to modify a noun (in this case, 모과).
--- word ---
term: 모과로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 모과 + ~(으)로
meaning: with a quince
notes: 모과 is a quince. The particle ~(으)로 indicates the tool, method, or means used to perform an action. Since 모과 ends in a vowel, ~로 is used.
--- word ---
term: 해봤어
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~아/어 보다 + ~았/었~ + ~어
meaning: I tried doing (it)
notes: The key pattern here is ~아/어 보다, which means 'to try doing' or 'to experience' something. It's combined with 하다 (to do) and put into the informal past tense.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어 보다
meaning: Indicates trying or experiencing an action. It's less about 'effort' and more about 'giving something a go'.
example: 김치를 먹어 봤어요? (Have you ever tried kimchi?)
level: Beginner

=== [2:17] 이거 모과 맞나? ===

breakdown: 이것
meaning: this thing

--- word ---

term: 맞나?

breakdown: 맞다 + ~나?
meaning: I wonder if it's correct
notes: The ~나? ending is used for asking a question to oneself, expressing doubt or wonder. It creates a softer, more speculative tone than a direct question. It's common in monologues or when thinking aloud.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~(으)나?
meaning: A question ending used to express doubt or wonder to oneself. It is used in informal speech and monologues.
example: 지금 비가 오나? (I wonder if it's raining now?)

=== [2:19] 배인가? ===
meaning: Is it a pear?
tone: Informal, speculative, self-correcting.
--- word ---
term: 배인가?
type: Noun + Copula + Question Ending
breakdown: 배 + 이다 + ~ㄴ가?
meaning: I wonder if it's a pear
notes: 배 means 'pear'. The ending ~인가? is the noun version of the self-questioning ending ~나?. It attaches to the copula 이다 (to be) to express wonder about whether something is a certain noun.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 인가?
meaning: A question ending used with nouns to express doubt or wonder to oneself. The equivalent of V/A + ~(으)나? for nouns.
example: 저 사람이 한국 사람인가? (I wonder if that person is Korean.)

=== [2:27] 서양배면 ===
meaning: If it's a Western pear, then...
tone: Informal, trailing off, thinking aloud.
--- word ---
term: 서양배면
type: Noun + Copula + Conditional
breakdown: 서양배 + 이다 + ~(으)면
meaning: if it's a Western pear
notes: This is a contraction of 서양배이면. The conditional ending ~(으)면 means 'if' or 'when'. For nouns, it attaches to the copula 이다 (to be). The sentence is left unfinished, a common feature of natural speech when someone is formulating their next thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + (이)면
meaning: A conditional pattern meaning 'if it is (Noun)'. Use ~이면 after a consonant and ~면 after a vowel.
example: 주말이면 영화를 봐요. (If it's the weekend, I watch movies.)

=== [2:31] 뭔가 그냥 무화과 같이 생겼어 ===

--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: Adverb
breakdown: 뭐 (what) + ~ㄴ가 (uncertainty suffix)
meaning: something, kind of
notes: A contraction of '무엇인가'. Used colloquially to mean 'kind of' or 'for some reason,' adding a sense of vagueness to a statement. It softens the speaker's assertion.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, simply
notes: A very common adverb indicating that there's no special reason for something. For example, '왜 왔어?' (Why did you come?) -> '그냥' (Just because).
--- word ---
term: 무화과
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: fig
notes: Literally 'flowerless fruit' (無花果). Hwasa is using it as a creative description, likely for her spiky hairstyle.
--- word ---
term: 같이
type: Particle
breakdown: 같다 (to be like) + ~이 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: like, as
notes: Attached to a noun to mean 'like [noun]'. It is often interchangeable with '처럼'. For example, '너같이' and '너처럼' both mean 'like you'.
--- word ---
term: 생겼어

breakdown: 생기다 (to look, to be formed) + ~었어 (past tense, informal)
meaning: it looks
notes: The informal past tense of 생기다. Although it's a past tense form, it's used to describe a current, resulting state of appearance. '잘생겼어' means 'He is handsome,' not 'He was handsome'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 같이 생기다
meaning: To look like [Noun]. A common pattern for describing physical appearance.
example: 그 강아지는 곰같이 생겼어. (That puppy looks like a bear.)

=== [2:34] 뭔지 알지? ===

=== [3:02] 이 생머리뿐이더라고 ===

=== [3:16] 이거 우현이가 선물해줬어 ===

term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것 (this thing)

notes: A colloquial contraction of 이것 (this thing). In spoken Korean, 이거, 그거 (that thing), and 저거 (that thing over there) are used far more often than their full forms.

notes: This uses the pattern V + ~아/어 주다, which indicates an action performed for someone's benefit. 선물하다 (to gift) becomes 선물해주다 (to gift for someone). The informal past tense ~었어 is then added.

=== [3:19] 고맙지 ===

--- word ---
term: 고맙지

meaning: I'm thankful, of course

=== [3:21] 여기까지 와가지고 ===
meaning: Coming all the way here...
tone: Casual, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 여기까지
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 여기 + ~까지
meaning: to here / all the way here
notes: 여기 means 'here.' The particle ~까지 means 'to,' 'until,' or 'as far as,' marking a destination or the extent of something in space or time.
--- word ---
term: 와가지고
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: came and... / because he came...
notes: This is a colloquial form of ~아/어 와서. The pattern V + ~아/어 가지고 has two main uses in spoken Korean: 1) indicating a sequence of actions ('do A and then B'), and 2) giving a reason ('because of A, B happened'). Here, it implies both: 'He came all this way and (gave me a gift)...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: A connective pattern used in speech to indicate a sequence of actions or a reason/cause. It's a more colloquial alternative to ~아/어서.
example: 숙제를 다 해 가지고 지금 놀고 있어요. (I'm playing now because I finished all my homework.)

=== [3:25] 내가 돈 쓰는 건 괜찮은데 ===
meaning: It's okay for me to spend money, but...
tone: Casual, explanatory, contrasting.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + ~가
meaning: I (subject)
notes: While 나는 sets 'me' as the general topic, 내가 (using the subject particle ~가) specifically marks 'I' as the one performing the action (spending money). It adds a slight emphasis on the doer of the action.
--- word ---
term: 돈
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: money
notes: A fundamental noun in Korean.
--- word ---
term: 쓰는
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 쓰다 + ~는
meaning: spending / that is spent
notes: This is the present tense adjective form of the verb 쓰다 ('to use, to spend'). The ~는 ending turns the verb into a modifier that can describe a noun. Here, 돈 쓰는 modifies the following noun 건.
--- word ---
term: 건
type: Noun (contracted)
breakdown: 것 + 은
meaning: the thing / the act of
notes: 건 is a common spoken contraction of 것은. 것 is a pronoun meaning 'thing' or 'fact,' and ~은 is the topic particle. The phrase 돈 쓰는 것 means 'the act of spending money.'
--- word ---
term: 괜찮은데
type: Adjective
breakdown: 괜찮다 + ~은데
meaning: it's okay, but...
notes: The ~(으)ㄴ/는데 ending is used to provide background information or context for the following clause. It often implies a contrast or a reason. Here, it sets up the contrast with the next sentence: 'It's fine when *I* spend money, *but*...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 것
meaning: This pattern, called nominalization, turns a verb into a noun phrase. It means 'the act of doing [verb]' or 'the thing that one does.'
example: 매일 운동하는 것은 건강에 좋아요. (Exercising every day is good for your health.)

=== [3:37] 나한테 어울리는 걸 샀대 ===

term: 나한테
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + ~한테
meaning: to me / for me
notes: ~한테 is a colloquial particle meaning 'to' or 'from' a person. It's the spoken equivalent of the more formal ~에게. In the context of 어울리다 ('to suit'), it means 'suits me.'
--- word ---
term: 어울리는
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 어울리다 + ~는
meaning: that suits / that matches

meaning: the thing (object)

--- word ---

type: Verb (quoted speech)
breakdown: 사다 + ~았~ + ~다고 해
meaning: he said he bought
notes: ~대 is a contraction of ~다고 해, which is used for reported or quoted speech. It means 'someone said that...'. The base verb is 사다 ('to buy'), in the past tense 샀다. So, 샀대 means '(He) said (he) bought.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~대(요)
meaning: A colloquial ending for indirect quotation, short for ~다고 하다/해요. It's used to report something you heard someone else say.
example: 친구가 오늘 춥대. (My friend said it's cold today.)

notes: 우현 is a name. The particle ~이 is often attached to names ending in a consonant in casual speech when the name is mentioned. It adds a sense of familiarity and friendliness and is very common when talking about or to friends.

=== [3:44] 별이 언니 ===
meaning: Byul-i unnie.
tone: Casual, naming someone.
--- word ---
term: 별이
type: Noun + Suffix
breakdown: 별 (Byul) + 이
meaning: Byul
notes: 별 (Byul) is the name of her MAMAMOO bandmate, Moonbyul. The suffix ~이 is often attached to names ending in a consonant to make them sound more affectionate and easier to pronounce. It is not the subject particle 이/가 in this context.
--- word ---
term: 언니
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Older sister
notes: A term used by a female speaker to refer to an older female friend, relative, or senior colleague. Hwasa uses it for her older bandmate, Moonbyul, showing their close relationship. Fans also call Hwasa '언니'.

=== [3:46] 현재는 별이 언니 현 매니저고요 ===
meaning: Currently, he's Byul-i unnie's current manager, and...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 현재는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 현재 (hyeonjae) + 는 (neun)
meaning: Currently, as for now
notes: 현재 means 'the present' or 'currently'. The topic particle 는/은 is used here to set the topic of the sentence, implying a contrast with the past, which she discusses in the next sentence.
--- word ---
term: 별이
type: Noun
breakdown: 별 (Byul) + 이
meaning: Byul's
notes: In casual speech, the possessive particle 의 (ui), meaning 'of' or 's, is often omitted. Here, '별이 언니' functions as a possessive phrase meaning 'Byul-i unnie's'.
--- word ---
term: 언니
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: older sister
notes: Used by a female speaker to refer to an older female. Here it refers to her bandmate, Moonbyul.
--- word ---
term: 현
type: Prefix
breakdown: 
meaning: current
notes: 현 (現) is a Sino-Korean prefix meaning 'current' or 'present'. It attaches directly to nouns, as in 현 대통령 (current president).
--- word ---
term: 매니저고요
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 매니저 (maenijeo) + 이고 (igo) + 요 (yo)
meaning: is a manager, and...
notes: This is a combination of 매니저 (manager), the connective form of the copula 이다 (to be) which is 이고 (is, and), and the politeness particle 요. The ending ~고요 is often used to add information or to trail off a sentence while still being slightly polite, even within a generally informal (반말) context.

=== [3:49] 예전에 이제 우리 마마무 할 때는 또 ===
meaning: Back in the day, when we were doing MAMAMOO stuff, well...
tone: Informal, nostalgic, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 예전에
type: Adverb
breakdown: 예전 (yejeon) + 에 (e)
meaning: In the past, before
notes: 예전 refers to 'the old days' or 'a time in the past'. It's combined with the time-marking particle 에. It has a more distant, nostalgic feel than '전에' (before).
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, well
notes: While 이제 literally means 'now', in conversation it's frequently used as a filler word to transition between thoughts, similar to 'well', 'so', or 'you know' in English.
--- word ---
term: 우리
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: we/our
notes: Koreans often use 우리 (we/our) in contexts where English speakers would use 'my', such as 우리 집 (my house) or 우리 엄마 (my mom), emphasizing a sense of belonging to a group.
--- word ---
term: 마마무
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: MAMAMOO
notes: The name of Hwasa's K-pop group.
--- word ---
term: 할
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 하다 (hada) + ~ㄹ
meaning: that (we) were doing
notes: This is the future/prospective modifier form of the verb 하다 (to do). It modifies the following noun 때 (time), creating the meaning 'the time of doing' or 'when we were doing'.
--- word ---
term: 때는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 때 (ttae) + 는 (neun)
meaning: at the time
notes: 때 means 'time' or 'when'. The topic particle 는 is attached to emphasize 'at that specific time' in contrast to the present.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: also, again
notes: Here, 또 is used as a conversational filler to add a bit of emphasis or to indicate a shift in thought, like 'and also' or 'then'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Indicates the time 'when' a verb's action takes place. It connects a verb clause to the noun 때 (time).
example: 공부할 때 음악을 들어요. (I listen to music when I study.)
level: Beginner

=== [3:54] 애기였는데 말이지 ===
meaning: he was just a baby, you know.
tone: Informal, affectionate, reminiscent.
--- word ---
term: 애기였는데
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 애기 (aegi) + 이었다 (ieotda) + ~는데 (neunde)
meaning: was a baby, and/but
notes: 애기 is a common, slightly cuter pronunciation of 아기 (agi), meaning 'baby'. The ending ~는데 provides background information for a following, unstated thought (e.g., '...but now he's all grown up').
--- word ---
term: 말이지
type: Phrase
breakdown: 말이다 (marida) + ~지 (ji)
meaning: you know / I mean
notes: A very common colloquial phrase used at the end of a sentence for emphasis or to seek implicit agreement from the listener. It adds a friendly, 'I'm telling you' nuance.
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + ~(이)었는데 / V/A + ~았/었는데
meaning: A past-tense connective ending used to provide background information or context for the main clause, which is often implied. It can also show a contrast between the past and present.
example: 어제는 날씨가 좋았는데 오늘은 비가 와요. (The weather was good yesterday, but it's raining today.)
level: Intermediate

=== [4:04] 이걸 보고 내 생각이 났대 ===
meaning: He said he thought of me when he saw this.
tone: Informal, reporting what someone said.
--- word ---
term: 이걸
type: Pronoun + Particle (Contracted)
breakdown: 이것 (igeot) + 을 (eul)
meaning: this thing
notes: 이걸 is a common spoken contraction of 이것을, where 이것 means 'this thing' and 을 is the object particle.
--- word ---
term: 보고
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 (boda) + ~고 (go)
meaning: saw and...
notes: The ~고 ending connects two actions sequentially. Here it means 'he saw this and then...' (he thought of me).
--- word ---
term: 내
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 나 (na) + 의 (ui)
meaning: my
notes: 내 is the possessive form of 나 (I/me, informal). It is a contraction of 나의.
--- word ---
term: 생각이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 생각 (saenggak) + 이 (i)
meaning: thought
notes: The phrase '생각이 나다' is a set expression meaning 'to think of', 'to recall', or 'to come to mind'.
--- word ---
term: 났대
type: Verb + Ending (Contracted)
breakdown: 나다 (nada) + ~았~ (past tense) + ~다고 해 (dago hae)
meaning: (he) said it came up
notes: This is a casual reported speech form. The ending ~대 is a contraction of ~다고 해, meaning 'he/she/they say that...'. So, 생각이 났대 means 'He said he thought of (me)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~대 (Reported Speech)
meaning: A casual, contracted form of ~다고 해(요), used to report something you heard from someone else. It attaches to verb stems.
example: 친구가 영화가 재미있었대. (My friend said the movie was fun.)
level: Intermediate

=== [4:09] 내일 한국 가 ===

breakdown:

notes: 내일 can function as both a noun ('Tomorrow is my birthday') and an adverb ('I'm leaving tomorrow'), as it does here.

breakdown: 
meaning: Korea

=== [4:23] 무서워 ===
meaning: I'm scared.
tone: Informal, expressive. A simple, emotional statement.
--- word ---
term: 무서워
type: Adjective
breakdown: 무섭다 + ~어
meaning: scary, scared
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말/banmal) conjugation of the adjective 무섭다 (museopda), meaning 'to be scary.' It follows the 'ㅂ' irregular conjugation rule: the 'ㅂ' is dropped and '워' is added when a vowel follows. The informal polite form would be 무서워요 (museowoyo).

=== [4:26] 할 게 너무 많아 ===
meaning: There's so much to do.
tone: Informal, feeling overwhelmed.
--- word ---
term: 할
type: Verb (future determiner form)
breakdown: 하다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: to do / that I will do
notes: This is the future determiner form of 하다 (hada), which modifies the following noun (게). It turns the verb into a description of the noun, indicating a future action related to it.
--- word ---
term: 게
type: Noun + particle (contracted)
breakdown: 것 + 이
meaning: thing (as subject)
notes: 게 (ge) is a very common colloquial contraction of 것이 (geosi), which is 'thing' (것) plus the subject particle (이). In spoken Korean, 게 is used far more often than the full form.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, too, very
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too much' in a negative sense, in modern colloquial Korean, 너무 (neomu) is widely used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative contexts, much like 'so' in English (e.g., 너무 좋아 - 'so good').
--- word ---
term: 많아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 많다 + ~아
meaning: is a lot, are many
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말/banmal) conjugation of 많다 (manta), meaning 'to be many/much.' The informal polite form is 많아요 (manayo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 것
meaning: Used to nominalize a verb phrase, creating a noun that means 'thing to (do),' 'something to (do),' or 'the fact that one will (do).'
example: 먹을 게 없어. (meogeul ge eopseo.) - There's nothing to eat.
level: Beginner

=== [4:28] 그래서 아마 난 또 짐을 싸고 ===
meaning: So I'll probably pack my bags again, and...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~서
meaning: so, therefore
notes: 그래서 (geuraeseo) connects two clauses, indicating that the first clause is the reason or cause for the second. It's a fundamental connective word for showing logical sequence.
--- word ---
term: 아마
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: probably, maybe
notes: 아마 (ama) expresses probability or conjecture. It is often paired with a verb ending that also indicates uncertainty, such as ~(으)ㄹ 것이다 (e.g., 아마 비가 올 거예요 - 'It will probably rain').
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + particle (contracted)
breakdown: 나 + 는
meaning: I (as the topic)
notes: 난 (nan) is the spoken contraction of 나는 (naneun). 나 (na) is the informal pronoun for 'I,' and 는 (neun) is the topic marking particle. Using contractions is a key feature of natural, spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again, also
notes: 또 (tto) indicates repetition. It's similar to 다시 (dasi), but 또 often implies repeating a similar type of action, whereas 다시 can mean re-doing the exact same action from the beginning.
--- word ---
term: 짐을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 짐 + 을
meaning: luggage (as object)
notes: 짐을 싸다 (jimeul ssada) is a common set phrase that means 'to pack one's bags/luggage.'
--- word ---
term: 싸고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 싸다 + ~고
meaning: pack and...
notes: The ~고 (~go) ending is a versatile connector used to link actions in chronological order. Here, the sentence trails off, implying that other actions will follow packing.

=== [4:41] 이것저것 뭘 하고 ===
meaning: do this and that, and...
tone: Informal, listing activities, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 이것저것
type: Pronoun/Adverb
breakdown: 이것 + 저것
meaning: this and that, various things
notes: 이것저것 (igeotjeogeot) is a compound word used to refer to a variety of unspecified items or tasks. It's a very common colloquial expression for when you don't need to list things specifically.
--- word ---
term: 뭘
type: Pronoun + particle (contracted)
breakdown: 뭐 + 를
meaning: what (as object)
notes: 뭘 (mwol) is a contraction of 뭐를 (mworeul), the object form of 'what.' In a statement like this, '이것저것 뭘 하다' means 'to do this and that,' with 뭘 acting as a placeholder for 'something.'
--- word ---
term: 하고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 + ~고
meaning: do and...
notes: The ~고 (~go) ending connects clauses sequentially. The sentence is left unfinished, suggesting a continuation of a list of tasks she has to do.

=== [4:44] 이번에는 최대한 ===
meaning: This time, as much as possible...
tone: Informal, determined, setting up a goal.
--- word ---
term: 이번에는
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 이번 + 에 + 는
meaning: this time (specifically)
notes: The combination of the time particle 에 (e) and the topic/contrast particle 는 (neun) emphasizes 'this time,' contrasting it with previous or future times.
--- word ---
term: 최대한
type: Adverb
breakdown: 最大한
meaning: as much as possible, to the maximum
notes: 최대한 (choedaehan) is a Sino-Korean word (from Hanja) meaning 'to the greatest extent.' It's used to express the intention to apply maximum effort. Its opposite is 최소한 (choesohan), 'at the minimum, at least.'

=== [4:48] 뭔가를 좀 빠르게 처리를 해보고 싶어 ===
meaning: I want to try to handle things a bit more quickly.
tone: Informal, expressing a desire or goal.
--- word ---
term: 뭔가를
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 뭔가 + 를
meaning: something (as object)
notes: 뭔가 (mwonga) means 'something,' referring to an unspecified thing or matter. It's a contraction of 무엇인가 (mueosinga).
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a little, a bit
notes: 좀 (jom) is a contraction of 조금 (jogeum). Besides meaning 'a little,' it's often used to soften requests or statements, making them sound more polite or less abrupt.
--- word ---
term: 빠르게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 빠르다 + ~게
meaning: quickly
notes: The ~게 (~ge) suffix attaches to an adjective stem to transform it into an adverb, similar to adding '-ly' in English. 빠르다 (ppareuda, 'to be fast') becomes 빠르게 (ppareuge, 'quickly').
--- word ---
term: 처리를
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 처리 + 를
meaning: handling, processing
notes: 처리 (cheori) is a noun for 'processing' or 'handling.' The phrase 처리를 하다 (cheorireul hada) means 'to handle/process something.'
--- word ---
term: 해보고
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~아/어 보다 + ~고
meaning: try doing and
notes: This combines the verb 하다 (hada) with the grammar pattern ~아/어 보다 (~a/eo boda), which means 'to try doing' something to see how it goes. It implies experimentation or giving something a shot.
--- word ---
term: 싶어
type: Auxiliary adjective
breakdown: 싶다 + ~어
meaning: want to
notes: The pattern 'V-고 싶다' (V-go sipda) is used to express the speaker's desire to do the action of the verb. This is the informal, non-polite (반말/banmal) form.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어 보고 싶다
meaning: Expresses the desire 'to try' doing something. It combines the 'to try' pattern (~아/어 보다) with the 'to want' pattern (~고 싶다).
example: 한국에 가 보고 싶어. (hanguge ga bogo sipeo.) - I want to try going to Korea.
level: Intermediate

=== [4:52] 그래서 비행기에서 굴어떨어질 수 있도록 ===
meaning: So that I can just crash on the plane...
tone: Informal, explanatory, setting up a reason.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~서
meaning: so, therefore
notes: Connects the previous statement (wanting to finish work quickly) as the reason for the action described in the next sentence (working her body hard).
--- word ---
term: 비행기에서
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 비행기 + 에서
meaning: on the airplane
notes: The particle 에서 (eseo) marks the location where an action takes place. Use 에서 for actions and 에 (e) for static location (e.g., 집에 있어요 - 'I am at home').
--- word ---
term: 굴어떨어질
type: Verb (future determiner form)
breakdown: 굴어떨어지다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: that can crash/pass out
notes: 굴어떨어지다 (gureotteoreojida) is a highly colloquial and vivid verb. Literally 'to roll and fall,' it's used figuratively to mean 'to pass out from exhaustion' or 'to crash' into sleep.
--- word ---
term: 수
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: way, ability
notes: 수 (su) is a dependent noun used almost exclusively in the grammar pattern ~(으)ㄹ 수 있다/없다 to mean 'can/cannot' do something.
--- word ---
term: 있도록
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 있다 + ~도록
meaning: so that one can
notes: The ~도록 (~dorok) ending expresses purpose or reason, meaning 'so that' or 'in order to.' The full pattern here, ~(으)ㄹ 수 있도록, means 'so that (I) can...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 수 있도록
meaning: A pattern used to express the purpose of an action, meaning 'so that one can (verb).' The main clause states the action taken to achieve this purpose.
example: 들을 수 있도록 크게 말해주세요. (deureul su itdorok keuge malhaejuseyo.) - Please speak loudly so that I can hear.
level: Intermediate

=== [4:55] 몸을 엄청 굴려야지 ===
meaning: I have to really work my body hard.
tone: Informal, self-motivating, determined.
--- word ---
term: 몸을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 몸 + 을
meaning: body (as object)
notes: 몸 (mom) means 'body.' The phrase 몸을 굴리다 (momeul gullida) is an idiom meaning 'to work oneself hard' or 'to push one's body to the limit.'
--- word ---
term: 엄청
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, extremely
notes: 엄청 (eomcheong) is a colloquial adverb for emphasis, similar to 'really' or 'incredibly.' It's very common in casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 굴려야지
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 굴리다 + ~어야지
meaning: have to work (it) hard
notes: The ending ~어야지 (~eoyaji) is an informal expression of intention or obligation, often said to oneself. It's like a personal resolution: 'I must...' or 'I should...'. It's a combination of the obligation form ~어야 하다 and the ending ~지.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~어야지 / ~아야지
meaning: Expresses a personal resolution or a sense of obligation, often as a monologue or self-encouragement. It's softer and more personal than the standard obligation form ~어야 한다.
example: 내일 일찍 일어나야지. (naeil iljjik ireonayaji.) - I've got to wake up early tomorrow.
level: Intermediate

=== [4:57] 앨범이라니 ===
meaning: An album, you say?
tone: Informal, surprised, reacting to a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 앨범이라니
type: noun + ending
breakdown: 앨범 (album) + -이라니 (ending for surprise)
meaning: An album, you say?!
notes: The ending -(이)라니 is used to express surprise, disbelief, or sometimes disapproval about a statement the speaker just heard or a fact they just learned. It attaches to nouns. For verbs/adjectives, -다니 is used. Example: '그가 가수라니!' (To think he's a singer!)
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)라니
meaning: Expresses surprise or disbelief upon hearing something about the noun. It's like saying 'To think that it's [Noun]!' or '[Noun], you say?!'.
example: 이게 선물이라니? (This is a gift, you say? I can't believe it!)
level: Intermediate

=== [5:05] 드디어 세상이 올바르게 돌아간다 ===
meaning: Finally, the world is running correctly.
tone: Informal, dramatic, reading a fan's comment aloud.
--- word ---
term: 드디어
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: finally, at last
notes: Used to express that something long-awaited has finally happened. It carries a sense of anticipation being met.
--- word ---
term: 세상이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 세상 (world) + -이 (subject particle)
meaning: the world
notes: 세상 means 'world'. The particle -이 marks it as the subject of the sentence. -이 is used after nouns ending in a consonant, while -가 is used after nouns ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 올바르게
type: adverb
breakdown: 올바르다 (to be correct) + -게 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: correctly, properly
notes: The -게 suffix is a common way to turn descriptive verbs (adjectives) into adverbs, describing how an action is performed. It's similar to adding '-ly' in English.
--- word ---
term: 돌아간다
type: verb
breakdown: 돌아가다 (to turn, go back) + -ㄴ다 (plain form ending)
meaning: is turning, is running
notes: 돌아가다 is a compound verb from 돌다 (to turn) and 가다 (to go). The -ㄴ다/-는다 ending is the plain form present tense, often used in writing, diaries, or when speaking to oneself. In casual speech among friends, it can give a declarative or narrative feel.

=== [5:08] 뭔 소리야 ===
meaning: What are you talking about?
tone: Informal, playful, dismissive.
--- word ---
term: 뭔
type: determiner
breakdown: 무슨 (what kind of) -> 뭔 (contraction)
meaning: what
notes: 뭔 is a very common colloquial contraction of 무슨 (what, what kind of). It's used before a noun.
--- word ---
term: 소리야
type: noun + verb ending
breakdown: 소리 (sound) + -이야 (informal 'to be')
meaning: talk is it?
notes: The entire phrase '뭔 소리야?' is a fixed expression meaning 'What are you talking about?' or 'What nonsense is this?'. 소리 literally means 'sound', but here it means 'talk'. -이야 is the informal (반말) form of 이다 (to be) used after a noun ending in a vowel.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 뭔 소리야?
meaning: A very common, informal phrase meaning 'What are you talking about?' or 'What's that supposed to mean?'. It expresses confusion or dismisses something as nonsense.
example: 갑자기 그만둔다고? 뭔 소리야? (You're quitting suddenly? What are you talking about?)
level: Beginner

=== [5:09] 지금도 올바르게 돌아가고 있어? ===

tone: Informal, rhetorical question, playfully challenging a fan.
--- word ---
term: 지금도
type: adverb + particle
breakdown: 지금 (now) + -도 (also, even)
meaning: even now, now too
notes: 지금 means 'right now', this very moment. It's often contrasted with 이제, which means 'now' in the sense of a new situation starting from this point. The particle -도 adds the meaning of 'too' or 'even'.
--- word ---
term: 올바르게
type: adverb
breakdown: 올바르다 (to be correct) + -게 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: correctly
notes: The -게 suffix changes an adjective into an adverb, modifying the following verb.
--- word ---

type: verb
breakdown: 돌아가다 (to turn, go back) + -고 있다 (present progressive)
meaning: is turning
notes: This is the first part of the -고 있다 grammar pattern, which expresses a continuous action, similar to the '-ing' form in English.
--- word ---
term: 있어?
type: auxiliary verb
breakdown: 있다 (to be) + -어? (informal question ending)
meaning: is it?
notes: This completes the '-고 있다' pattern. The -어? ending makes it an informal (반말) question.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + -고 있다
meaning: Expresses a continuing action or state, equivalent to the present progressive (-ing) in English.
example: 지금 책을 읽고 있어요. (I am reading a book now.)
level: Beginner

=== [5:11] 그래 안 그래? ===

--- word ---

type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 is a negation adverb placed before a verb or adjective. It's the most common way to form short negatives in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 그래?
type: verb
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so) -> 그래?
meaning: Isn't it so?
notes: The phrase '그래 안 그래?' is a very common tag question used to strongly seek agreement, similar to '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English.

=== [5:36] 그러니까 ===
meaning: So...
tone: Informal, transitional, connecting thoughts.
--- word ---
term: 그러니까
type: Conjunction / Adverb
breakdown: 그렇다 + -(으)니까
meaning: So / Therefore / That's why
notes: A very common conversational connector. While it's based on the grammar pattern -(으)니까 (because/so), in speech it often functions like "So...", "Right...", or "I mean..." to connect thoughts or agree before elaborating. It's less about strict logical consequence and more about conversational flow.

=== [5:39] 난 도대체 어느 시간에 살고 있었던 거야 ===
meaning: What on earth time zone was I living in?
tone: Informal, rhetorical, expressing confusion about the time difference.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + 는 (topic particle)
meaning: I / As for me
notes: 난 is the common spoken contraction of 나는. Using the topic particle 는 here emphasizes 'I' as the subject of the confusion, contrasting her situation with her audience's.
--- word ---
term: 도대체
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: On earth / In the world
notes: An adverb that adds strong emphasis to a question, often expressing frustration, disbelief, or intense curiosity. It's similar to saying "what on earth," "why in the world," or "how the heck." It almost always appears with a question word like 어느 (which), 뭐 (what), or 왜 (why).
--- word ---
term: 어느
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: Which / What
notes: Used before a noun to ask 'which' or 'what'. 어느 시간 literally means 'which time,' referring to the time zone she feels like she's in.
--- word ---
term: 시간에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 시간 (time) + -에 (time/location particle)
meaning: in/at a time
notes: The particle -에 is used to mark a specific point in time or a location. Here, it specifies 'in' which time.
--- word ---
term: 살고
type: Verb
breakdown: 살다 (to live) + -고
meaning: living
notes: This is the stem of the present progressive form -고 있다, which means 'to be doing' an action. 살고 있다 means 'to be living'.
--- word ---
term: 있었던
type: Auxiliary Verb + Ending
breakdown: 있다 (to be) + -었- (past tense) + -던 (retrospective modifier)
meaning: which was...
notes: This combination creates a past progressive modifier. -고 있었다 means 'was living'. Adding -던 turns the whole phrase into an adjective that describes a noun, with a nuance of recalling or looking back on that past state.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 거 (thing/fact) + -이야 (informal copula)
meaning: is the thing that...
notes: A very common informal sentence ending, contracted from 것이다. The pattern -(으)ㄴ/는 거야 is used to state a fact, provide an explanation, or ask a question with a sense of discovery or emphasis, making the tone conversational.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 있었던 + Noun
meaning: A grammar pattern used to describe a noun based on an action that was in progress at some point in the past. It combines the past progressive (-고 있었다) with the retrospective adjectival ending (-던).
example: 어제 내가 보고 있었던 영화 (The movie I was watching yesterday).
level: Intermediate

=== [6:00] 여기는 11시 40분인데 딱 내가 오는 시간 ===
meaning: It's 11:40 here, which is exactly the time I come on.
tone: Informal, explanatory, setting the scene.
--- word ---
term: 여기는
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 여기 (here) + 는 (topic particle)
meaning: Here is
notes: 여기 means 'here' or 'this place'. The topic particle 는/은 marks 'here' as the topic of the sentence, contrasting it with another place (like Korea). It's like saying, 'As for here...'.
--- word ---
term: 11시
type: noun
breakdown: 열한 (11) + 시 (hour)
meaning: 11 o'clock
notes: Time in Korean uses two number systems. Native Korean numbers (하나, 둘, 셋...) are used for the hour (시), while Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼...) are used for minutes (분) and seconds (초).
--- word ---
term: 40분인데
type: noun + verb ending
breakdown: 사십분 (40 minutes) + 이다 (to be) + ~ㄴ데 (connective ending)
meaning: it's 40 minutes, and...
notes: The ~ㄴ/은/는데 ending is extremely common. It connects two clauses, where the first clause provides background information or context for the second. Here, 'It's 11:40' is the context for 'it's the time I come on'.
--- word ---
term: 딱
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: exactly, perfectly
notes: 딱 is a versatile adverb that implies something fits perfectly, happens at the exact right moment, or is just right. It adds a sense of precision or satisfaction.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 (I, casual) + 가 (subject particle)
meaning: I
notes: 내가 is the casual way to say 'I' as the subject of a sentence. It's a contraction of 나 + 가. The formal equivalent is 제가 (저 + 가).
--- word ---
term: 오는
type: verb modifier
breakdown: 오다 (to come) + ~는 (present tense modifier)
meaning: that comes
notes: This is how you turn a verb into an adjective to describe a noun. The ~는 ending attaches to action verb stems to describe a noun in the present tense. For example, 먹는 사람 (a person who is eating).
--- word ---
term: 시간
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: time
notes: 시간 can mean 'time' in general, a specific 'time' (like 2 o'clock), or a duration of 'hours'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 + Noun
meaning: Modifies a noun with a present-tense action verb, meaning 'Noun that is V-ing' or 'Noun that V-s'.
example: 지금 읽는 책은 재미있어요. (The book I'm reading now is fun.)
level: Beginner

=== [6:04] 너무 좋은 시간이야 ===
meaning: It's such a good time.
tone: Informal, pleased, expressing satisfaction.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very
notes: Traditionally, 너무 meant 'too much' in a negative sense. However, in modern colloquial Korean, it's very commonly used to mean 'very' or 'so' in a positive sense, similar to 'so good' in English.
--- word ---
term: 좋은
type: adjective modifier
breakdown: 좋다 (to be good) + ~은 (modifier ending)
meaning: good
notes: To use a descriptive verb (adjective) like 좋다 to modify a noun, you add ~ㄴ/은 to the stem. Stems ending in a vowel take ~ㄴ (e.g., 예쁘다 -> 예쁜), and stems ending in a consonant take ~은 (e.g., 좋다 -> 좋은).
--- word ---
term: 시간이야
type: noun + verb ending
breakdown: 시간 (time) + 이다 (to be) + ~이야 (casual ending)
meaning: it's the time
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) way of saying 'is a time'. The ending ~이야 is used after a noun ending in a consonant, while ~야 is used after a vowel (e.g., '사과야' - It's an apple).

pattern: V + ~아야/어야겠다
meaning: Expresses a speaker's intention, resolution, or realization that they should do something. It's a personal feeling or thought.

=== [6:14] 그래? 미국에서 살아야 되나? ===
meaning: Really? Should I live in the US?
tone: Informal, questioning, reacting to a fan.
--- word ---
term: 그래?
type: expression
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so) -> 그래 (casual form) + ?
meaning: Really? Is that so?
notes: A very common conversational reaction. It can express surprise, agreement, or skepticism depending on intonation. It's the casual form of 그래요?.
--- word ---
term: 미국에서
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 미국 (USA) + 에서 (location particle)
meaning: in the US
notes: The particle 에서 marks the location where an action takes place. It's different from 에, which marks a static location or destination. Since 'living' (살다) is an action, 에서 is used.
--- word ---
term: 살아야
type: verb conjugation
breakdown: 살다 (to live) + ~아야 되다/하다 (must/should)
meaning: have to live
notes: This is the first part of the ~아야/어야 되다/하다 grammar pattern, which expresses obligation or necessity. The full pattern is completed by 되나? in this sentence.
--- word ---
term: 되나?
type: verb ending
breakdown: 되다 (to be okay, to work) + ~나? (casual question ending)
meaning: I wonder if I should...?
notes: The ~나(요)? ending is a casual way to ask a question, often to oneself or when pondering something. It can feel softer or more speculative than a standard ~아/어요? question.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아야/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, equivalent to 'must' or 'have to'.
example: 내일 시험이 있어서 공부해야 돼요. (I have an exam tomorrow, so I have to study.)
level: Beginner

level: Beginner

=== [6:32] 누가 그랬어 ===
meaning: Who said that?
tone: Informal, playful, demanding an answer.
--- word ---
term: 누가
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 누구 (who) + 가 (subject particle)
meaning: Who
notes: 누가 is the subject form of 'who'. When 누구 is the subject of the sentence, it contracts with the particle 가 to become 누가. A common mistake is to say '누구는' when '누가' is needed.
--- word ---
term: 그랬어
type: verb
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so, to say so) + ~었어 (past tense, casual)
meaning: said that / did that
notes: 그렇다 is a very versatile verb. In this context, 그랬어 refers back to the previous action (a fan saying 'cancel that') and means 'Who said/did that?'. It's the casual past tense form of 그래요.

=== [6:33] 실명까? ===
meaning: Should I reveal your real name?
tone: Informal, joking, mock-threatening.
--- word ---
term: 실명까?
type: slang expression
breakdown: 실명 (real name) + 까다 (to peel, to expose) + ~ㄹ까? (question ending)
meaning: Reveal the real name?
notes: This is a slang expression. 까다 literally means 'to peel', but here it's used colloquially to mean 'to expose' or 'to reveal' something hidden, like someone's identity. The ~(으)ㄹ까? ending makes it a speculative question, as if she's wondering aloud, 'Hmm, should I...?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ까(요)?
meaning: A question ending used to make a suggestion ('Shall we...?') or to express supposition ('I wonder if...?').
example: 우리 영화 볼까? (Shall we watch a movie?)
level: Beginner

=== [6:36] 누구야? ===
meaning: Who is it?
tone: Informal, questioning, direct.
--- word ---
term: 누구야?
type: Pronoun + verb ending
breakdown: 누구 (who) + -이야 (is/are, informal ending)
meaning: Who is it?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) way to ask 'Who is it?'. It's formed by attaching the informal copula '-(이)야' to the pronoun '누구'. The polite form would be '누구세요?' (nugu-seyo?). Using '누구야?' is appropriate with close friends or people younger than you.

=== [6:38] 지켜주자 ===
meaning: Let's protect them.
tone: Informal, suggestive, protective.
--- word ---
term: 지켜주자
type: Verb
breakdown: 지키다 (to protect) + -어 주다 (to do for someone) + -자 (let's)
meaning: Let's protect (them).
notes: This combines the pattern V-어/아 주다, which means to do an action for someone's benefit, with the informal propositive ending -자 ('let's'). '지켜주다' means 'to protect for someone.' The '-자' ending is used with friends and peers. The formal equivalent is '-(으)ㅂ시다'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-자
meaning: An informal propositive ending used to say 'Let's...'. Used with friends or those younger.
example: 우리 같이 영화 보자. (Let's watch a movie together.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-어/아 주다
meaning: Indicates that the action is performed for the benefit of someone else.
example: 문을 열어 주세요. (Please open the door for me.)
level: Beginner

=== [6:40] 미안합니다 ===
meaning: I'm sorry.
tone: Formal, polite, apologetic.
--- word ---
term: 미안합니다
type: Adjective (in verb form)
breakdown: 미안하다 (to be sorry) + -ㅂ니다 (formal polite ending)
meaning: I am sorry.
notes: This is a formal and polite way to apologize. The '-ㅂ니다/습니다' ending is used in formal situations like broadcasts, presentations, or when speaking to superiors. The less formal but still polite form is '미안해요' (mianhaeyo). The informal (반말) version is '미안해' (mianhae).

=== [6:43] 벌써 8월 이렇게 갔어 ===
meaning: August already went by like this.
tone: Informal, reflective, slightly surprised.
--- word ---
term: 벌써
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Already, so soon
notes: '벌써' is used when something has happened earlier than expected, often conveying a sense of surprise. It's different from '이미' (imi), which also means 'already' but is more neutral and states a fact without the nuance of surprise.
--- word ---
term: 8월
type: Noun
breakdown: 팔 (eight) + 월 (month)
meaning: August
notes: Korean months are formed by combining a Sino-Korean number (일, 이, 삼...) with '월' (wol), which means 'month.' So, January is 1월 (일월), February is 2월 (이월), etc.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + -게 (adverbial ending)
meaning: Like this, in this way
notes: A very common adverb. Similarly, '그렇게' (geureoke) means 'like that' (in that way), and '저렇게' (jeoreoke) means 'like that' (over there).
--- word ---
term: 갔어
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 (to go) + -았어 (past tense, informal)
meaning: went
notes: This is the standard informal past tense conjugation ('-았/었어'). The verb '가다' (gada), meaning 'to go,' is used here metaphorically to mean time has 'passed' or 'gone by'.

--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -인가(요)?

meaning: not
notes: A short-form negator placed directly before a verb or adjective. It's the most common way to make a negative sentence in spoken Korean. The long form is -지 않다 (e.g., 나와 있지 않네).

meaning: Indicates a continuing state that resulted from a completed action. The action of '나오다' (to appear) has happened, and the result '나와 있다' (is showing/is out) continues.

=== [7:46] 나는 얼굴은 무조건 생수로 씻으니까 ===
meaning: Because I always wash my face with bottled water.
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + 는 (topic particle)
meaning: As for me, I
notes: This is the standard informal way to say 'I' as the topic of a sentence. In casual conversation, it's very often contracted to '난'.
--- word ---
term: 얼굴은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 얼굴 (face) + 은 (topic particle)
meaning: my face
notes: The topic particle '은/는' is used here to create a contrast. By saying '얼굴은' (as for my face), she implies that she treats her face differently from the rest of her body, which she explains in the next sentence.
--- word ---
term: 무조건
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: unconditionally, no matter what
notes: An adverb that strongly emphasizes that something is done without any conditions or exceptions. It adds a sense of a strict, personal rule.
--- word ---
term: 생수로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 생수 (bottled water) + 로 (instrumental particle)
meaning: with bottled water
notes: The particle '(으)로' indicates the tool, method, or means by which an action is performed. Here, bottled water is the 'tool' for washing.
--- word ---
term: 씻으니까
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 씻다 (to wash) + ~(으)니까
meaning: because I wash
notes: The `~(으)니까` ending states a reason. It's often used when the reason is a personal discovery or the basis for a suggestion or command. It's more subjective than the other common causal ending, `~아/어서`.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)니까
meaning: Indicates the reason or cause for the following clause. Often used when the main clause is a command, suggestion, or a statement of the speaker's personal experience or judgment.
example: 날씨가 추우니까 따뜻하게 입으세요. (Because the weather is cold, please dress warmly.)
level: Beginner

=== [7:49] 얼굴은 그나마 괜찮았는데 ===
meaning: My face, at least, was okay, but...
tone: Informal, comparative, trailing off.

term: 얼굴은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 얼굴 (face) + 은 (topic particle)
meaning: As for my face
notes: The topic particle '은/는' highlights '얼굴' (face) as the topic and contrasts it with another unstated subject (her body), which she is about to discuss.
--- word ---
term: 그나마
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: at least, thankfully
notes: This adverb is used to indicate that despite a generally negative or difficult situation, there is one positive or acceptable aspect. It carries a nuance of 'better than nothing' or 'fortunately'.
--- word ---

breakdown: 괜찮다 (to be okay) + ~았~ (past tense) + ~는데
meaning: it was okay, but...
notes: The `~는데` ending is a versatile connector used to provide background information before making a main point, often implying a contrast. Here, it sets up the expectation that what follows will be different or contrary to her face being okay.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V + ~는데/~은데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background context for the following clause. It can be used to show contrast ('but'), provide a reason ('so'), or simply introduce a topic ('and').
example: 어제는 바빴는데 오늘은 한가해요. (I was busy yesterday, but I'm free today.)

=== [7:52] 이제 몸은 조금 2주 동안은 뒤집어지더라고 ===

term: 몸은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 몸 (body) + 은 (topic particle)
meaning: as for my body
notes: The particle '은' explicitly contrasts her body with her face, which she just mentioned was fine. This is a common function of the topic marker '은/는'.
--- word ---
term: 조금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: a little, a bit
notes: Used to soften the following statement, making it sound less severe. It's a very common adverb in daily conversation.
--- word ---
term: 2주
type: Noun
breakdown: 이 (two) + 주 (week)
meaning: two weeks
notes: Korean uses two number systems. For counting weeks, months, or using numbers in general, the Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼...) are used. '주' is the counter for weeks.

type: Noun + Particle

meaning: it was breaking out (I recall)
notes: The verb '뒤집어지다' literally means 'to be flipped over'. In a skincare context, it's a very common colloquialism for skin 'breaking out' or having a bad reaction. The `~더라고` ending indicates the speaker is recalling something they personally experienced or witnessed.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~더라고(요)
meaning: A retrospective ending used to state a fact or observation from the speaker's direct past experience. It conveys a sense of personal recollection, sometimes with a hint of mild surprise.
example: 그 친구가 노래를 정말 잘하더라고. (I saw that friend sings really well.)
level: Intermediate

=== [7:58] 그러다가 ===
meaning: And then...
tone: Informal, transitional.
--- word ---
term: 그러다가
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so) + ~다가
meaning: and then, after that
notes: A common transitional phrase. The `~다가` grammar pattern indicates that an action or state is interrupted by another. '그러다가' is a fixed expression meaning 'while that was happening' or 'then'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~다가
meaning: Indicates that the action in the first clause was interrupted or followed by the action in the second clause. The subject of both clauses is usually the same.
example: 공부하다가 잠들었어요. (I fell asleep while studying.)
level: Intermediate

breakdown: 씻다 (to wash) + ~는데?

=== [8:07] 그냥 난 한국물이 많나봐 ===
meaning: I guess I'm just suited for Korean water.
tone: Informal, self-reflective, conclusive.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just
notes: Here, '그냥' acts as a conversational filler, softening the statement and making it sound like a casual thought or realization.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun (contracted)
breakdown: 나는 (I + topic particle)
meaning: I
notes: This is the spoken contraction of '나는'. It is extremely common in informal speech and writing.
--- word ---
term: 한국물이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 한국물 (Korean water) + 이 (subject particle)
meaning: Korean water
notes: A compound noun from '한국' (Korea) and '물' (water). The particle '이' marks it as the subject of the verb '맞다' (to suit).
--- word ---
term: 맞나봐
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 맞다 (to fit, to suit) + ~나 봐
meaning: I guess it suits me
notes: The verb '맞다' here means 'to be compatible with' or 'to suit'. The `~나 봐` ending expresses a guess or assumption based on evidence (in this case, her skin breaking out abroad but not in Korea).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~나 보다 / A + ~(으)ㄴ가 보다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's assumption or guess about a situation based on some observation or evidence. It translates to 'I guess...', 'It seems that...', or 'It looks like...'.
example: 사람이 많은 걸 보니 저 식당이 맛있나 봐요. (Seeing all the people, I guess that restaurant is delicious.)
level: Intermediate

=== [8:10] 한국 돌아가면 먹고 싶은 거 있냐고? ===

tone: Informal, responsive, repeating a question.
--- word ---
term: 한국
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Korea
notes: 
--- word ---
term: 돌아가면
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 돌아가다 (to go back) + ~(으)면
meaning: when/if I go back
notes: The `~(으)면` ending expresses a condition ('if') or a future time ('when').
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 먹다 (to eat) + ~고 싶다
meaning: want to eat
notes: The `~고 싶다` pattern is attached to a verb stem to express the speaker's desire to perform that action.
--- word ---
term: 싶은
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 싶다 (to want) + ~은 (adjective modifier)
meaning: that (I) want
notes: This is the adjectival form of '싶다', used to modify the following noun, '거' (thing). So, '먹고 싶은 거' means 'the thing that I want to eat'.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (colloquial)
breakdown: 것 (thing)
meaning: thing
notes: '거' is the colloquial, spoken form of '것', which means 'thing' or 'the one'.
--- word ---
term: 있냐고?
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 있다 (to have/exist) + ~냐고?
meaning: (are you asking) if there is?
notes: This is an informal indirect quotation ending for a question. Hwasa is repeating a fan's question from the comments before she answers it. The full form would be something like '...있냐고 물어봤어요?' (Did you ask if there is...?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~(느)냐고(요)?
meaning: An indirect quotation form used to repeat a question someone else asked. In conversation, it's often used on its own to confirm one has heard the question correctly.
example: A: 몇 살이에요? (How old are you?) B: 몇 살이냐고요? 스무 살이에요. ((Are you asking) how old I am? I'm twenty.)
level: Intermediate

=== [8:27] 먹고 싶은 게 너무 많아 ===
meaning: There are so many things I want to eat.
tone: Informal, expressive. Emphasizing her strong cravings.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: verb
breakdown: 먹다 + ~고
meaning: eat and...
notes: The verb stem '먹다' (to eat) is connected to '싶다' using the '~고' connector. This form is the base for the '~고 싶다' (want to) pattern.
--- word ---
term: 싶은
type: auxiliary adjective
breakdown: 싶다 + ~은
meaning: that (I) want
notes: This is the adjectival form of '~고 싶다' (to want to). The '~은/는' ending modifies the following noun, '게' (thing). So, '먹고 싶은 게' means 'the thing that I want to eat'.
--- word ---
term: 게
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 것 + 이 (contraction)
meaning: the thing that (is)
notes: '게' is a very common spoken contraction of '것이' (geos-i), which means 'thing' + the subject particle '이'. Using '게' instead of '것이' makes speech sound more natural and fluid.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / too / very
notes: In colloquial Korean, '너무' is frequently used to mean 'very' or 'really' in both positive and negative contexts, although traditionally it meant 'too (excessively)'.
--- word ---
term: 많아
type: adjective
breakdown: 많다 + ~아
meaning: there are many
notes: This is the informal, non-polite conjugation of '많다' (to be many/much). This '해' style (해체) is used with close friends, family, or people younger than the speaker.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고 싶다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's desire to do a certain action; 'to want to (verb)'.
example: 영화를 보고 싶어. (I want to watch a movie.)
level: Beginner

=== [8:29] 근데 못 먹어 ===
meaning: But I can't eat it.
tone: Informal, disappointed.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 (contraction)
meaning: but / however
notes: '근데' is the colloquial, shortened form of '그런데' and is extremely common in spoken Korean to mean 'but' or 'by the way'.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: '못' negates a verb to express inability ('cannot'), distinct from '안' which expresses unwillingness ('do not'). For example, '못 먹어' (I can't eat, e.g., due to circumstances) vs. '안 먹어' (I won't eat, by choice).
--- word ---
term: 먹어
type: verb
breakdown: 먹다 + ~어
meaning: eat
notes: Informal, non-polite conjugation of '먹다' (to eat). This is the '해' style (해체) ending.

term: 없었는데
type: verb
breakdown: 없다 + ~었~ + ~는데
meaning: I didn't have, but...
notes: The '~는데' ending is a versatile connector. Here, it provides background information ('I wasn't thinking about it...') for the main clause that follows ('...but because they kept talking about it...').
--- word ---
term: 언니들이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 언니 + ~들 + ~이

term: 하도
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so much / excessively

breakdown: 
meaning: that
notes: '그' refers to something already mentioned or known to both the speaker and listener.
--- word ---

--- word ---
term: 하니까
type: verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~니까
meaning: because (they) said it
notes: The '~(으)니까' ending indicates a reason or cause. It's often used when the reason is a personal experience or discovery, and it frequently precedes a suggestion, command, or a statement of result.

=== [8:41] 지금 세뇌당했어 ===

--- word ---
term: 지금
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: '지금' refers to the present moment, 'right now'. It's often contrasted with '이제' (ije), which means 'now' in the sense of a new state beginning from this point forward.
--- word ---
term: 세뇌당했어
type: verb
breakdown: 세뇌당하다 + ~었어
meaning: was brainwashed
notes: This is a passive verb. '세뇌' (senoe) means 'brainwashing'. The suffix '당하다' (danghada) is attached to some nouns to create a passive verb, meaning 'to be subjected to' or 'to suffer' the action of the noun. The ending is past tense informal.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 당하다
meaning: A passive voice construction meaning 'to be subjected to (the noun's action)'. It often carries a negative connotation.
example: 사기당했어. (I got scammed.)
level: Intermediate

meaning: So, therefore
notes: A common connector that links a reason to a result. It's used to start a sentence that follows logically from a previous, often unstated, thought. Here, she's likely thinking, 'I'm craving Korean food, so...'
--- word ---
term: 동대문
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Dongdaemun

term: 시장
type: Noun
breakdown:

term: 쪽에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 쪽 + 에
meaning: near, in the direction of

term: 있는
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -는
meaning: that is/which is located
notes: This is the present tense adjectival form of the verb 있다 (to exist, to be at a location). It modifies a noun that follows it. Here, the noun (likely 'restaurant') is omitted, which is common in speech when the context is clear.

pattern: V-는 (Noun)
meaning: Present tense verb modifier. It turns a verb into a descriptive clause that modifies a following noun.
example: 지금 먹는 음식 (The food that I am eating now)

=== [9:00] 나 요새 이제 ===
meaning: Me, these days, now...
tone: Informal, hesitant, starting a new thought.
--- word ---
term: 나
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: I, me (informal)
notes: This is the informal pronoun for 'I', used with friends or people younger than you. The formal equivalent is 저 (jeo). Hwasa uses 나 because she is speaking casually to her fans.
--- word ---
term: 요새
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: These days, recently
notes: 요새 (yosae) is very similar to 요즘 (yojeum) and they are often used interchangeably in conversation. 요새 can feel slightly more colloquial.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now' but often implies a change from the past or a new beginning from this point forward. It differs from 지금 (jigeum), which refers to the immediate present moment. For example, '이제 갈게' (I'll get going now) vs. '지금 뭐해?' (What are you doing right now?).

=== [9:04] 닭볶음탕을 안 먹은 지 너무 오래돼서 이름도 까먹었네 ===
meaning: It's been so long since I've eaten dakbokkeumtang that I even forgot the name.
tone: Informal, conversational, expressing mild self-reproach.
--- word ---
term: 닭볶음탕을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 닭볶음탕 + 을
meaning: Dakbokkeumtang (spicy braised chicken)
notes: 을 (eul) is the object particle, attached to a noun that ends in a consonant. It marks 'dakbokkeumtang' as the direct object of the verb 'to eat'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: A simple way to negate a verb or adjective by placing it directly before the word. It is very common in spoken Korean. The alternative is the V-지 않다 pattern (e.g., 먹지 않다).
--- word ---
term: 먹은
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -(으)ㄴ
meaning: (that I) ate
notes: This is the past tense adjectival form of 먹다 (to eat). It's used here as part of the grammar pattern 'V-(으)ㄴ 지' to talk about time elapsed since an action.
--- word ---
term: 지
type: Bound Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: since (time has passed)
notes: A bound noun that cannot be used alone. It is always used in the pattern 'V-(으)ㄴ 지 [time] 되다' to express how long it has been since an action occurred.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, too, very
notes: While its dictionary meaning is 'too' (implying excess), in modern colloquial Korean, 너무 is frequently used to mean 'very' or 'really' in both positive and negative sentences.
--- word ---
term: 오래돼서
type: Verb
breakdown: 오래되다 + -어서
meaning: because it's been a long time
notes: 오래되다 means 'for a long time to have passed'. The -어서 ending connects this clause as the reason for the following clause ('I forgot the name').
--- word ---
term: 이름도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 이름 + 도
meaning: even the name
notes: The particle 도 (do) means 'also' or 'too'. When used in a context like this, it adds emphasis and can be translated as 'even'.
--- word ---
term: 까먹었네
type: Verb
breakdown: 까먹다 + -었- + -네
meaning: I forgot!
notes: 까먹다 is a colloquial, informal word for 'to forget' (the standard word is 잊다 or 잊어버리다). The -네 ending expresses mild surprise or realization upon discovering something new. It adds a nuance of 'Oh, I see I've forgotten'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄴ 지 [time] 되다
meaning: Indicates how much time has passed since an action occurred. Literally 'it has become [time] since V-ing'.
example: 한국에 온 지 1년 됐어요. (It has been one year since I came to Korea.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-네(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to express mild surprise, realization, or admiration about something the speaker has just learned or noticed.
example: 이 케이크 정말 맛있네! (Wow, this cake is really delicious!)
level: Beginner

breakdown: 캐릭터 + 의
meaning: character's
notes: 의 (ui) is the possessive particle, equivalent to 's in English. In spoken Korean, its pronunciation is often simplified to '에' (e).
--- word ---
term: 헤어스타일인
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 헤어스타일 + 이다 + -ㄴ
meaning: being a hairstyle
notes: This is the descriptive form of the copula (이다, to be), which allows the noun phrase '애니메이션 캐릭터의 헤어스타일' to modify the following noun '것'.
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Bound Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: thing, fact
notes: Used in the common grammar pattern '-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다' to mean 'it seems like' or 'I think that'.
--- word ---
term: 같습니다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 + -습니다
meaning: it seems, it is like
notes: The formal polite ending (합쇼체, hapshoche). Hwasa is reading a fan's comment, which was likely written in this formal style. This contrasts with her own usual informal speech (반말, banmal).
--- grammar ---
pattern: N인 것 같다 / A/V-(으)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, opinion, or impression softly. It translates to 'I think...', 'It seems like...', or 'It looks like...'.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (I think it's raining.)
level: Beginner

=== [9:33] 한 번만 맛있는 거 먹고 ===

meaning: one

--- word ---

breakdown: 먹다 + -고
meaning: eat and...

--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -고
meaning: A connective ending that links clauses. It can mean 'and' for listing actions or 'and then' for showing sequence.
example: 밥을 먹고 학교에 갔어요. (I ate and then went to school.)
level: Beginner

=== [9:41] 귤이야 ===

pattern: Noun + -(이)야
meaning: The casual, non-polite (반말) way of stating 'It is [Noun]'. Use -이야 after a consonant and -야 after a vowel.
example: 이건 내 책이야. (This is my book.)

=== [10:07] 저런 킬러라면 암살당해도 여한 없어 ===
