--- word ---
term: 저런
type: Determiner
breakdown: 저렇다 -> 저런
meaning: like that / that kind of
notes: 저런 is a form of 저렇다 (to be like that) used to modify a noun. It refers to something known to both speaker and listener but not necessarily present. Compare with 이런 (like this) and 그런 (like that).
--- word ---
term: 킬러라면
type: Noun + Conditional
breakdown: 킬러 + -라면
meaning: if it's a killer
notes: The conditional ending -(이)라면 attaches to nouns to mean 'if it is/were [Noun]'. It is often used for hypothetical situations. -라면 is used after a vowel, -이라면 after a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 암살당해도
type: Verb
breakdown: 암살당하다 + -아도
meaning: even if (I) am assassinated
notes: 암살당하다 is the passive form, 'to be assassinated'. The verb ending -아/어도 means 'even if' or 'even though', indicating the following clause happens regardless of this condition.
--- word ---
term: 여한
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: lingering regret
notes: A Sino-Korean word (餘恨) meaning 'remaining regret'. It is most commonly used in the expression 여한이 없다 (to have no regrets).
--- word ---
term: 없어
type: Adjective
breakdown: 없다 + -어
meaning: there are no
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말) form of 없다 (to not exist/have). The full phrase is '여한이 없어', but the subject particle -이 is often omitted in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -아/어도
meaning: A concessive ending meaning 'even if' or 'even though'.
example: 피곤해도 공부할 거예요. (Even if I'm tired, I'm going to study.)
level: Intermediate

=== [10:53] 이거 봐 이제 일하러 가야 된다잖아 ===
meaning: Look at this. They're saying I have to go to work now.
tone: Informal, complaining, playful.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것 -> 이거
meaning: this
notes: 이거 is the common spoken form of 이것 (this thing). '이거 봐' (Look at this) is a very common phrase to draw someone's attention.
--- word ---
term: 봐
type: Verb (imperative)
breakdown: 보다 + -아
meaning: look
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) command form of 보다 (to see/look). It's used among close friends or to people of lower status.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 means 'now' with a sense of change from the past ('from now on'). It differs from 지금, which means 'right now' at this exact moment.
--- word ---
term: 일하러
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 일하다 + -(으)러
meaning: in order to work
notes: The pattern 'V-(으)러 가다/오다' expresses the purpose for going or coming somewhere. 일하러 가다 means 'to go to work'.
--- word ---
term: 가야
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 + -아야
meaning: have to go
notes: This is the first part of the grammar pattern -아야/어야 되다/하다, which expresses obligation or necessity ('must', 'have to').
--- word ---
term: 된다잖아
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + -ㄴ다 + -잖아
meaning: they say (I) have to
notes: A colloquial contraction of -ㄴ다고 하잖아. It combines indirect quotation (-ㄴ다고 하다) with -잖아, which is used to state something the listener should already know. The full meaning is like, 'See? They're saying I have to..., you know?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아야/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, equivalent to 'must' or 'have to' in English.
example: 숙제를 해야 돼요. (I have to do my homework.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -다잖아 / -라잖아
meaning: A colloquial ending for reported speech, combining -다고 하다 (they say that...) and -잖아 (you know...). It's used to remind the listener of something that was said.
example: 그 사람 온다잖아. (You know they said he's coming.)
level: Intermediate

=== [10:56] 그러니까 이 시간에 오면 안 좋은 거지 ===
meaning: So, that's why it's not good to come at this time.
tone: Casual, explanatory, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 그러니까
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그러하다 + -니까
meaning: So / Therefore / That's why
notes: A very common conjunction in spoken Korean used to state a reason or conclusion based on what was just said. It connects a cause/reason to a resulting statement or command.
--- word ---
term: 이
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: This
notes: Placed directly before a noun to indicate something close to the speaker. '이 시간' means 'this time'. The other basic determiners are '그' (that, near the listener) and '저' (that, far from both).
--- word ---
term: 시간에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 시간 + -에
meaning: at the time
notes: 시간 (sigan) means 'time'. The particle -에 is a time-marking particle, indicating a specific point in time when an action occurs.
--- word ---
term: 오면
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + -면
meaning: if (one) comes
notes: The conditional ending -(으)면 means 'if' or 'when'. It's attached to a verb or adjective stem to form a conditional clause. 오다 means 'to come'.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: A short-form negator placed before a verb or adjective. It's generally used in spoken Korean for its brevity. The long-form equivalent is ~지 않다 (e.g., 좋지 않다).
--- word ---
term: 좋은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 좋다 + -은
meaning: good
notes: This is the descriptive form of the adjective 좋다 (to be good), used to modify the following noun (것). The ending -은 is used after a consonant stem, while -ㄴ is used after a vowel stem.
--- word ---
term: 거지
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 것 + -이지
meaning: it is..., right?
notes: A contraction of '것이지'. '것' is a placeholder noun for 'thing' or 'fact'. The ending '-지' is used in casual speech to confirm something the speaker believes the listener already knows or will agree with.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -(으)ㄴ/는 거지
meaning: Used to state a fact or conclusion as if it's obvious or to seek agreement. It carries the nuance of 'It's a fact that...' or 'Of course it's...'.
example: 열심히 했으니까 당연히 피곤한 거지. (Of course you're tired, since you worked hard.)
level: Intermediate

=== [10:58] 그래서 내가 늦게 오는 거야 ===
meaning: That's why I come on late.
tone: Casual, direct explanation.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So / Therefore
notes: A conjunction that indicates a cause-and-effect relationship. The preceding clause is the reason for the following clause. It's one of the first conjunctions learners encounter.
--- word ---
term: 내가

breakdown: 나 + -가
meaning: I (subject)
notes: 나 is the informal pronoun for 'I'. -가 is the subject marking particle, used after a vowel. In casual conversation, '내가' is used much more frequently than the formal '제가'.
--- word ---
term: 늦게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 늦다 + -게
meaning: Late
notes: The suffix -게 attaches to the stem of an adjective (like 늦다, 'to be late') to turn it into an adverb, describing how an action is performed. For example, '늦게 오다' (to come late).
--- word ---
term: 오는
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + -는
meaning: coming
notes: This is the present tense descriptive form of the verb 오다 (to come). It acts like an adjective to modify the following noun, '거' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 것 + -이야
meaning: it is...
notes: A very common informal sentence ending (반말, banmal). It's a contraction of '것이야' and is used to state a fact, provide an explanation, or declare an intention. It makes the statement sound definitive and explanatory.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~는 거야
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to explain a situation or state a fact with a sense of finality or emphasis. It's the informal version of ~는 것이다.
example: 지금 집에 가는 거야. (I'm going home now.)
level: Beginner

=== [11:13] 일하러 가야 돼서 못 보는 것보다 ===

breakdown: 일하다 + -(으)러
meaning: to work
notes: The '-(으)러' ending indicates the purpose of movement, used with verbs like 가다 (to go) and 오다 (to come).

breakdown: 가다 + -아야 되다 + -서
meaning: because (I/you) have to go

breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: A negator indicating inability or impossibility to perform an action. It's placed before an action verb. Differentiate from '안', which indicates not doing something by choice or simple negation.
--- word ---
term: 보는
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 + -는
meaning: watching
notes: The present tense adjectival form of 보다 (to see/watch), which modifies the following noun '것' (thing).
--- word ---
term: 것보다
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 것 + -보다
meaning: than the thing (of watching)
notes: The particle '-보다' means 'than' and is used for comparisons. This entire clause sets up the first half of a comparison that will be completed in the next sentence.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 보다
meaning: A comparative particle meaning 'than'. It is attached to the noun that serves as the standard of comparison.
example: 이것보다 저것이 더 좋아요. (That one is better than this one.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아야/어야 되다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, equivalent to 'must' or 'have to' in English.
example: 내일 일찍 일어나야 돼요. (I have to wake up early tomorrow.)
level: Beginner

=== [11:15] 차라리 자면서 이렇게 졸려서 졸린 상태로 보는 게 더 낫지 ===

--- word ---
term: 차라리
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Rather / Preferably
notes: Used when choosing a better, though perhaps not ideal, option among alternatives. It is frequently paired with '...는 게 낫다' (it's better to...).
--- word ---
term: 자면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다 + -(으)면서
meaning: while sleeping
notes: The '-(으)면서' ending indicates that two actions are being performed by the same subject at the same time. 자다 means 'to sleep'.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Like this / In this way
notes: Refers to the manner in which something is done, often pointing to a current state or action. Here, it describes her own sleepy state.
--- word ---
term: 졸려서
type: Verb
breakdown: 졸리다 + -어서
meaning: because I'm sleepy
notes: 졸리다 means 'to be sleepy/drowsy'. The '-(아/어)서' ending indicates a reason or cause for the following state or action.
--- word ---
term: 졸린
type: Adjective
breakdown: 졸리다 + -ㄴ
meaning: sleepy
notes: The descriptive form of 졸리다, modifying the noun 상태 (state). The adjectival ending '-ㄴ' is attached to verb/adjective stems ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 상태로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 상태 + -(으)로
meaning: in a state of
notes: 상태 means 'state' or 'condition'. The particle '-(으)로' can indicate the means, method, or status by which something is done.
--- word ---
term: 보는
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 + -는
meaning: watching
notes: The present tense descriptive form of 보다 (to watch), modifying the noun '것' (which is contracted to '게').
--- word ---
term: 게
type: Noun (Contraction)
breakdown: 것 + -이
meaning: the thing that...
notes: A very common spoken-language contraction of '것이'. '것' is a placeholder noun ('thing'/'fact'), and '-이' is the subject particle.
--- word ---
term: 더
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: More
notes: Used to make comparisons, typically placed before an adjective or another adverb to intensify it.
--- word ---
term: 낫지
type: Adjective
breakdown: 낫다 + -지
meaning: is better, right?
notes: 낫다 (natda) is an adjective meaning 'to be better'. The ending '-지' seeks agreement from the listener, adding a '..., right?' nuance.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 차라리 ~는 게 낫다
meaning: A common expression meaning 'It would be better to...' or 'I would rather...'. It's used to suggest a preferable alternative, especially when the other options are undesirable.
example: 비가 오니까 차라리 영화 보는 게 낫겠어. (Since it's raining, it would be better to just watch a movie.)
level: Intermediate

breakdown: 새벽 + 에
meaning: at dawn

--- word ---

type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + ~았~ + ~을
meaning: when (I) came
notes: This is the past tense verb '오다' (oda) modified by '~을/ㄹ' to describe the following noun '때' (ttae). '왔을 때' literally means 'the time that I came'.

type: Noun + Particle

--- word ---
term: 했어
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~았어
meaning: it was
notes: The informal past tense form of '하다' (hada). It combines with '좋긴' to form the full expression '좋긴 했어' (jokin haesseo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective-긴 하다
meaning: Acknowledges a fact, but often implies a following contrast. 'It's true that... but...'
example: 비싸긴 한데 맛있어요. (It's true that it's expensive, but it's delicious.)
level: Intermediate

breakdown: 
meaning: social sense, mood

=== [11:57] 알다가도 모르겠어 ===
meaning: I think I know, but then I don't.
tone: Informal, contemplative, expressing confusion.
--- word ---
term: 알다가도
type: Verb phrase
breakdown: 알다 (to know) + -다가 (connective ending) + -도 (particle)
meaning: just when I think I know...
notes: The pattern V-다가도 expresses that even while in the state of V, an unexpected or contrary situation occurs. It highlights a surprising shift.
--- word ---
term: 모르겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 (to not know) + -겠- (infix) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: I just don't know.
notes: The infix -겠- often expresses conjecture or intention, but in this common phrase, it conveys a strong sense of 'I can't seem to figure it out,' making the feeling of not knowing more emphatic than a simple 몰라 (molla).
--- grammar ---
pattern: 알다가도 모르겠다
meaning: A common idiom used to express confusion about a situation or a person's behavior that seems unpredictable or hard to grasp.
example: 사람 마음은 알다가도 모르겠어. (People's feelings are so hard to figure out.)
level: Intermediate

type: Verb connective
breakdown: 펴다 + ~었~ + ~는데
meaning: I straightened it, and...
notes: The verb is 펴다 (to straighten/unfold). The ~는데 ending is used to provide background information for the following clause. It connects two related actions or situations, often implying a contrast or unexpected result. It's very common in conversation.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, for real
notes: A very common adverb used in casual speech to add emphasis, similar to 'really' or 'seriously'. It can be used interchangeably with 정말 (jeongmal) in most conversational contexts.
--- word ---
term: 많이
type: Adverb
breakdown: 많다 + ~이
meaning: a lot, much
notes: This is the adverbial form of the adjective 많다 (to be many/much). It's used to modify verbs, indicating a large quantity or degree of an action, like 'eat a lot' (많이 먹다) or 'sleep a lot' (많이 자다).
--- word ---
term: 자랐더라
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 자라다 + ~았~ + ~더라
meaning: it had grown (I realized)
notes: The ~더라 ending is a retrospective marker. The speaker uses it to state a fact they personally observed or realized in the past. It adds a nuance of mild surprise or new discovery, as if saying, 'I looked and, to my surprise, it had grown'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~더라
meaning: Expresses the speaker's realization or observation of a past event or state. It conveys a sense of recalling something they personally witnessed.
example: 어제 보니 그 식당 문 닫았더라. (When I looked yesterday, I saw that restaurant was closed.)
level: Intermediate

=== [12:51] 이렇게 많이 자랄 줄이야? ===
meaning: Who knew it would grow this much?!
tone: Informal, rhetorical question expressing strong surprise.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: like this, this much
notes: A contraction of 이러하게. It's used to indicate the manner or degree of something. It often pairs with an adjective or another adverb to add emphasis, as in 'why is it this cold?' (왜 이렇게 추워요?).
--- word ---
term: 많이
type: Adverb
breakdown: 많다 + ~이
meaning: a lot, much
notes: The adverbial form of the adjective 많다 (to be many/much). It modifies the verb 자라다 (to grow) to emphasize the extent of the growth.
--- word ---
term: 자랄
type: Verb modifier
breakdown: 자라다 + ~(으)ㄹ
meaning: that would grow
notes: The ~(으)ㄹ ending turns a verb into a future descriptive form that can modify a noun. Here, it modifies the noun 줄 (fact, way).
--- word ---
term: 줄이야?
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 줄 + ~이야?
meaning: The fact that...?!
notes: This is part of the `~(으)ㄹ 줄이야` pattern. It's a rhetorical question expressing strong surprise or disbelief, similar to saying 'I can't believe that...' or 'Who would have thought that...?' It's more emphatic than `~(으)ㄹ 줄 몰랐다` (I didn't know...).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~(으)ㄹ 줄이야
meaning: An exclamatory expression used to show strong surprise or disbelief about a fact or situation. It functions as a rhetorical question.
example: 네가 1등을 할 줄이야! (I can't believe you got first place!)
level: Intermediate

=== [12:55] 몰랐네 ===
meaning: I didn't realize.
tone: Informal, conclusive, expressing a past state of not knowing.
--- word ---
term: 몰랐네
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 모르다 + ~았~ + ~네
meaning: Oh, I didn't know.
notes: The verb is 모르다 (to not know). The ~네 ending expresses the speaker's current realization, surprise, or impression about something. Combining the past tense ~았~ with ~네 creates the meaning 'Oh, I see now that I didn't know before'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~네(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to express mild surprise, admiration, or a new realization upon directly seeing, hearing, or experiencing something.
example: 눈이 오네. (Oh, it's snowing.)
level: Beginner

=== [12:56] 이제는 진짜 단발이야 ===

--- word ---
term: 이제는
type: Adverb + Particle
breakdown: 이제 + ~는
meaning: Now (as for now)
notes: 이제 means 'now', but often implies a change from the past into the present and future. It's different from 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now'. The topic particle ~는 adds emphasis, highlighting the contrast between 'before' and 'now'.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, genuinely
notes: A common adverb in casual speech to mean 'really' or 'truly'. It emphasizes that her hair is no longer in a transitional phase but is now genuinely a bob cut.
--- word ---

breakdown: 단발 + ~이야
meaning: it is a bob cut
notes: 단발 (danbal) is the Korean word for a bob haircut. ~이야 is the informal, non-polite conjugation of the verb 이다 (to be) used with nouns ending in a consonant. The polite form would be 단발이에요 (danbarieyo).

=== [12:59] 쇼컷이 아닌 것 같아 ===

--- grammar ---

meaning: Expresses the speaker's opinion or guess that something is not N. 'I think it's not N' or 'It doesn't seem like N'.

=== [13:01] 근데 여기 귀를 파는 게 있어가지고 ===

breakdown: 
meaning: here, this part
notes: Refers to a specific location, in this case, the area of her hair around her ears.
--- word ---

type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 귀 + ~를
meaning: the ear (as object)
notes: 귀 means 'ear'. The particle ~를 marks it as the direct object of the verb 파다 (to dig/cut out).
--- word ---
term: 파는
type: Verb modifier
breakdown: 파다 + ~는
meaning: that cuts/digs
notes: The verb 파다 literally means 'to dig', but in a hairstyling context, it means to cut out a section or create a shape, like an undercut. The ~는 ending makes it a present-tense modifier for the next noun.

type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 것 + ~이
meaning: the thing that is
notes: 게 is a very common colloquial contraction of the noun 것 (thing) followed by the subject particle ~이. So, 파는 게 means 'the act of cutting' or 'the part that is cut'.

breakdown: 있다 + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: because there is...
notes: The pattern `~아/어 가지고` is often used in speech to mean 'because' or 'so'. It connects a reason (there is a part cut around the ear) to a result that is stated next. The sentence trails off, but this phrase sets up the reason for her next statement.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: A connective used to link a reason to a result, similar to ~아/어서. It's very common in spoken Korean. It can also mean 'and then' when linking sequential actions.
example: 돈이 없어가지고 못 샀어. (I couldn't buy it because I didn't have money.)

pattern: Verb Stem + -았/었어(요)?
meaning: Forms a simple past tense question. Adding -요 at the end makes it polite (해요체, haeyoche), while omitting it makes it informal (해체, haeche or 반말, banmal).

meaning: the most

--- word ---
term: 게
type: Pronoun + Particle (Contraction)
breakdown: 것 + 이
meaning: the thing that...
notes: This is a very common spoken contraction of '것이'. '것' means 'thing' and '이' is the subject particle. Using '게' instead of '것이' makes speech sound more natural and casual.
--- word ---
term: 하원앨리스
type: Proper Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Ha-won Alice
notes: This appears to be a misremembered title. It's likely a reference to the anime '학원 앨리스' (Gakuen Alice / Alice Academy), as '학원' (hak-won, academy) sounds similar to '하원' (ha-won).
--- word ---
term: 밖에
type: Particle
breakdown: 
meaning: only, nothing but
notes: This particle is attached to a noun and MUST be followed by a negative predicate like '없다' (to not exist) or '모르다' (to not know). It emphasizes that the noun is the only option available.
--- word ---
term: 없어서
type: Verb + Ending
breakdown: 없다 + ~아/어서
meaning: because there isn't
notes: The '~아/어서' ending indicates a reason or cause. The sentence trails off, implying the full thought is something like, 'Because I only know this anime, I don't know which character you mean.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + 밖에 없다
meaning: Means 'there is nothing but N' or 'only N exists'. It's a strong way to express that something is the sole option. It must be used with a negative verb.
example: 냉장고에 물밖에 없어요. (There's nothing but water in the fridge.)
level: Beginner

=== [14:28] 누군지가 지금 중요할 것 같아 ===
meaning: I think who it is is what's important right now.
tone: Informal, thoughtful, slightly playful.
--- word ---
term: 누군지가
type: Pronoun + Verb + Nominalizer + Particle
breakdown: 누구 (who) + -이다 (to be) + -ㄴ지 (nominalizing ending) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: who it is (as the subject)
notes: This construction turns the clause '누구인지' (who it is) into a noun phrase that can function as the subject of the main sentence. The '-ㄴ지' ending is used to embed a question or point of uncertainty into another clause.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: Refers to the present moment. It's often compared with '이제' (ije), which also means 'now' but carries a nuance of change from the past (e.g., 'from now on'). '지금' is for 'right now'.
--- word ---
term: 중요할
type: Adjective
breakdown: 중요하다 (to be important) + -ㄹ (future/conjecture modifier)
meaning: that will be important
notes: The '-ㄹ/을' ending modifies the adjective '중요하다' so it can describe the following noun ('것'). It adds a sense of future action or supposition.
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: thing / fact
notes: A dependent noun that must be modified by a preceding clause or adjective. '중요할 것' means 'the thing that will be important'.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 (to be like, to seem) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: it seems / I think
notes: The '-아' ending makes this the informal (반말 - banmal) form. The polite version is '같아요' (gat-ayo). The entire '-(으)ㄹ 것 같다' pattern is a very common and natural way to express an opinion or guess.
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ 것 같다
meaning: Expresses supposition, opinion, or a guess about a situation. It's a softer way of stating something than a direct declarative sentence. Translates to 'I think...', 'It seems like...', or 'It looks like...'.
example: 내일은 날씨가 좋을 것 같아. (I think the weather will be good tomorrow.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: Clause + -ㄴ/은/는지 + Particle
meaning: A pattern used to embed a clause (often a question with who, what, where, etc.) into a larger sentence, turning it into a noun phrase that can be marked by particles like the subject particle '-가/이'.
example: 그 사람이 어디에 사는지가 궁금해요. (I'm curious about where that person lives.)
level: Intermediate

=== [14:35] 체인소맨이라는 애니에서 레제라는 캐릭터가 있어 ===

meaning: called 'Chainsaw Man'

type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 애니 + ~에서
meaning: in an anime
notes: '애니' is a loanword from Japanese, short for '애니메이션' (animation), and is the standard word for anime. The particle '~에서' indicates the location where an action or state exists, meaning 'in', 'at', or 'from'.

type: Noun + Grammar Pattern
breakdown: 레제 + ~라는
meaning: called Reze
notes: The pattern '~라는' is used after a noun ending in a vowel to mean 'called...' or 'named...'. It's the same pattern as '이라는' but for vowel-ending nouns.

type: Noun + Particle

meaning: a character
notes: '캐릭터' is a loanword for 'character'. The particle '~가' is the subject marker, used here because '캐릭터' ends in a vowel. It indicates that the 'character' is the subject of the verb '있어' (to exist).
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 (to exist/be) + ~어
meaning: there is
notes: This is the informal, non-polite present tense conjugation of '있다'. It's used among close friends or people of similar age. The polite form is '있어요'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + ~(이)라는 + Noun
meaning: A pattern used to introduce or define a noun, meaning 'a (Noun) that is called (Noun)'.

breakdown: 다이너마이트 + ~잉
meaning: Dynamite (cutesy)
notes: A playful, cute pronunciation of 다이너마이트 (dainomaiteu - dynamite). The ~잉 (~ing) ending is a form of 애교 (aegyo), or cute behavior, often used in casual speech to sound softer. 'Sexy Dynamite' is a nickname Hwasa uses for herself.

=== [15:16] 앞머리는 계속 있긴 했는데 ===
meaning: I did have bangs continuously, but...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 앞머리는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 앞머리 + ~는
meaning: As for my bangs...
notes: 앞머리 literally means 'front head (hair)'. The topic particle ~는/은 introduces the topic and often implies a contrast with something else.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Continuously / Still
notes: An adverb indicating that an action or state continues without stopping.
--- word ---
term: 있긴
type: Verb + Particle (contracted)
breakdown: 있다 + ~기는
meaning: did have
notes: This is a contraction of 있기는 (itgineun). The pattern '~기는 하다' is used to acknowledge a fact while implying a contrast or exception that will follow.
--- word ---
term: 했는데
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 하다 + ~았/었~ + ~는데
meaning: ...but
notes: Part of the '~긴 하다' pattern. The ~는데 ending connects clauses, provides background, and often introduces a contrast. Leaving the sentence on ~는데 makes it sound like it's trailing off.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~긴 했는데
meaning: Acknowledges a verb's action ('It's true that...'), but implies a following contrast. The full pattern is V + ~기는 하다.
example: 먹긴 했는데 아직 배고파요. (I did eat, but I'm still hungry.)
level: Intermediate

=== [15:24] 머리도 계속 그대로긴 한데 ===
meaning: My hair has also stayed the same, but...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 머리도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 머리 + ~도
meaning: My hair also...
notes: 머리 can mean 'head' or 'hair'. Here, it refers to her hairstyle. The particle ~도 means 'also' or 'too'.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Continuously / Still
notes: An adverb indicating that an action or state continues without stopping.
--- word ---
term: 그대로긴
type: Adverb + Copula + Particle (contracted)
breakdown: 그대로 + 이다 + ~기는
meaning: is the same
notes: A contraction of 그대로이기는 (geudaeroigineun). 그대로 means 'as it is' or 'unchanged'. Used in the '~기는 하다' pattern to acknowledge that her hair is the same, while implying a contrast.
--- word ---
term: 한데
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 하다 + ~는데
meaning: ...but
notes: The second part of the '~긴 하다' pattern. The ~는데 ending connects the clauses and implies contrast, leaving the sentence unfinished.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + ~(이)긴 한데
meaning: Acknowledges a fact about a noun ('It's true that it's [Noun]...'), but implies a following contrast. The full pattern is Noun + ~(이)기는 하다.
example: 친한 친구긴 한데, 자주 못 봐. (He is a close friend, but we can't see each other often.)
level: Intermediate

=== [15:33] 손글씨지 ===
meaning: It's handwriting, of course.
tone: Informal, confirming, matter-of-fact.
--- word ---
term: 손글씨지
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 손글씨 + 이다 + ~지
meaning: It's handwriting.
notes: 손글씨 is a compound of 손 (son, 'hand') + 글씨 (geulssi, 'writing'). The ending ~지 is used informally to state something the speaker assumes the listener already knows or agrees with, adding a nuance of 'of course' or 'you know'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + ~(이)지
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to state a fact confidently, as if it's obvious or already known to the listener.
example: 당연히 한국 사람이지. (Of course I'm Korean.)

=== [15:34] 오늘 뭔가 스타일링이 새로워? ===
meaning: Is my styling somehow new today?
tone: Informal, inquisitive. Asking fans for their opinion.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: today
notes: A fundamental time-related noun. It can function as a noun (e.g., '오늘이 금요일이에요' - Today is Friday) or as an adverb ('오늘 와요' - I'm coming today) without needing a particle.
--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: Pronoun/Adverb
breakdown: 뭐 + ㄴ가
meaning: something, somehow
notes: A contraction of '무엇인가'. It's used colloquially to express a vague feeling or when you can't pinpoint exactly what something is. It adds a sense of 'for some reason' or 'kind of'. Example: '뭔가 이상해' (Something's strange).
--- word ---
term: 스타일링이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 스타일링 + 이
meaning: styling (as the subject)
notes: 스타일링 is a loanword from English 'styling'. The particle '이' marks it as the subject of the sentence. '이' is used after nouns ending in a consonant, while '가' is used after nouns ending in a vowel (e.g., '머리가' - hair is).
--- word ---
term: 새로워?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 새롭다 + ~어
meaning: is it new?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) question form of '새롭다' (to be new). The standard polite form would be '새로워요?'. Dropping the '요' makes it casual, suitable for talking to close friends or fans in this context.

=== [15:42] 별이 다섯 개 ===
meaning: Five stars.
tone: Humorous, referential. Describing her spiky hair.
--- word ---
term: 별이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 별 + 이
meaning: star(s) (are)
notes: 별 means 'star'. The particle '이' marks it as the subject. In this phrase, it implies 'There are stars' or 'The stars are...'. The context completes the meaning.
--- word ---
term: 다섯
type: Numeral
breakdown: 
meaning: five
notes: This is the native Korean number for five, used for counting objects, age, and hours. The Sino-Korean number for five is '오' (o), used for dates, money, minutes, etc.
--- word ---
term: 개
type: Counter Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: item(s)
notes: The most common counter for inanimate objects. When counting, the structure is Noun + Number + Counter (e.g., '사과 다섯 개' - five apples). Here, the noun '별' is mentioned first, so it's '별이 다섯 개' (stars, five of them).
--- grammar ---
pattern: 별이 다섯 개
meaning: A popular catchphrase meaning 'five stars', originating from a famous 2000s mattress commercial. It's used humorously to mean something is of top quality or, in this case, to describe her spiky 'star-like' hair.
example: 이 식당 음식은 별이 다섯 개야! (This restaurant's food is five stars!)
level: Intermediate

=== [15:44] 별이 다섯 개라 그래 ===
meaning: (It's) because it's five stars.
tone: Explanatory, casual, humorous.
--- word ---
term: 별이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 별 + 이
meaning: star(s) (are)
notes: 별 means 'star'. The particle '이' marks it as the subject of the clause.
--- word ---
term: 다섯
type: Numeral
breakdown: 
meaning: five
notes: The native Korean number for five. It modifies the counter '개'.
--- word ---
term: 개라
type: Noun + Copula + Ending
breakdown: 개 + (이)라서
meaning: because it is... items
notes: This is a colloquial contraction of '개라서'. The pattern 'Noun + -(이)라서' means 'because it is (Noun)'. '라서' is used for nouns ending in a vowel, and '이라서' for nouns ending in a consonant. The '서' is often dropped in casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 그래
type: Verb
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~어
meaning: it is so / that's why
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) form of '그렇다' (to be so). It's a very common conversational verb used to agree ('that's right') or to conclude a thought ('that's how it is').
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)라(서) 그래
meaning: A common conversational pattern used to give a reason. It translates to 'It's because (Noun)'.
example: 학생이라서 돈이 없어. (I don't have money because I'm a student.)
level: Beginner

=== [15:47] 타투를 했어? ===
meaning: Did you get a tattoo?
tone: Informal, questioning. Reading a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 타투를
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 타투 + 를
meaning: a tattoo (as the object)
notes: 타투 is a loanword from English 'tattoo'. The particle '를' marks it as the direct object of the verb '하다' (to do). '를' is used after nouns ending in a vowel, while '을' is used after nouns ending in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 했어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~었어
meaning: did you do?
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) past tense question form of '하다' (to do). '타투를 하다' is the standard expression for 'to get a tattoo'. The polite form would be '했어요?'.

=== [15:54] 무슨 타투를 했어? ===
meaning: What kind of tattoo did you get?
tone: Informal, follow-up question. Reading a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 무슨
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: what / what kind of
notes: '무슨' is used before a noun to ask about its type or identity. It's often confused with '어떤' (which/what kind of) and '무엇' (what). '무슨' asks to identify something unknown (무슨 일이야? - What's going on?), while '어떤' asks about the characteristics of something known (어떤 스타일 좋아해? - What kind of style do you like?).
--- word ---
term: 타투를
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 타투 + 를
meaning: a tattoo (as the object)
notes: 타투 is a loanword from English 'tattoo'. The particle '를' marks it as the direct object of the verb '했어' (did).
--- word ---
term: 했어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~었어
meaning: did you do?
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) past tense question form of '하다' (to do). The phrase '무슨 타투를 했어?' is a natural way to ask 'What tattoo did you get?'

=== [15:56] 푸들해서 갑자기 고양이야 ===
meaning: From a poodle, it's suddenly a cat?
tone: Confused, playful, informal. Reacting to a fan's comment.
--- word ---
term: 푸들해서
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 푸들 + 하다 + ~어서
meaning: did a poodle and then...
notes: This is a creative use of language. '푸들' (poodle) is combined with '하다' (to do) to mean 'get a poodle tattoo'. The '~어서' ending connects this action sequentially to the next clause, meaning 'after getting a poodle tattoo...'. This kind of 'Noun + 하다' formation is very common.
--- word ---
term: 갑자기
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: suddenly
notes: A common adverb used to describe an unexpected or abrupt change or action. It can be placed at the beginning of a sentence or right before the verb it modifies.
--- word ---
term: 고양이야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 고양이 + ~이야
meaning: it's a cat?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) form of 'it is a cat'. The copula '이다' (to be) attaches to a noun. '이야' is used for nouns ending in a vowel (like 고양이), and '이야' is used for nouns ending in a consonant (e.g., '사람이야' - it's a person).

=== [16:16] 감사해요 ===
meaning: Thank you.
tone: Polite, standard, appreciative.
--- word ---
term: 감사해요
type: Verb
breakdown: 감사하다 + ~아요/어요
meaning: Thank you.
notes: This is the polite, informal 'haeyo' (해요) style. It's formed from the verb stem 감사하- (gamsaha-) + the polite ending ~어요 (eoyo), which contracts to ~해요 (haeyo). It's less formal than 감사합니다 (gamsahamnida) but more formal than the casual 반말 (banmal) 고마워 (gomawo). It's a very common and safe way to say 'thank you' in most daily situations.

=== [16:25] 근육 보여? ===

=== [16:36] 보여줘야지 ===

--- word ---

type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Back
notes: 
--- word ---
term: 상태를

notes: `상태` means 'state' or 'condition.' `~를` is the object particle, used because `상태` ends in a vowel and is the object of the verb `몰랐어` (didn't know).

=== [16:53] 나도 팬들이 올려준 걸 보고 알았어 ===

--- word ---
term: 나도
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + ~도
meaning: Me too, I also
notes: The particle `~도` means 'also' or 'too.' It replaces subject (`~이/가`) or topic (`~은/는`) particles when used.
--- word ---
term: 팬들이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 팬 + ~들 + ~이
meaning: The fans
notes: `팬` is 'fan,' `~들` is the plural marker, and `~이` is the subject particle, indicating the fans performed the action.
--- word ---
term: 올려준
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 올려주다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: that (they) uploaded
notes: The base verb is `올려주다` (to upload for someone's benefit), from `올리다` (to upload) + `~어 주다`. The `~ㄴ` ending changes the past-tense verb into an adjective that modifies the following noun, `걸`.
--- word ---
term: 걸
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 것 + 을 (contraction)
meaning: the thing that
notes: `걸` is a common spoken contraction of `것을`. `것` means 'thing' or 'the fact that,' and `~을` is the object particle. Here, it refers to the photos or videos the fans uploaded.
--- word ---
term: 보고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 보다 + ~고
meaning: after seeing
notes: The `~고` ending connects two clauses sequentially, meaning 'and' or 'and then.' It indicates that the action of 'seeing' happened before the action of 'finding out'.
--- word ---
term: 알았어
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + ~았어
meaning: I found out
notes: Informal past tense of `알다` (to know). In this context, it means 'came to know' or 'found out,' rather than 'already knew'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-고
meaning: A connective ending that links two actions or states in chronological order. It means 'A and then B'.
example: 밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요. (I ate and then watched a movie.)
level: Beginner

--- word ---
term: 가가지고

breakdown: 가다 + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: went and...
notes: A very common colloquial connective form. It can mean 'A and then B' (sequence) or 'Because of A, B' (reason). Here, it indicates sequence: 'I went and then...'. It's often shortened to '가갖고' in speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~게 되다
meaning: Indicates a change of state or coming to be in a certain situation, often outside of the subject's direct control. It means 'to end up ~ing' or 'to get to ~ing'.
example: 한국 음식을 좋아하게 됐어요. (I've come to like Korean food.)

=== [17:26] 아 진짜 몰랐어 ===

tone: Informal, emphatic, expressing genuine surprise.

type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah, oh
notes: A common interjection used to express surprise, realization, or pain. Its meaning is heavily dependent on intonation.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, for real
notes: Extremely common in spoken Korean to add emphasis, similar to 'literally' or 'seriously' in English. More colloquial than formal alternatives like 정말 (jeongmal) or 참 (cham).
--- word ---
term: 몰랐어
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 + -았- + -어
meaning: I didn't know
notes: This is the past tense informal form of 모르다 (moreuda, to not know). It's a '르' irregular verb; when a vowel ending like -아/어 is added, the '르' changes to '라' or '러' and an extra 'ㄹ' is added to the preceding syllable. So, 모르 + 았어 becomes 몰랐어 (mollasseo).

=== [17:35] 아니 그러니까 선생님도 되게 뿌듯해지거든 ===

--- word ---
term: 아니
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: No, I mean...
notes: While it means 'no', it's often used in conversation not to negate the previous statement, but as a filler word to introduce a clarification or a slightly different point, similar to 'Well...' or 'I mean...' in English.
--- word ---
term: 그러니까
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: so, therefore, that's why
notes: Used to connect a cause/reason to a result. In conversation, it can also mean 'I mean...' or 'What I'm saying is...' to rephrase or clarify a point.
--- word ---
term: 선생님도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 선생님 + 도
meaning: the trainer too
notes: 선생님 (seonsaengnim) literally means 'teacher', but it's a respectful title for experts in many fields, including personal trainers. The particle 도 (do) means 'also' or 'too'.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really
notes: A colloquial adverb meaning 'very'. It's very common in speech but less so in formal writing. Synonyms include 아주 (aju), 매우 (maeu), and 진짜 (jinjja).
--- word ---
term: 뿌듯해지거든
type: Verb
breakdown: 뿌듯하다 + -아/어지다 + -거든
meaning: because (he/she) becomes proud
notes: A combination of three grammar points: 1. 뿌듯하다 (ppudeuthada) - to be proud/satisfied. 2. -아/어지다 (-a/eojida) - shows a change of state, 'to become'. 3. -거든 (-geodeun) - an ending used to provide a reason or background information the listener might not know.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A + -아/어지다
meaning: Attaches to an adjective to indicate a change of state, meaning 'to become (adjective)'.
example: 날씨가 추워졌어요. (Nalssiga chuwojyeosseoyo.) - The weather has become cold.
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -거든(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to provide a reason or background for a previous or following statement. It has a nuance of '...you see,' or '...because...'.
example: 어제 못 잤거든. (Eoje mot jatgeodeun.) - (That's because) I couldn't sleep yesterday, you see.
level: Intermediate

term: 제일
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: the most, number one
notes: Used to form the superlative (e.g., 'the best', 'the biggest'). It is often interchangeable with 가장 (gajang), but 제일 is considered slightly more colloquial.
--- word ---
term: 마음에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 마음 + 에
meaning: in the heart/mind
notes: 마음 (maeum) means 'heart' or 'mind'. Combined with the location particle 에 (e), it's part of the idiom '마음에 들다' (maeume deulda).
--- word ---
term: 드는
type: Verb
breakdown: 들다 + -는
meaning: that I like
notes: The present tense modifying form of 들다 (deulda, to enter). The full idiom 마음에 들다 literally means 'to enter the heart' and is the standard way to say 'to like' or 'to be to one's liking'.
--- word ---
term: 근육이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 근육 + 이
meaning: muscle (is)
notes: 근육 (geunyuk) means 'muscle'. The particle 이 (i) marks it as the subject of the clause.
--- word ---
term: 어디냐고?
type: Interrogative + Ending
breakdown: 어디 + -냐 + -고
meaning: (you ask) where is it?
notes: This is a quoted question. 어디 (eodi) is 'where', -냐 (-nya) is an informal question ending, and -고 (-go) is the quoting particle. She is repeating a question she read in the comments.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 마음에 들다
meaning: An idiomatic expression meaning 'to like' or 'to be to one's liking'. It is used when something is appealing to you.
example: 이 옷이 마음에 들어요. (I osi maeume deureoyo.) - I like these clothes.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(느)냐고 (하다)
meaning: A grammar pattern used for quoting questions. The full form includes 하다 (to do/say), but it's often omitted in speech.
example: 친구가 언제 오냐고 물었어요. (Chinguga eonje onyago mureosseoyo.) - My friend asked when I'm coming.
level: Intermediate

=== [17:52] 근육 ===
meaning: Muscle...
tone: Informal, repeating the key word while thinking.
--- word ---
term: 근육
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: muscle
notes: She repeats the noun from the fan's question to herself as she considers her answer.

=== [17:54] 어깨인 것 같아 ===
meaning: I think it's my shoulders.
tone: Informal, speculative, giving a personal opinion.
--- word ---
term: 어깨인
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 어깨 + 이다 + -ㄴ
meaning: that is shoulder(s)
notes: This form combines the noun 어깨 (eokkae, shoulder) with the copula (verb 'to be') 이다 (ida) and the noun-modifying ending -ㄴ (-n). It allows the noun to describe the following noun, 것 (geot).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: thing, the fact that
notes: A dependent noun used in many grammatical constructions. Here, it works with -ㄴ/은/는 것 같다 to mean 'it seems like' or 'I think that'.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 + -아
meaning: it seems, it's like
notes: The informal present tense form of 같다 (gatda, to be like/similar). It's used to express conjecture, opinion, or resemblance.
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + -(이)ㄴ/는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, thought, or opinion about something. It softens the statement, making it less direct. Translates to 'I think it's N' or 'It seems to be N'.
example: 저 사람은 학생인 것 같아요. (Jeo sarameun haksaengin geot gatayo.) - I think that person is a student.
level: Beginner

=== [18:03] 난 그냥 어깨를 좋아해 ===
meaning: I just like shoulders.
tone: Informal, stating a simple, direct preference.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + 는
meaning: as for me, I
notes: A common contraction of 나는 (naneun), which is the informal 'I' + the topic particle 는 (neun). Using the topic particle here emphasizes that this is her personal preference.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, simply
notes: A very versatile adverb. It can mean 'just' or 'simply', implying there's no special reason. It can also mean 'to leave things as they are'.
--- word ---
term: 어깨를
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 어깨 + 를
meaning: shoulders
notes: 어깨 (eokkae) means 'shoulder'. The particle 를 (reul) marks it as the direct object of the verb 좋아해 (joahae, to like).
--- word ---
term: 좋아해
type: Verb
breakdown: 좋아하다 + -아/어
meaning: I like
notes: The informal present tense of 좋아하다 (joahada, to like). A common point of confusion for learners is the difference with 좋다 (jota, to be good). 좋아하다 is a verb that takes an object (e.g., 어깨를 좋아해), while 좋다 is a descriptive verb/adjective that describes a subject (e.g., 어깨가 좋아 - The shoulders are good).

=== [18:05] 사람의 어깨를 좋아해 ===
meaning: I like people's shoulders.
tone: Casual, declarative, expressing a personal preference.
--- word ---
term: 사람의
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 사람 (person) + ~의 (possessive particle)
meaning: person's
notes: The particle ~의 indicates possession, similar to 's in English. In casual conversation, it's often omitted if the context is clear (e.g., 사람 어깨), but its inclusion here makes the statement sound a bit more deliberate.
--- word ---
term: 어깨를
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 어깨 (shoulder) + ~를 (object particle)
meaning: shoulders (as the object)
notes: ~를 is the object marking particle used after a noun ending in a vowel. It marks '어깨' as the direct object of the verb '좋아해'. The consonant version is ~을.
--- word ---
term: 좋아해
type: Verb
breakdown: 좋아하다 (to like) -> 좋아하- + ~아/어 (informal present tense)
meaning: (I) like
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) form of 좋아하다. The polite form is 좋아해요. The subject 'I' (나는) is omitted, which is very common in Korean when the context makes it obvious.

=== [18:07] 어깨 선이 너무 좋아 ===
meaning: I really like the shoulder line.
tone: Casual, emphatic, expressing a strong preference.
--- word ---
term: 어깨
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: shoulder
notes: Here, 어깨 functions as a modifier for the next noun, 선 (line), creating the compound noun '어깨 선' (shoulder line).
--- word ---
term: 선이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 선 (line) + ~이 (subject particle)
meaning: the line (is)
notes: The particle ~이 marks '선' (line) as the subject of the sentence. It's used after nouns ending in a consonant. The vowel version is ~가.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: very, really
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too' in a negative sense, in modern colloquial Korean, 너무 is very commonly used to mean 'very' or 'really' in positive contexts, as seen here.
--- word ---
term: 좋아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 좋다 (to be good) -> 좋- + ~아/어 (informal present tense)
meaning: (it's) good / (I) like
notes: This is the adjective 좋다 (to be good), not the verb 좋아하다 (to like). When the subject is marked with ~이/가, 좋다 often translates to 'to like'. For example, '사과가 좋아' means 'I like apples' (literally, 'Apples are good'). This is a common point of confusion for learners.

=== [18:13] 근데 내 어깨가 뭐 예쁜 어깨인지는 모르겠는데 ===
meaning: But I don't know if my shoulders are, like, pretty shoulders...
tone: Casual, self-deprecating, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데 -> 근데 (contraction)
meaning: but, however
notes: 근데 is a very common spoken contraction of 그런데. It's used to connect contrasting ideas or to change the subject.
--- word ---
term: 내
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 나 (I) + ~의 (possessive particle) -> 내
meaning: my
notes: 내 is the informal possessive pronoun 'my', a contraction of 나의. The formal equivalent is 제, which is a contraction of 저의.
--- word ---
term: 어깨가
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 어깨 (shoulder) + ~가 (subject particle)
meaning: shoulders (as the subject)
notes: The particle ~가 marks '어깨' as the subject of the clause. It is used after nouns that end in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 뭐
type: Interjection / Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: like, well
notes: Here, 뭐 is used as a filler word to make the sentence sound more conversational and less direct, similar to 'like' or 'well' in English. It can also mean 'what'.
--- word ---
term: 예쁜
type: Adjective (modifier form)
breakdown: 예쁘다 (to be pretty) + ~ㄴ (modifier ending)
meaning: pretty
notes: The ~ㄴ/은 ending attaches to an adjective stem to allow it to modify a following noun. Since 예쁘다 ends in a vowel, ~ㄴ is added.
--- word ---
term: 어깨인지는
type: Noun + copula + grammar
breakdown: 어깨 (shoulder) + 이다 (to be) + ~ㄴ지는
meaning: whether it is shoulders
notes: This combines the noun '어깨' with the copula '이다' (to be) and the grammar pattern ~ㄴ/은/는지는, which means 'whether or not...'. It is used to embed a question or uncertainty into a sentence.
--- word ---
term: 모르겠는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 (to not know) + ~겠~ (conjecture) + ~는데 (background/contrast ending)
meaning: I don't know, but...
notes: The ~겠~ suffix here softens the statement, like 'I guess I don't know'. The ~는데 ending provides background or contrast, leaving the sentence unfinished and trailing off.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~(으)ㄴ/는지는 모르겠다
meaning: Used to express uncertainty about a fact or quality, meaning 'I don't know if/whether...'.
example: 그 사람이 학생인지는 모르겠어요. (I don't know if that person is a student.)
level: Intermediate

type: Noun + particle

term: 되게

breakdown: 
meaning: very, really

meaning: Expresses a guess, opinion, or impression softly. It translates to 'I think that...' or 'It seems like...'.

=== [18:24] 뭐 어때 ===
meaning: So what?
tone: Casual, rhetorical, confident, dismissive.
--- word ---
term: 뭐
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: what
notes: Used here as part of a common idiomatic expression.
--- word ---
term: 어때
type: Adjective
breakdown: 어떻다 (to be how) -> 어때 (informal present tense)
meaning: how is it?
notes: 어떻다 is an irregular adjective. Its informal present tense form is 어때, and the polite form is 어때요. The phrase '뭐 어때?' is a fixed expression.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 뭐 어때(요)?
meaning: A common rhetorical phrase meaning 'So what?', 'Who cares?', or 'What's wrong with that?'. It's used to dismiss a potential problem or criticism.

notes: This adverb is often accompanied by a physical gesture to show 'how' something is. Hwasa is likely gesturing to show the shape of her shoulders.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 조금 (a little) -> 좀 (contraction)
meaning: a bit, kind of
notes: 좀 is a very common contraction of 조금. It's used to soften a statement, making it sound less direct. Here it means 'kind of' or 'a little'.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + ~게 (adverbial ending)
meaning: like this
notes: Repeating the word adds emphasis to the manner or gesture she is describing.
--- word ---
term: 산
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: mountain
notes: Here, '산' (mountain) is used with '모양' (shape) to describe her shoulders as being mountain-shaped.

breakdown: 모양 (shape) + 이다 (to be) + ~기도 하다
meaning: it is also the shape of
notes: This is part of the '~기도 하다' grammar pattern, which means 'to also be...' or 'is also...'. It adds another characteristic to the subject.
--- word ---

meaning: and...
notes: The ~고 ending connects clauses and can mean 'and' or 'and then'. Here, it's used to trail off, leaving the sentence unfinished as if she's about to list another characteristic.
--- grammar ---

meaning: Means 'to also be (Noun)' or 'is also (Noun)'. It's used to add another quality, role, or identity to the subject.

=== [18:34] 너 그래? ===

tone: Informal, direct question to a fan.
--- word ---
term: 너
type: pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: you
notes: This is the informal pronoun for 'you' (반말, banmal). It should only be used with people of the same age or younger, or close friends. Using it with older people or strangers is considered rude. The formal equivalent is the listener's title, like 선생님 (seonsaengnim), or omitting the subject.
--- word ---
term: 그래?
type: verb
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~아/어?
meaning: Really? / Is that so?
notes: This is the informal, non-polite conjugation of 그렇다 (geureota), meaning 'to be so'. It's a very common conversational phrase used to show agreement, ask for confirmation, or express surprise. The rising intonation makes it a question.

=== [18:40] 하체는 열심히 더 해야 되고 ===
meaning: I have to work harder on my lower body, and...
tone: Informal, self-reflective, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 하체는
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 하체 + 는
meaning: lower body (as for)
notes: 하체 (hache) means 'lower body', often used in a fitness or workout context. The particle ~는 (neun) is a topic marker, often used to introduce a topic or for contrast. Here, she's likely contrasting her lower body with her upper body, which she was just discussing.
--- word ---
term: 열심히
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: diligently, hard
notes: A very common adverb used to describe doing something with effort and zeal. It's derived from the noun 열심 (yeolsim), meaning 'enthusiasm' or 'diligence'.
--- word ---
term: 더
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: more
notes: Placed before a verb or adverb to indicate a greater degree. For example, 더 빨리 (deo ppalli) means 'faster'.
--- word ---
term: 해야
type: verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~아/어야
meaning: have to do
notes: This is part of the ~아/어야 하다/되다 (a/eoya hada/doeda) grammar pattern, which expresses obligation or necessity. 하다 means 'to do'.
--- word ---
term: 되고
type: verb ending
breakdown: 되다 + ~고
meaning: and...
notes: The ~고 (go) ending connects two clauses, meaning 'and' or 'and then'. Here, it leaves the sentence unfinished, a common feature in spoken Korean where the rest of the thought is implied or will be stated next.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, meaning 'must' or 'have to'.
example: 지금 가야 돼요 (jigeum gaya dwaeyo) - I have to go now.
level: Beginner

=== [18:58] 맞아 그래서 나도 이렇게 어깨가 드러나는 옷을 입으면 ===

--- word ---

breakdown: 맞다 + ~아/어
meaning: That's right.
notes: The informal way to agree with someone. The polite form is 맞아요 (majayo). It's a very common conversational response.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: so, therefore
notes: A common conjunction used to connect two sentences where the first is the reason or cause for the second.
--- word ---
term: 나도
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 나 + 도
meaning: I also, me too
notes: 나 (na) is the informal pronoun for 'I'. The particle ~도 (do) means 'also' or 'too', and it replaces the subject or object particles 이/가 (i/ga) or 을/를 (eul/reul).
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: like this
notes: A contraction of 이러하게 (ireohage). It's often used while gesturing to demonstrate what 'this' is. The related words are 그렇게 (geureoke - like that) and 저렇게 (jeoreoke - like that over there).
--- word ---
term: 어깨가
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 어깨 + 가
meaning: shoulders (as subject)
notes: 어깨 (eokkae) means 'shoulder'. The particle ~가 (ga) marks it as the subject of the verb 드러나다 (deureonada - to be revealed).
--- word ---
term: 드러나는
type: verb
breakdown: 드러나다 + ~는
meaning: that reveals
notes: 드러나다 (deureonada) means 'to be revealed' or 'to come into view'. The ~는 (neun) ending turns the verb into a present-tense adjective that modifies the following noun, 옷 (ot - clothes).

term: 옷을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 옷 + 을
meaning: clothes (as object)

breakdown: 입다 + ~(으)면
meaning: if/when (I) wear
notes: The ~(으)면 (eumyeon) ending attaches to a verb stem to create a conditional clause, meaning 'if' or 'when'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 + Noun
meaning: A present-tense noun-modifying form. It turns an action verb into an adjective describing the noun.
example: 책을 읽는 사람 (chaegeul ingneun saram) - a person who is reading a book.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/Adj + ~(으)면
meaning: A conditional ending meaning 'if' or 'when'. Use ~으면 after a consonant and ~면 after a vowel.
example: 비가 오면 집에 있을 거예요 (biga omyeon jibe isseul geoyeyo) - If it rains, I'll stay home.
level: Beginner

=== [19:03] 뭔가 그날의 기분도 좋고 ===

type: noun + particle

term: 좋고
type: adjective
breakdown: 좋다 + ~고
meaning: is good, and...
notes: 좋다 (jota) means 'to be good'. The ~고 (go) ending connects this clause to another one, listing related facts or actions. It implies she is about to say another positive thing.

=== [19:06] 좀 그게 있는 것 같아 ===
meaning: I think there's something like that.
tone: Informal, speculative, conversational.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: adverb
breakdown: 조금
meaning: a bit, kind of
notes: A contraction of 조금 (jogeum). While it means 'a little', it's often used in conversation to soften a statement, making it sound less direct or assertive, similar to 'kind of' or 'sort of'.
--- word ---
term: 그게
type: pronoun + particle
breakdown: 그것 + 이
meaning: that thing
notes: A common colloquial contraction of 그것이 (geugeos-i). 그것 (geugeot) means 'that thing' (near the listener or previously mentioned), and 이 (i) is the subject particle.
--- word ---
term: 있는
type: verb
breakdown: 있다 + ~는
meaning: that exists
notes: The present tense noun-modifying form of 있다 (itda - to exist). It is part of the larger grammar pattern ~는 것 같다 (neun geot gatda).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: noun
breakdown: 
meaning: thing, fact
notes: A dependent noun that means 'thing' or 'fact'. It's used in many common grammar patterns, including ~는 것 (neun geot), which nominalizes a verb clause.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: adjective
breakdown: 같다 + ~아/어
meaning: it seems, I think
notes: The informal conjugation of 같다 (gatta - to be like, to seem). It's used to express an opinion, guess, or impression in a gentle, non-assertive way.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, thought, or opinion about a present action or state. It translates to 'I think...' or 'It seems like...'.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요 (biga oneun geot gatayo) - I think it's raining.
level: Beginner

=== [19:08] 그날의 기분이 그냥 가벼워 ===
meaning: My mood on that day is just light.
tone: Informal, descriptive, clarifying.
--- word ---
term: 그날의
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 그날 + 의
meaning: that day's
notes: 그날 (geunal) means 'that day'. The particle ~의 (ui) indicates possession. In speech, it's often pronounced as 에 (e) or omitted.
--- word ---
term: 기분이
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 기분 + 이
meaning: mood (as subject)
notes: 기분 (gibun) means 'mood'. The particle ~이 (i) marks it as the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, simply
notes: A very versatile adverb. It can mean 'just as it is', 'for no particular reason', or 'simply'. Here, it implies the lightness of her mood is a simple, direct result.
--- word ---
term: 가벼워
type: adjective
breakdown: 가볍다 + ~아/어
meaning: is light
notes: This is the informal conjugation of 가볍다 (gabyeopda - to be light). This is a 'ㅂ' irregular verb. When conjugating with a vowel ending like ~아/어요, the ㅂ changes to 우, which then combines with 어 to become 워 (가볍 + 어요 -> 가벼워요).

meaning: is difficult/hard

=== [19:12] 항상 뭔가 조금이라도 무거운 옷을 입으면 ===
meaning: If I always wear clothes that are even a little bit heavy...
tone: Informal, setting up a condition, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 항상
type: adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: always
notes: A common adverb for frequency. Similar words include 늘 (neul - always) and 언제나 (eonjena - always).
--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: pronoun/adverb
breakdown: 무엇인가
meaning: somehow, kind of
notes: Used here as a filler or to soften the statement, making 'a little bit heavy' sound less precise and more like a general feeling.
--- word ---
term: 조금이라도
type: adverb + particle
breakdown: 조금 + 이라도
meaning: even a little bit
notes: The particle ~(이)라도 (irado) means 'at least' or 'even if it's just...'. It implies that even the smallest amount of the preceding noun is enough to cause the following result. For example, 물이라도 마셔 (murirado masyeo) - 'At least drink some water'.
--- word ---
term: 무거운
type: adjective
breakdown: 무겁다 + ~(으)ㄴ
meaning: heavy
notes: This is the noun-modifying form of 무겁다 (mugeopda - to be heavy). It's a 'ㅂ' irregular adjective. The ㅂ changes to 우 before the ~(으)ㄴ ending, becoming 무거운 (mugeoun).
--- word ---
term: 옷을
type: noun + particle
breakdown: 옷 + 을
meaning: clothes (as object)
notes: 옷 (ot) means 'clothes'. The particle ~을 (eul) marks it as the direct object of the verb 입으면 (ibeumyeon).
--- word ---
term: 입으면
type: verb
breakdown: 입다 + ~(으)면
meaning: if/when (I) wear
notes: The ~(으)면 (eumyeon) ending creates a conditional clause. The sentence is left incomplete, implying the negative consequence of wearing heavy clothes will be explained next.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + (이)라도
meaning: Means 'at least' or 'even if it's just (the noun)'. It suggests the noun is not the best option, but is better than nothing or is sufficient.
example: 커피는 없어요. 차라도 괜찮아요? (keopineun eopseoyo. charado gwaenchanayo?) - There's no coffee. Is even tea okay?
level: Intermediate

=== [19:17] 게을러지는 것 같아 ===
meaning: I think I'm getting lazy.
tone: Informal, self-reflective, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 게을러지는
type: Adjective
breakdown: 게으르다 + ~어지다 + ~는
meaning: getting lazy
notes: This is the verb '게으르다' (to be lazy) combined with the '~어지다' pattern, which indicates a change of state ('to become'). The final '~는' turns the entire phrase into an adjective that can modify a noun, in this case, '것' (thing/fact).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: the fact that / the thing
notes: Here, '것' acts as a nominalizer, turning the preceding clause '게을러지는' (getting lazy) into a noun phrase. This is essential for the '~것 같다' (it seems like) pattern.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 같다 + ~아
meaning: it seems / I think
notes: The informal, non-polite ending '~아' is attached to the adjective stem '같다' (to be like). This form, '같아', is very common in casual conversation (반말, banmal) among friends or people of similar age.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~는/은/을 것 같다
meaning: Used to express a guess, supposition, or opinion ('I think...', 'It seems like...'). It softens the statement compared to a direct declaration.
example: 비가 오는 것 같아요. (It seems like it's raining.)
level: Beginner

=== [19:18] 이게 좀 몸도 무거워지고 ===
meaning: This... my body is getting heavy and...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 이게
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 이것 + 이 (subject particle)
meaning: this thing
notes: A common contraction of '이것이'. '이것' means 'this thing'. In spoken Korean, contractions like 이게 (this thing is), 그게 (that thing is), and 저게 (that thing over there is) are far more common than their full forms.
--- word ---
term: 좀
type: Adverb
breakdown: 조금 (contraction)
meaning: a bit / kind of
notes: '좀' is a contraction of '조금' (a little). It's frequently used in conversation not just to mean 'a little', but also to soften a statement or request, making it sound less direct or abrupt.
--- word ---
term: 몸도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 몸 + ~도
meaning: my body also
notes: The particle '~도' means 'also' or 'too'. It attaches to a noun to indicate that it is an addition to something previously mentioned or implied. Here, it adds 'my body' to the list of things contributing to her feeling lazy.
--- word ---
term: 무거워지고
type: Verb
breakdown: 무겁다 + ~어지다 + ~고
meaning: getting heavy and...
notes: The adjective '무겁다' (to be heavy) is combined with '~어지다' to mean 'to become heavy'. The '~고' ending is a connective, used to link clauses. It can mean 'and' or indicate a sequence of events. The sentence is left unfinished, which is common in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A + ~아/어지다
meaning: This pattern attaches to an adjective stem to indicate a change of state, meaning 'to become [adjective]'.
example: 날씨가 추워졌어요. (The weather became cold.)
level: Beginner

breakdown: 이제 + 는
meaning: Now (as for now)
notes: 이제 (ije) means "now." The topic particle 는 (neun) is added for emphasis or contrast, implying "from now on" or "as for now (unlike before)." It sets the current time as the topic of the sentence. It's often contracted to 이젠 (ijen) in speech.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: An extremely common adverb that modifies verbs to indicate that an action is done well, properly, or to a good extent.
--- word ---
term: 먹고
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + ~고
meaning: eat and...
notes: The verb stem 먹다 (meokda, "to eat") is combined with the connecting ending ~고 (~go), which links two clauses or actions in sequence. It means "and" or "and then." For example, "밥을 먹고 영화를 봤어요" (babeul meokgo yeonghwareul bwasseoyo) means "I ate and then watched a movie."
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: An extremely common adverb that modifies verbs to indicate that an action is done well, properly, or to a good extent. Here, it modifies the verb 쉬어 (swieo), "to rest."
--- word ---
term: 쉬어
type: Verb
breakdown: 쉬다 + ~어
meaning: rest
notes: This is an informal, non-polite command form (반말, banmal). It's formed by dropping the ~요 (~yo) from the standard polite form 쉬어요 (swieoyo). It's used with close friends, family, or people younger than the speaker. Hwasa uses it here to speak to her fans in a very close and familiar way.

meaning: Be

--- word ---
term: happy
type: Loanword (Adjective)
breakdown: 
meaning: happy
notes: The second part of the English phrase. The Korean equivalent would be 행복해 (haengbokhae) in an informal command form.

=== [20:49] 나로 말할 것 같으면 ===
meaning: If you're asking about me...
tone: Informal, introductory, slightly dramatic. A set phrase.
--- word ---
term: 나로
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + ~(으)로
meaning: as for me
notes: 나 (na) is the informal pronoun for "I/me." The particle ~(으)로 (~(eu)ro) has many meanings, including "with/by means of." In this specific phrase, it helps to designate "me" as the subject of the following explanation.
--- word ---
term: 말할
type: Verb (future determiner form)
breakdown: 말하다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: that will be spoken of
notes: 말하다 (malhada) means "to speak." The ~ㄹ/을 (~l/eul) ending turns the verb into a future tense adjective that can modify a noun. Here, it modifies the noun 것 (geot).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: thing / fact
notes: 것 (geot) is a dependent noun meaning "thing," "fact," or "the act of." It's extremely common and is often used in grammar patterns. Here, 말할 것 (malhal geot) literally means "the thing to speak of."
--- word ---
term: 같으면
type: Adjective + Conditional ending
breakdown: 같다 + ~(으)면
meaning: if it is like...
notes: 같다 (gatda) means "to be like" or "to be the same." The conditional ending ~(으)면 (~(eu)myeon) means "if" or "when." Together, it forms part of the set phrase.
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + ~(으)로 말할 것 같으면
meaning: A set phrase used to introduce a topic about someone or something, often oneself. It translates to "If you're asking about..." or "As for..." It has a slightly formal or literary feel, but Hwasa uses it playfully here to introduce herself.
example: 그 사람으로 말할 것 같으면, 정말 착해요. (As for that person, they are really kind.)
level: Intermediate

=== [21:12] 미국에서 재밌는 썰 풀어주라고? ===
meaning: (You're asking me to) tell a fun story from the US?
tone: Informal, questioning, repeating a fan's request.
--- word ---
term: 미국에서
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 미국 + ~에서
meaning: in/from the US
notes: 미국 (miguk) means "USA." The particle ~에서 (~eseo) indicates the location where an action takes place. It can mean "in," "at," or "from." Context confirms the speaker is in the US.
--- word ---
term: 재밌는
type: Adjective
breakdown: 재미있다 + ~는
meaning: fun / interesting
notes: The descriptive verb/adjective 재미있다 (jaemiitda) means "to be fun/interesting." The ~는 (~neun) ending is used to turn it into an adjective that modifies a noun in the present tense.
--- word ---
term: 썰
type: Noun (slang)
breakdown: 
meaning: story / tale
notes: 썰 (sseol) is modern slang derived from the word 설 (seol), meaning "tale" or "theory." It refers to a personal story, anecdote, or piece of gossip. It's very common in online and casual speech among younger generations. The phrase 썰을 풀다 (sseoreul pulda) means "to tell a story."
--- word ---
term: 풀어주라고?
type: Verb + Quoted command ending
breakdown: 풀다 + ~어 주다 + ~라고?
meaning: (you ask me to) tell?
notes: A complex ending. 1) 풀다 (pulda) means "to untie," but in 썰을 풀다 it means "to tell a story." 2) ~어 주다 (~eo juda) indicates doing an action for someone. 3) ~라고? (~rago?) is an informal ending for quoting a command or request and turning it into a question. The full meaning is "Are you telling me to tell a story (for you)?"
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~어/아 주다
meaning: An auxiliary verb construction that means "to do [verb] for someone." It adds a sense of favor or service to the main verb.
example: 문을 열어주세요. (Please open the door for me.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~(으)라고?
meaning: An informal quoted speech ending used to repeat or question what someone else has commanded or requested. It implies surprise or clarification.
example: 지금 가라고? (You're telling me to go now?)
level: Intermediate

=== [21:15] 분명히 진짜 일이 많았거든? ===
meaning: There were definitely a lot of things that happened, you know?
tone: Informal, conversational, setting up a story.
--- word ---
term: 분명히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 분명하다 + ~히
meaning: clearly, definitely
notes: An adverb that adds certainty or emphasis to a statement. It's formed by adding the adverbial suffix ~히 to the adjective stem 분명하다 (to be clear).
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly
notes: A very common word in spoken Korean, used for emphasis. It's often used interchangeably with 정말 (jeongmal), but 진짜 can feel slightly more colloquial or casual.
--- word ---
term: 일이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 일 + ~이
meaning: things that happened
notes: 일 means 'work', 'matter', or 'event'. The particle ~이 is the subject marker used after a noun ending in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 많았거든?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 많다 + ~았~ + ~거든
meaning: there were a lot, you see?
notes: The ending ~거든(요) is used to provide a reason or background information. When used with a question mark as ~거든?, it seeks the listener's agreement or engagement, similar to saying '...you know?' or '...you see?' in English. It creates a friendly, intimate tone.

notes: Refers to something previously mentioned or known to both the speaker and listener. It's one of the three core demonstratives: 이 (this), 그 (that), 저 (that over there).

meaning: fig

=== [21:39] 아 이거는 공식적으로 풀어도 되는지를 모르겠다 ===

tone: Informal, hesitant, expressing uncertainty.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah
notes: An expression of realization or hesitation.
--- word ---
term: 이거는
type: Pronoun + Particle (Contracted)
breakdown: 이것 + 은
meaning: as for this one
notes: 이거 is the colloquial form of 이것 (this thing). The topic particle ~는 highlights 'this story' as the subject of her uncertainty.
--- word ---
term: 공식적으로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 공식적 + ~으로
meaning: officially
notes: The particle `~(으)로` is attached to the noun `공식적` (official) to form an adverb.
--- word ---
term: 풀어도
type: Verb
breakdown: 풀다 + ~아도
meaning: even if I tell
notes: Here, 풀다 (to untie, to solve) is used colloquially to mean 'to tell a story' or 'to reveal information'. The `~아/어도 되다` pattern expresses permission.
--- word ---
term: 되는지를
type: Grammatical Unit
breakdown: 되다 + ~는지 + ~를
meaning: whether it's okay
notes: This combines `되다` (to be okay) with the `~는지` grammar pattern, which creates an embedded question ('whether...'). The object particle `~를` marks this entire clause as the object of the verb `모르겠다` (I don't know).
--- word ---
term: 모르겠다
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 + ~겠~ + ~다
meaning: I don't know
notes: A common set phrase. Using `~겠~` here expresses a current thought or guess ('I guess I don't know'). It often sounds softer and more thoughtful than a blunt `몰라` (I don't know).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어도 되다
meaning: Expresses permission, meaning 'it is okay to...' or 'one may...'.
example: 여기 앉아도 돼요? (Is it okay if I sit here?)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는지 모르다
meaning: Used to express uncertainty about an action or state, meaning 'I don't know if/whether...'. It forms an indirect question.
example: 그 사람이 언제 오는지 몰라요. (I don't know when that person is coming.)
level: Intermediate

term: 이거는
type: Pronoun + Particle (Contracted)
breakdown: 이것 + 은
meaning: as for this
notes: 이거 is the colloquial form of 이것 (this thing). The topic particle ~는 sets 'this story' apart as the thing she will ask about.
--- word ---
term: 한번

breakdown: 한 + 번
meaning: once, (let's) try
notes: Literally 'one time', but often used idiomatically to mean 'to give something a try'. `한번 물어볼게` means 'I'll try asking'.

=== [21:48] 이거 말해도 괜찮나? ===
meaning: Is it okay if I talk about this?
tone: Informal, hesitant, self-questioning.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 이것
meaning: this thing
notes: A common contraction of '이것' (igeot), meaning 'this thing'. In spoken Korean, contractions like 이거 (this), 그거 (that), and 저거 (that over there) are used far more frequently than their full forms.
--- word ---
term: 말해도
type: Verb
breakdown: 말하다 + ~아/어도
meaning: even if I say
notes: The '~아/어도' ending means 'even if' or 'even though'. It's often paired with '괜찮다' (to be okay) or '되다' (to be permissible) to ask for or grant permission. For example, '먹어도 돼요?' means 'May I eat this?'.
--- word ---
term: 괜찮나?
type: Adjective + Ending
breakdown: 괜찮다 + ~나?
meaning: is it okay?
notes: The '~나?' ending is an informal, self-questioning suffix used in casual speech (반말). It conveys a sense of wondering or musing to oneself, rather than asking another person directly. For example, '이게 맞나?' (I wonder if this is right?).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어도 괜찮다/되다
meaning: Expresses permission or asks if an action is acceptable. 'It's okay to...' or 'May I...?'
example: 지금 전화해도 괜찮아요? (Is it okay to call now?)
level: Beginner

=== [21:50] 진짜 재밌었는데 그건 ===
meaning: That was really funny, though.
tone: Informal, reflective, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, truly
notes: A very common adverb used in casual speech to mean 'really'. It can also be used as a noun ('진짜야' - 'It's real') or an interjection ('진짜?' - 'Really?'). It's often used interchangeably with '정말' (jeongmal), though '진짜' can feel slightly more colloquial.
--- word ---
term: 재밌었는데
type: Adjective
breakdown: 재미있다 + ~었~ + ~는데
meaning: it was fun, but...
notes: The '~는데' ending is extremely versatile. Here, it provides background context ('it was fun...') and implies that more information or a contrasting thought is coming. It's also used to soften a statement or let a sentence trail off, which is common in conversation.
--- word ---
term: 그건
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 그것 + 은
meaning: that thing (as for that)
notes: A contraction of '그것은' (geugeos-eun). Placing the topic at the end of the sentence is a feature of spoken Korean, often done for emphasis or as an afterthought. The standard order would be '그건 진짜 재밌었는데'.

=== [21:54] 근데 좀 조심스러워 ===

term: 근데

breakdown: 그런데
meaning: but, however
notes: A common contraction of '그런데' (geureonde). While both mean 'but' or 'by the way', '근데' is much more frequent in casual conversation. It's used to introduce a contrasting idea or to change the subject.

breakdown: 조금

--- word ---
term: 조심스러워

notes: This is the informal, non-polite (해체, haeche) conjugation of '조심스럽다'. The '~스럽다' suffix attaches to nouns to create adjectives, meaning 'to be like' or 'to have the quality of' that noun. For example, '사랑스럽다' (to be lovely) from '사랑' (love).

=== [21:56] 근데 그거 무화과 잼과 베이컨이라는 메뉴더라고 ===

--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: but
notes: A common contraction of '그런데' (geureonde). In casual speech, '근데' is overwhelmingly preferred for introducing a contrasting point or changing the topic.
--- word ---
term: 그거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 그것
meaning: that thing
notes: The colloquial, contracted form of '그것' (geugeot), meaning 'that thing'. It's the standard way to refer to 'it' or 'that' in everyday conversation.
--- word ---
term: 무화과
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: fig
notes: Literally 'flower-less fruit' (無花果) because the flowers bloom inside the fruit and are not visible.
--- word ---
term: 잼과
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 잼 + ~과
meaning: jam and
notes: The particle '~과/와' means 'and' or 'with'. '~과' is used after a noun ending in a consonant (like '잼'), and '~와' is used after a vowel. This is more common in written or slightly more formal contexts than '~하고'.
--- word ---
term: 베이컨이라는
type: Noun + Grammar Pattern
breakdown: 베이컨 + ~(이)라는
meaning: called 'bacon'
notes: The pattern 'Noun + ~(이)라는' is used to introduce the name of something. It translates to 'called...' or 'named...'. For example, '화사라는 가수' means 'a singer called Hwasa'.
--- word ---
term: 메뉴더라고
type: Noun + Verb + Ending
breakdown: 메뉴 + 이다 + ~더라고
meaning: it was a menu item (I recall)
notes: The '~더라고' ending is used when recalling and reporting a past event that the speaker personally experienced or witnessed. It often carries a nuance of a new realization or mild surprise about that past fact. This is an informal (반말) ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~더라고(요)
meaning: A retrospective ending used to state a fact the speaker learned through direct experience in the past. It often implies a slight surprise or a new realization upon recollection.
example: 어제 그 식당에 가니까 사람이 많더라고요. (When I went to that restaurant yesterday, I saw that it was crowded.)
level: Intermediate

=== [22:05] 그래서 ===
meaning: So...
tone: Informal, transitional, leading to a conclusion.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: so, therefore
notes: A standard conjunction used to show a cause-and-effect relationship. It connects a reason (previous sentence) to a result (the following sentence). In speech, it can also be used as a filler word to transition to the next point.

=== [22:06] 피그와 피그가 있는 메뉴야 ===
meaning: It's a menu with 'pig' and 'fig'.
tone: Informal, playful, delivering a pun.

term: 피그와
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 피그 + ~와
meaning: pig and
notes: Hwasa is using the English word 'pig' (피그) to refer to the bacon. The particle '~와' means 'and' and is attached to nouns ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 피그가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 피그 + ~가
meaning: fig
notes: Here, Hwasa uses the English word 'fig' (피그), which sounds identical to how she pronounced 'pig'. This creates a pun. '~가' is the subject marker, indicating that 'fig' is also part of the menu.
--- word ---
term: 있는
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + ~는
meaning: that has / which is in it
notes: The '~는' ending turns an action verb into a modifier that can describe a noun in the present tense. '있는 메뉴' literally means 'a menu that has...'. For example, '책을 읽는 사람' (a person who is reading a book).
--- word ---
term: 메뉴야
type: Noun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 메뉴 + ~이야
meaning: it's a menu
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) form of '메뉴예요' (menyuyeyo). The copula '이다' (to be) becomes '~이야' after a noun ending in a vowel and '~이야' after a consonant in this casual style.

=== [22:13] 안 돼 안 돼 진짜 좀 ===
meaning: No, no, really, just...
tone: Informal, emphatic, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: A common adverb used to negate verbs and adjectives by placing it directly before them. It's typically used with shorter, native Korean verbs, while the `~지 않다` form is often used for longer, Sino-Korean verbs.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 + ~어
meaning: it's okay / it works
notes: `돼` is a contraction of `되어`. In this context, `안 돼` is a strong exclamation meaning "No way!", "Don't!", or "It's not allowed." A common mistake for learners is confusing `되` (verb stem) and `돼` (conjugated form). A simple tip is that `돼` can end a sentence, but `되` cannot.

term: 안

meaning: it's okay / it works
