meaning: camera
notes: A loanword from English. The sentence is incomplete, implying she pointed and said 'this, camera' to indicate the problem.

=== [30:40] 그러면은 ===
meaning: If I do that, then...
tone: Casual, transitional, leading to a result.
--- word ---
term: 그러면은
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~(으)면 + ~은/는
meaning: if so, then, in that case
notes: A combination of 그러면 (if so) and the topic particle ~은/는 for emphasis. In speech, it's often shortened to 그럼 (geureom). It connects a condition to its result.

=== [30:42] 못 알아들으셔 ===
meaning: They couldn't understand.
tone: Casual, reporting a result, respectful.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: Used before an action verb to express inability. It's different from 안, which expresses unwillingness. '못 해요' means 'I can't do it,' while '안 해요' means 'I don't do it'.
--- word ---
term: 알아들으셔
type: Verb
breakdown: 알아듣다 + ~(으)시 + ~어
meaning: understand (honorific)
notes: 알아듣다 means 'to understand what is said'. The verb includes the honorific suffix ~(으)시, which shows respect to the person being talked about (the subject). Even though Hwasa is speaking informally (반말) to her fans, she respectfully refers to the stranger. The final ~어 is the informal ending.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~(으)시
meaning: The honorific suffix. It is inserted into a verb or adjective stem to show respect for the subject of the sentence. It's a key part of the Korean honorifics system.
example: 선생님께서 오십니다. (The teacher is coming.)
level: Beginner

=== [30:44] 계속 그렇게 ===
meaning: (They) just kept on like that...
tone: Casual, descriptive, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: continuously, keep
notes: An adverb indicating that an action or state continues without stopping. It can be used with a wide range of verbs.
--- word ---
term: 그렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~게
meaning: like that
notes: Refers to the manner of an action previously mentioned or understood from context. Here, it means 'they continued filming themselves like that'. The sentence is left unfinished, letting the listener infer the rest of the situation.

=== [30:45] 계속 나를 막 찍어 ===
meaning: They just keep taking pictures of me.
tone: Informal, narrative, amused. Describing a fan's flustered reaction.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: continuously, keep
notes: Used to indicate that an action is ongoing or repeated without stopping. It's placed before the verb it modifies. For example, '계속 공부했어요' means 'I kept studying.'
--- word ---
term: 나를
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -를
meaning: me (as the object)
notes: 나 is the informal pronoun for 'I/me'. -를 is the object marking particle used after a vowel. In casual speech, this is often contracted to 날 (nal), as in '날 봐' (look at me).
--- word ---
term: 막
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, recklessly, without thinking
notes: A very common colloquial adverb that adds a nuance of doing something carelessly, intensely, or without a specific plan. It's often difficult to translate directly but implies a certain spontaneity or lack of restraint. Here, it emphasizes the fan's frantic picture-taking.
--- word ---
term: 찍어
type: Verb
breakdown: 찍다 + -어
meaning: takes a picture
notes: The informal, non-polite present tense conjugation of 찍다 (to take a photo/film). This form, called 해체 (haeche), is used with close friends, younger people, or in this case, by an idol speaking casually to her fans.

=== [30:47] 당신이 찍히고 있는데 ===
meaning: When you're the one being filmed...
tone: Informal, narrative, pointing out irony.
--- word ---
term: 당신이
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 당신 + -이
meaning: you (are)
notes: 당신 is the formal 'you', but its usage is tricky. It can sound confrontational or overly dramatic between strangers. Here, Hwasa uses it in a narrative context to refer to a generic 'you' (the fan in the story), not directly addressing the listener. In most daily conversations, Koreans avoid using a direct 'you' and instead use the person's name or title.
--- word ---
term: 찍히고
type: Verb
breakdown: 찍히다 + -고
meaning: being filmed and...
notes: 찍히다 is the passive form of 찍다 (to film), meaning 'to be filmed'. The -고 ending is a connective that links clauses, often translated as 'and' or used to list actions in sequence.
--- word ---
term: 있는데
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 있다 + -는데
meaning: are... but/and
notes: The pattern V-고 있다 creates the present progressive tense ('is V-ing'). The -는데 ending provides background information for the following clause, often creating a soft contrast. The full phrase 찍히고 있는데 means 'is being filmed, and/but...' setting up a situation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-히- (Passive Voice)
meaning: A common way to form the passive voice, indicating the subject is acted upon. It's added to the verb stem. For example, 찍다 (to take a picture) becomes 찍히다 (to be photographed), and 보다 (to see) becomes 보이다 (to be seen).
example: 산이 보여요. (The mountain can be seen.)
level: Intermediate

=== [30:49] 어떡하지? ===
meaning: What should I do?
tone: Informal, self-talk, rhetorical. Expressing her thought at that moment.
--- word ---
term: 어떡하지?
type: Expression
breakdown: 어떻게 하지?
meaning: What should I do?
notes: A very common contraction of 어떻게 하지? (eotteoke haji?). It's used frequently in self-talk or when asking for advice in an informal situation, expressing worry, or being in a bind. It's a key phrase for sounding natural in casual conversation.

=== [30:50] 그러다가 이제 시간이 없으니까 난 지나치는데 ===
meaning: So while that's happening, since there's no time, I just pass by, but...
tone: Informal, narrative, explanatory. The sentence trails off.
--- word ---
term: 그러다가
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + -다가
meaning: while doing that, then
notes: The grammar pattern V-다가 indicates that an action is interrupted by another action. Here, 그러다가 (from 그렇다, 'to be so') means 'while that was happening' or 'in the middle of that'.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, then
notes: 이제 means 'now', but in narrative contexts like this, it often functions as a filler word to transition to the next point, similar to 'so then' or 'well'.
--- word ---
term: 시간이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 시간 + -이
meaning: time (as the subject)
notes: 시간 means 'time'. The particle -이 marks it as the subject of the clause '시간이 없으니까' (because there is no time).
--- word ---
term: 없으니까
type: Adjective
breakdown: 없다 + -(으)니까
meaning: because there isn't
notes: -(으)니까 is a grammar pattern that means 'because' or 'since'. It often states a reason that the listener is likely already aware of and is commonly used when giving a command or suggestion based on that reason.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -는
meaning: I (as for me)
notes: A common contraction of 나는. 나 is the informal 'I', and -는 is the topic marking particle. Using the topic marker here contrasts herself ('as for me, I pass by') with the fan's actions.
--- word ---
term: 지나치는데
type: Verb
breakdown: 지나치다 + -는데
meaning: pass by, but...
notes: 지나치다 means 'to pass by' or 'to go too far'. The -는데 ending provides background context and often implies a contrast or a following thought that is left unsaid. The sentence trails off, which is common in spoken Korean.

=== [30:53] 그러니까 한 분은 그래서 이제 하다가 ===
meaning: So, one person, because of that, was trying to do that and then...
tone: Informal, narrative, slightly hesitant. Natural unscripted speech.
--- word ---
term: 그러니까
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: so, therefore
notes: Used to connect a cause/reason to a result. It can also be used as a filler word to mean 'I mean...' or 'so...' while gathering one's thoughts, which is how it's used here.
--- word ---
term: 한
type: Determiner
breakdown: 하나
meaning: one
notes: When counting nouns, the native Korean number 하나 (one) changes to 한 before the noun. For example, 'one person' is 한 사람 (han saram).
--- word ---
term: 분은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 분 + -은
meaning: person (as for one person)
notes: 분 is the honorific counter for people, more polite than 명 (myeong) or 사람 (saram). Even though Hwasa is speaking informally (banmal), she uses a polite noun for her fan, showing respect.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: so, for that reason
notes: Indicates a direct cause-and-effect relationship. It's similar to 그러니까 but often feels more direct. The repetition of '그러니까', '그래서', and '이제' is very typical of spontaneous speech.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, then
notes: Again used here as a conversational filler to mark a transition in the story.
--- word ---
term: 하다가
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -다가
meaning: while doing...
notes: V-다가 indicates an interruption of an action. Here, '하다가' is used vaguely to mean 'while trying to do that (take a picture)...' before the sentence trails off.

=== [30:57] 그래서 이제 자기 핸드폰을 원망하는 팬도 봤어 ===

meaning: so, for that reason
notes: Connects the previous situation (the fan fumbling) to the result (blaming the phone).
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: then
notes: Functions as a filler word here, marking the progression of the story.
--- word ---
term: 자기
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: one's own
notes: A reflexive pronoun used to refer back to the subject of the sentence. Here, it means 'their (the fan's) own phone'. It's a neutral term that can mean 'my own', 'your own', 'his/her own' depending on the context.
--- word ---
term: 핸드폰을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 핸드폰 + -을
meaning: cell phone (as the object)
notes: 핸드폰 (haendeupon) is a direct Konglish borrowing of 'hand phone'. The particle -을 marks it as the object of the verb 원망하는 (blaming).

term: 원망하는
type: Verb
breakdown: 원망하다 + -는
meaning: blaming
notes: The -는 ending turns the verb 원망하다 (to blame, to resent) into a descriptive adjective that modifies the following noun (팬, fan). This structure, 'V-는 Noun', means 'the Noun that is V-ing'.
--- word ---
term: 팬도

meaning: I saw
notes: The informal, non-polite past tense of 보다 (to see). The -았/었- infix marks the past tense, and the -어 ending marks the informal speech level.

=== [31:01] 욕도 하더라고 ===
meaning: They were even swearing.
tone: Informal, anecdotal, amused surprise.
--- word ---
term: 욕도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 욕 + -도
meaning: swearing, too/even
notes: 욕 means 'a curse word' or 'profanity'. 욕을 하다 means 'to swear'. The particle -도 ('also/even') emphasizes her surprise that the fan's frustration went as far as swearing at their phone.
--- word ---
term: 하더라고
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 하다 + -더라고
meaning: they were doing (I recall)
notes: The -더라고 ending is used to state a fact that the speaker directly saw or experienced in the past. It conveys a sense of recalling or reporting a personal observation. It's a very common ending in conversational storytelling.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -더라고(요)
meaning: Used to express something the speaker personally saw, heard, or felt in the past. It's like saying 'I recall that...' or 'I saw that...'. It adds a nuance of personal experience to a past-tense statement.
example: 어제 그 식당에 가니까 사람이 많더라고요. (When I went to that restaurant yesterday, I saw that it was crowded.)
level: Intermediate

=== [31:27] 그것도 그래 ===
meaning: That's true, too.
tone: Informal, agreeable, conversational.
--- word ---
term: 그것도
type: Pronoun + particle
breakdown: 그것 (that thing) + 도 (also/too)
meaning: that too
notes: 그것 is the standard form for 'that thing.' In casual speech, it's often contracted to 그거. The particle 도 means 'also' or 'too,' and it replaces the subject/object particles (이/가, 을/를). For example, '나도 가' (I'm going too), not '내가도 가.'
--- word ---
term: 그래
type: Verb/Interjection
breakdown: 그렇다 (to be so, to be like that) conjugated to the informal, non-past tense.
meaning: that's right / yeah
notes: 그래 is a very common affirmative response in casual Korean, similar to 'yeah,' 'that's right,' or 'okay.' It's the informal, non-polite (반말) form of 그래요. It comes from the descriptive verb 그렇다.

=== [31:28] 너무 행복해서 까먹을 때도 있어 ===
meaning: Sometimes I'm so happy that I forget.
tone: Informal, reflective, personal.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very
notes: Traditionally, 너무 meant 'too (much)' with a negative connotation. However, in modern colloquial Korean, it's widely used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative contexts, like '너무 좋아' (I like it so much).
--- word ---
term: 행복해서
type: Verb
breakdown: 행복하다 (to be happy) + ~아/어서 (so, because)
meaning: because I'm happy
notes: The ~아/어서 ending connects two clauses, indicating a cause-and-effect relationship. The first clause (being happy) is the reason for the second (forgetting). It cannot be used with imperative or propositive endings like ~(으)세요 or ~ㅂ시다.
--- word ---
term: 까먹을
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 까먹다 (to forget) + ~을 (future/potential modifier ending)
meaning: (that I) forget
notes: 까먹다 is a colloquial and more casual equivalent of 잊다 (to forget). The ~(으)ㄹ ending turns the verb into a modifier that describes the following noun (때), indicating a future or potential action.
--- word ---
term: 때도
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 때 (time, moment) + 도 (also, even)
meaning: times too / sometimes
notes: The phrase ~(으)ㄹ 때도 있다/없다 means 'there are times when... / there are no times when...'. It's a common way to express 'sometimes' or 'never'.
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 (to exist, to have)
meaning: there is
notes: This is the informal, non-polite (반말) form of 있어요. It's used among close friends, family, or to people younger than the speaker. Hwasa uses 반말 throughout this live stream with her fans.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Expresses 'the time when...' or 'when...' something happens.
example: 공부할 때 음악을 들어요. (I listen to music when I study.)
level: Beginner

=== [31:31] 그걸 폰으로 담아야 또 기억이 나고 ===
meaning: I have to capture it on my phone to remember it again, and...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 그걸
type: Pronoun + particle (contracted)
breakdown: 그것 (that thing) + 을 (object particle)
meaning: that
notes: 그걸 is a common contraction of 그것을. In spoken Korean, contractions like this (이것을 -> 이걸, 저것을 -> 저걸) are used very frequently to make speech flow more smoothly.
--- word ---
term: 폰으로
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 폰 (phone) + 으로 (with/by means of)
meaning: with a phone
notes: 폰 is a shortened form of 핸드폰 (hand phone), the Korean word for 'cell phone'. The particle ~(으)로 indicates the tool, method, or means by which an action is performed.
--- word ---
term: 담아야
type: Verb
breakdown: 담다 (to put in, to capture) + ~아야 하다 (must, have to)
meaning: must capture
notes: This is from the V + ~아야/어야 하다/되다 pattern, meaning 'must' or 'have to.' The final verb (하다/되다) is often omitted in casual conversation when the context is clear, leaving just ~아야/어야.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: again
notes: 또 means 'again' or 'also.' It can indicate repetition or add another item to a list. It's similar to 다시 (again), but 또 often has a nuance of 'in addition' or 'what's more.'
--- word ---
term: 기억이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 기억 (memory) + 이 (subject particle)
meaning: memory
notes: The phrase '기억이 나다' is a set expression meaning 'to remember' or 'for a memory to come up.' 기억 is the noun 'memory,' and 나다 means 'to emerge' or 'to occur.'
--- word ---
term: 나고
type: Verb
breakdown: 나다 (to emerge, to occur) + ~고 (and)
meaning: comes up, and...
notes: The ~고 particle connects two clauses or actions, often in sequence. Here, the sentence trails off, which is a very common feature of natural, spoken Korean, suggesting the thought is continuing.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 기억이 나다
meaning: A set phrase meaning 'to remember' or 'to recall.' It literally means 'a memory comes out.' The opposite, 'to not remember,' is '기억이 안 나다.'
example: 그 사람 이름이 기억이 안 나요. (I can't remember that person's name.)
level: Beginner

=== [31:34] 또 이렇게 보면서 돌려보면서 아리도 하고 ===

--- word ---
term: 또

breakdown: 
meaning: again
notes: Used here to indicate another action in the sequence of what she does with the photos/videos on her phone.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게

meaning: like this
notes: A contraction of 이리하게. It's used to describe how an action is performed. The three related adverbs are 이렇게 (like this), 그렇게 (like that), and 저렇게 (like that over there).
--- word ---
term: 보면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 (to see, to look) + ~(으)면서 (while doing)
meaning: while looking
notes: The ~(으)면서 grammar pattern is attached to a verb stem to indicate that two actions are happening at the same time. The subject of both verbs must be the same.
--- word ---
term: 돌려보면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 돌려보다 (to look back, to rewatch) + ~(으)면서 (while doing)
meaning: while rewatching
notes: 돌려보다 is a compound verb from 돌리다 (to turn) and 보다 (to see). It means to look back on something, like rewatching a video or looking through old photos.
--- word ---
term: 아리도
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 아리 (?) + 도 (also)
meaning: also reminisce
notes: 아리 is not a standard Korean word in this context. It might be a typo in the transcript for 아련 (wistful feeling) or a personal slang term. Given the context of looking at old photos, it implies a sentimental or wistful action.
--- word ---
term: 하고
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + ~고 (and)
meaning: and do
notes: The sentence trails off with ~고, indicating that the list of actions is not finished or that she's moving on to the next thought. This is very common in conversational Korean.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)면서
meaning: Indicates two actions performed by the same subject simultaneously. 'while doing X, do Y.'

level: Intermediate

=== [31:46] 항상 잘 관찰하고 있었지 ===
meaning: You were always observing me well, weren't you?
tone: Informal, affectionate, speaking to fans.
--- word ---
term: 항상
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: always
notes: 항상 is a common adverb for 'always.' Another similar word is 언제나, which also means 'always.' They are often interchangeable.
--- word ---
term: 잘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: well
notes: A very common adverb used to modify verbs. It can mean 'well,' 'skillfully,' or 'thoroughly.' For example, '잘 자' (sleep well) or '한국말 잘해요' (you speak Korean well).
--- word ---
term: 관찰하고
type: Verb
breakdown: 관찰하다 (to observe) + ~고 있다 (present progressive)
meaning: observing
notes: 관찰하다 is a Sino-Korean verb (觀察) meaning 'to observe.' It's often used in a more formal or scientific context than simply 보다 (to see/watch), but here it's used playfully to describe how closely fans watch her.
--- word ---
term: 있었지
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 있다 (auxiliary verb) + ~었~ (past tense) + ~지 (confirmation ending)
meaning: you were, weren't you?
notes: The ~지 ending is used to confirm something the speaker believes the listener also knows or agrees with. It's like adding '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' to a sentence. It creates a sense of shared understanding.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~고 있었다
meaning: The past progressive tense, equivalent to 'was/were -ing' in English. It describes an action that was ongoing in the past.
example: 내가 전화했을 때 자고 있었어. (You were sleeping when I called.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~지(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to seek agreement or confirm information that both the speaker and listener are aware of. The informal version is ~지.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (The weather's nice today, isn't it?)
level: Beginner

=== [31:50] 얼마나 웃긴지 몰라 ===
meaning: You have no idea how funny it is.
tone: Informal, amused, emphatic.
--- word ---
term: 얼마나
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: how (much/many)
notes: While often used in questions ('얼마나 걸려요?' - How long does it take?), it is also used in the pattern '얼마나 A/V~(으)ㄴ/는지 모르다' to make an emphatic statement.
--- word ---
term: 웃긴지
type: Verb
breakdown: 웃기다 (to be funny) + ~ㄴ지 (indirect question ending)
meaning: how funny it is
notes: 웃기다 is the causative form of 웃다 (to laugh), meaning 'to make someone laugh' or 'to be funny.' The ~(으)ㄴ/는지 ending is used to embed a question or an unknown piece of information within a larger sentence.
--- word ---
term: 몰라
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 (to not know)
meaning: (you) don't know
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말) form of 몰라요. The entire phrase '얼마나 웃긴지 몰라' is an idiom meaning 'It's so funny!'
--- grammar ---
pattern: 얼마나 + A/V + ~(으)ㄴ/는지 모르다
meaning: An idiomatic expression used to emphasize the degree of the adjective or verb. It literally translates to 'You don't know how...', but the actual meaning is 'It is SO...'
example: 그 영화가 얼마나 슬픈지 몰라요. (You have no idea how sad that movie is. / That movie is so sad.)
level: Intermediate

=== [31:53] 오디세이처럼 내 연인 올 배우자가 ===
meaning: Like in 'Odyssey', if my lover or spouse...
tone: Hypothetical, trailing off to set up a scenario.
--- word ---
term: 오디세이처럼
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 오디세이 (Odyssey) + -처럼
meaning: like 'Odyssey'
notes: -처럼 is a particle that means 'like' or 'as if'. It attaches directly to nouns to indicate similarity. Example: 천사처럼 (like an angel).
--- word ---
term: 내
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 나의 (contraction)
meaning: my
notes: 내 is the informal contraction of 나의 (my). In casual speech, 내 is almost always used instead of the full form. The formal equivalent is 저의, which contracts to 제.
--- word ---
term: 연인
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: lover / romantic partner
notes: This term refers to a romantic partner in a serious relationship. It's more formal and literary than the common term 남자 친구 (boyfriend) or 여자 친구 (girlfriend).
--- word ---
term: 올
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: or
notes: This is the English word 'or' pronounced with a Korean accent. In casual speech, it's sometimes used instead of the Korean words 또는 or 혹은. This is an example of code-switching.
--- word ---
term: 배우자가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 배우자 + -가
meaning: spouse
notes: 배우자 means 'spouse' and is a gender-neutral term. -가 is the subject particle, used here because the noun ends in a vowel. The sentence is incomplete, leaving the subject hanging.

=== [31:59] 7년 동안 다른 사람이랑 살았다 생각하면 어때? ===
meaning: What do you think if they lived with someone else for 7 years?
tone: Posing a direct, hypothetical question to the audience.
--- word ---
term: 7년
type: Noun
breakdown: 칠 (7) + 년 (year)
meaning: 7 years
notes: Korean uses two number systems. For counting years, Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼...) are used with the counter 년 (nyeon). For example, 10 years is 십 년 (simnyeon).
--- word ---
term: 동안
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: during, for (a period of time)
notes: 동안 is a noun that indicates a duration of time. It follows a time-related word. Example: 방학 동안 (during vacation), 3일 동안 (for 3 days).
--- word ---
term: 다른
type: Adjective
breakdown: 다르다 + -ㄴ
meaning: different / other
notes: This is the adjective form of the verb 다르다 (to be different). The -ㄴ ending modifies the following noun, 사람 (person).
--- word ---
term: 사람이랑
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사람 + -이랑
meaning: with another person
notes: -(이)랑 is a colloquial particle meaning 'with' or 'and', used in casual speech instead of the more standard -와/과 or -하고. Use -이랑 after a consonant and -랑 after a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 살았다
type: Verb
breakdown: 살다 + -았- + -다
meaning: lived
notes: This is the past tense plain form of 살다 (to live). The verb stem ends in ㄹ, which is dropped before adding certain endings like -았/었-.
--- word ---
term: 생각하면
type: Verb
breakdown: 생각하다 + -(으)면
meaning: if you think
notes: The -(으)면 ending means 'if' or 'when'. It attaches to verb and adjective stems. -으면 is used after a consonant, and -면 after a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 어때?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 어떻다 + -어(요)?
meaning: how is it? / what do you think?
notes: 어때? is the informal, non-polite question form of 어떻다 (to be how). It's a very common way to ask for an opinion. The polite form is 어때요?
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-았/었다(고) 생각하면
meaning: If you think that (something happened)...
example: 그가 떠났다고 생각하면 슬퍼요. (It's sad if I think that he left.)
level: Intermediate

=== [32:02] 왜 7년 동안 뭐 하느라고? ===
meaning: Why, what were they doing for 7 years?
tone: Inquisitive, asking for the reason within the story.
--- word ---
term: 왜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: why
notes: The most common interrogative adverb for asking for a reason. It can be used alone as a response ('Why?') or at the beginning of a question.
--- word ---
term: 7년
type: Noun
breakdown: 칠 (7) + 년 (year)
meaning: 7 years
notes: Sino-Korean numbers are used with 년 (year) to count durations. For example, 일 년 (one year), 이 년 (two years).
--- word ---
term: 동안
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: during, for
notes: A noun indicating a period of time. It always follows a noun or phrase specifying the duration, like 7년 동안 (for 7 years).
--- word ---
term: 뭐
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 무엇 (contraction)
meaning: what
notes: 뭐 is the common spoken contraction of 무엇 (what). It's used ubiquitously in informal and standard speech.
--- word ---
term: 하느라고?
type: Verb + Ending
breakdown: 하다 + -느라고
meaning: because of doing what?
notes: The grammar pattern -느라고 gives the reason for a negative outcome or inability to do something else. Here, it's used in a question to ask 'what were you so busy doing that...?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: -느라고
meaning: Indicates that the action in the first clause is the reason for a negative result or inability to do something else in the second clause (which is implied here).
example: 숙제하느라고 전화를 못 받았어요. (I couldn't answer the phone because I was doing homework.)
level: Intermediate

=== [32:07] 오디세이에서는 ===
meaning: In 'Odyssey'...
tone: Explanatory, providing context from the story.
--- word ---
term: 오디세이에서는
type: Noun + Particles
breakdown: 오디세이 + -에서 + -는
meaning: in 'Odyssey' (as for in 'Odyssey')
notes: The particle -에서 indicates a location or context. Adding the topic particle -는 emphasizes or contrasts this context. It implies 'As for in this story (unlike other situations)...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -에서는
meaning: Combines the location/context particle -에서 with the topic/contrast particle -는. It specifies a context and sets it as the topic, often for comparison.
example: 한국에서는 김치를 매일 먹어요. (In Korea, people eat kimchi every day.)
level: Beginner

=== [32:13] 집에 이제 잊어버려서 못 와서 ===
meaning: Because he forgot and couldn't come home...
tone: Explanatory, trailing off, continuing the plot summary.
--- word ---
term: 집에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 집 + -에
meaning: to home
notes: The particle -에 indicates a destination or location. When used with verbs of motion like 가다 (to go) or 오다 (to come), it means 'to'.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 means 'now'. It can refer to the present moment or a point in time from which a change occurs. In conversation, it's often used as a filler word to transition to a new point, much like 'well' or 'so' in English.
--- word ---
term: 잊어버려서
type: Verb
breakdown: 잊어버리다 + -어서
meaning: because he completely forgot
notes: 잊어버리다 is a compound verb from 잊다 (to forget) and the auxiliary verb 버리다. V-아/어 버리다 implies the action is completed thoroughly, often with a sense of regret or relief. The -어서 ending indicates the reason for what follows.
--- word ---
term: 못
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: 못 is an adverb placed before a verb to express inability to do something. It's different from 안, which expresses a lack of will or simple negation. 못 하다 (cannot do) vs. 안 하다 (do not do).
--- word ---
term: 와서
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + -아서
meaning: because he came / and he came
notes: The connective ending -아/어서 can mean 'because' (causal) or 'and then' (sequential). Here, it's part of a causal chain: '잊어버려서 못 와서...' (Because he forgot, he couldn't come, and so...).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 버리다
meaning: An auxiliary verb construction that adds nuance to the main verb. It can mean the action was completed fully, happened accidentally, or is regrettable.
example: 케이크를 다 먹어 버렸어요. (I ate up all the cake.)
level: Intermediate

type: Verb
breakdown: 걸리다 + -ㄴ
meaning: that (he) got
notes: This is the past tense adjectival form of 걸리다 (to get/catch an illness). It modifies the following noun, 거 (thing/fact).

meaning: A common informal question pattern used to ask for confirmation or express surprise about a past event or current state. It literally means 'Is it the case that...?'
example: 너 어제 집에 안 들어온 거야? (Is it true you didn't come home yesterday?)

=== [32:22] 난 용서할 수 있을 것 같은데 ===
meaning: I think I could forgive him.
tone: Contemplative, expressing a personal opinion softly.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -는
meaning: I (as for me)
notes: This is the informal contraction of 나는. The topic particle -는 here can imply a slight contrast: 'As for me (others might feel differently), I think...'
--- word ---
term: 용서할
type: Verb
breakdown: 용서하다 + -ㄹ
meaning: that can forgive
notes: This is the future adjectival form of 용서하다 (to forgive). It modifies the following noun 수 (ability).
--- word ---
term: 수
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: ability / way
notes: A bound noun used in the pattern `-(으)ㄹ 수 있다/없다` to express ability ('can') or inability ('cannot').
--- word ---
term: 있을
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + -을
meaning: that exists / that is possible
notes: The future adjectival form of 있다 (to exist). Here it's part of the `-(으)ㄹ 것 같다` pattern.
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: thing / fact
notes: A bound noun used in many grammatical patterns. Here, it's part of `-(으)ㄹ 것 같다`, meaning 'it seems like' or 'I think'.
--- word ---
term: 같은데
type: Adjective + Ending
breakdown: 같다 + -은데
meaning: it seems like, but...
notes: The ending -(으)ㄴ데 is used to provide background information or to soften a statement, making it less direct. It often carries a nuance of '...but' or '...and so'.

meaning: Expresses ability or possibility; 'can' or 'to be able to'.
example: 한국어를 할 수 있어요. (I can speak Korean.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄹ 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess, assumption, or opinion; 'I think...' or 'it seems like...'. It's a softer way to state an opinion than a direct statement.
example: 비가 올 것 같아요. (I think it's going to rain.)

=== [32:27] 연꽃 먹어서 기억 잃었어? ===

tone: Casual, rhetorical question, referencing a story.
--- word ---
term: 연꽃
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: lotus flower
notes: Refers to the Lotus-Eaters in the Odyssey, who forget their homes after eating the lotus fruit. Hwasa is using this as a metaphor for forgetting a past relationship.
--- word ---
term: 먹어서
type: Verb
breakdown: 먹다 + -어서
meaning: ate, so / ate and
notes: The -어서/아서 ending connects two clauses. It can indicate a reason ('because I ate...') or a sequence of events ('I ate and then...'). Here, it implies cause and effect: eating the lotus caused the memory loss.
--- word ---
term: 기억
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: memory
notes: Often used with 하다 (to do) to form the verb 기억하다 (to remember) or with 잃다 (to lose) to form 기억을 잃다 (to lose one's memory).
--- word ---
term: 잃었어?
type: Verb
breakdown: 잃다 + -었어?
meaning: did you lose?
notes: This is the past tense informal question form (반말, banmal). The base verb is 잃다 (to lose). The -었- indicates past tense, and the rising intonation (represented by '?') makes it a question.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-어서/아서
meaning: Indicates reason or sequential order between two clauses. 'Because V' or 'V and then...'
example: 배고파서 밥을 먹었어요. (I was hungry, so I ate.)
level: Beginner

meaning: with me (in contrast)

meaning: Used to provide background context before the main clause, or to end a sentence with a soft, expectant, or slightly contrasting nuance.

meaning: one year

=== [32:51] 어떻게 해야 돼? ===
meaning: What should I do?
tone: Casual, rhetorical question, asking for opinions.
--- word ---
term: 어떻게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: how
notes: A fundamental question word. Do not confuse with 어때? (How is it? / What do you think?). 어떻게 asks about the method or way, while 어때 asks for an opinion or description.
--- word ---
term: 해야
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -어야
meaning: must do
notes: This is part of the V-어야/아야 하다/되다 grammar pattern, which expresses obligation or necessity. Here, it's the first part of the pattern.
--- word ---
term: 돼?
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다
meaning: should I?
notes: The second part of the V-어야/아야 되다 pattern. 되다 means 'to become' or 'to be okay'. In this construction, it completes the meaning of 'have to' or 'should'. This is the informal question form.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-어야/아야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, equivalent to 'must' or 'have to' in English.
example: 내일 일찍 일어나야 돼요. (I have to wake up early tomorrow.)
level: Beginner

term: 좀

--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V-(으)ㄴ/는데?

=== [33:03] 기억을 되찾자마자 ===
meaning: ...as soon as he regained his memory.

--- grammar ---
pattern: V-자마자
meaning: Indicates that one action happens immediately after another. It translates to 'as soon as' or 'right after'.
example: 집에 도착하자마자 잠들었어요. (I fell asleep as soon as I got home.)

=== [33:06] 울면서 나를 바로 찾으러 왔어 ===
meaning: ...he came looking for me right away, crying.
tone: Informal, descriptive, narrative.
--- word ---
term: 울면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 울다 (to cry) + -면서
meaning: while crying
notes: The -(으)면서 grammar pattern attaches to a verb stem to show that two actions are being performed by the same subject simultaneously. Since 울다 ends in a consonant (ㄹ), the ㄹ is dropped before adding -면서.
--- word ---
term: 나를
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 (I/me) + -를
meaning: me (as the object)
notes: 나 is the informal pronoun for 'I/me'. -를 is the object particle used after a word ending in a vowel.
--- word ---
term: 바로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: right away, immediately
notes: This adverb emphasizes the immediacy of the action. It can also mean 'straight' or 'directly' in other contexts (e.g., 바로 가세요 - Please go straight).
--- word ---
term: 찾으러
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 찾다 (to look for) + -으러
meaning: in order to find
notes: The V-(으)러 오다/가다 pattern expresses the purpose of movement. One comes or goes 'in order to do' the verb. -으러 is used after a consonant stem.
--- word ---
term: 왔어
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 (to come) + -았어
meaning: (he) came
notes: This is the informal, non-polite past tense form (반말). The standard polite form would be 왔어요 (wasseoyo). Hwasa uses 반말 throughout this casual live stream with her fans.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)면서
meaning: Used to express two actions happening at the same time by the same subject. Translates to 'while V-ing'.
example: 밥을 먹으면서 TV를 봐요. (I watch TV while eating.)

meaning: ...it is, you know.

meaning: Expresses possibility or a potential situation. It's softer than a direct statement and translates to 'might V' or 'could V'.

--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-잖아(요)
meaning: Used to confirm information or state a reason that the speaker believes is obvious or already known to the listener. It adds a nuance of 'As you know...' or 'It's because...'.
example: 어제 말했잖아. (I told you yesterday, remember?)

meaning: disappeared?

=== [34:08] 머리 아파 ===
meaning: My head hurts. / This is a headache.

breakdown: 
meaning: head
notes: The basic noun for 'head.' It can also refer to 'hair' depending on the context (e.g., 머리를 자르다 - to cut one's hair).
--- word ---
term: 아파
type: Verb
breakdown: 아프다 + ~아
meaning: it hurts
notes: The informal, non-polite present tense conjugation of 아프다 (to be sick, to hurt). The phrase 머리 아파 is an idiom that can mean a literal headache or that a situation is mentally taxing and complicated.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 머리가 아프다
meaning: An idiomatic expression that can mean either a literal headache ('my head hurts') or that a situation is stressful, complex, or difficult to think about ('this is a headache'). The subject particle '가' is often omitted in casual speech.
example: 이 수학 문제는 너무 어려워서 머리 아파. (This math problem is so hard it's giving me a headache.)
level: Beginner

=== [34:16] 2026년인데 폰이 안 돼 ===
meaning: It's 2026, but the phone isn't working.
tone: Informal, complaining, hypothetical.
--- word ---
term: 2026년인데
type: Noun + verb ending
breakdown: 2026년 (year 2026) + 이다 (to be) + -ㄴ데 (but/and)
meaning: it's the year 2026, but...
notes: The ending -ㄴ/는데 provides background information for the next clause. It can show contrast ('but'), reason ('so'), or simply set the scene ('and'). It's extremely common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 폰이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 폰 (phone) + -이 (subject particle)
meaning: the phone
notes: 폰 is a common loanword from English 'phone'. The subject particle -이 is used because the noun ends in a consonant. If it ended in a vowel, -가 would be used (e.g., '카메라가' - the camera).
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: 안 is a short-form negator placed directly before a verb or adjective. It is very common in casual speech. The longer, more formal way to negate is by attaching -지 않다 to the verb stem (e.g., 되지 않다).
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다 (to work, to become) -> 되어 -> 돼
meaning: works
notes: 돼 is a contraction of 되어, the informal present tense form of 되다. In this context, '안 되다' is a common phrase meaning 'it doesn't work' or 'it's not allowed'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -(이)ㄴ데
meaning: It is [Noun], but/and...; used to provide background context for the following clause.
example: 학생인데 돈이 없어요. (I'm a student, so I don't have money.)
level: Beginner

=== [34:21] 우리 폰 없이 어떻게 살았어 그때? ===

--- word ---
term: 우리
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: we/our
notes: In Korean, 우리 can mean 'we' or 'our'. It is also often used to mean 'my' when referring to things that are part of a collective, like '우리 집' (my house) or '우리 엄마' (my mom). Here, it refers to 'we' as a generation.
--- word ---
term: 폰
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: phone
notes: A common loanword from English 'phone'.
--- word ---
term: 없이
type: Adverb
breakdown: 없다 (to not have) + -이 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: without
notes: The suffix -이 can be attached to some adjectives to turn them into adverbs. 없다 (to not exist) becomes 없이 (without).
--- word ---
term: 어떻게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 어떻다 (to be how) + -게 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: how
notes: A fundamental question word. It's formed by adding the adverbial suffix -게 to the descriptive verb 어떻다 (to be how).
--- word ---
term: 살았어

breakdown: 살다 (to live) + -았어 (past tense, informal)
meaning: lived
notes: This is the informal, non-polite past tense conjugation (반말/해체). The -았- infix indicates past tense, and the -어 ending is the informal non-polite form.
--- word ---
term: 그때?
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: back then?
notes: 그 means 'that' and 때 means 'time'. It refers to a specific time in the past that is understood by both the speaker and listener.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 없이
meaning: Indicates the absence of something, meaning 'without [Noun]'.
example: 설탕 없이 커피를 마셔요. (I drink coffee without sugar.)
level: Beginner

=== [34:34] 전쟁 때문에 나간 거야 ===

--- word ---
term: 때문에
type: Postpositional phrase
breakdown: 
meaning: because of
notes: Used after a noun to indicate the reason for something, often for a result with a neutral or negative nuance. For positive reasons, '덕분에' (thanks to) is often used.
--- word ---

breakdown: 나가다 (to go out, to leave) + -ㄴ (past tense modifier)
meaning: (who) left

--- word ---
term: 거야

breakdown: 것 (thing) + 이다 (to be) -> 것이야 -> 거야

notes: The ending -(으)ㄴ/는 거야 is a very common informal sentence ender used to state a fact or explanation with emphasis. It's a contraction of -(으)ㄴ/는 것이야.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 때문에
meaning: Indicates the reason or cause for a result, meaning 'because of [Noun]' or 'due to [Noun]'.

--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -(으)ㄴ/는 거야
meaning: An informal sentence ending that explains a fact or situation, often with a sense of emphasis or clarification. It translates to 'The thing is...' or 'It's that...'
example: 지금 공부하는 거야. (The thing is, I'm studying right now.)
level: Intermediate

=== [34:40] 근데 7년 동안 연락이 없었어 ===
meaning: But there was no contact for 7 years.
tone: Informal, setting up a hypothetical situation.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: But, however
notes: A very common contraction of 그런데 (geureonde). Used frequently in spoken Korean to start a sentence, change the subject, or connect contrasting ideas. It's more colloquial than 하지만 (hajiman).
--- word ---
term: 7년
type: Noun
breakdown: 7 + 년
meaning: 7 years
notes: 년 (nyeon) is the counter for years. It is used with Sino-Korean numbers (일, 이, 삼...). For age with native Korean numbers, 살 (sal) is used (e.g., 스무 살 - 20 years old).
--- word ---
term: 동안
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: during, for (a period of time)
notes: A dependent noun that indicates a duration. It always follows a noun specifying a period, such as '7년 동안' (for 7 years) or '방학 동안' (during vacation).
--- word ---
term: 연락이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 연락 + 이
meaning: contact (as subject)
notes: 연락 (yeollak) means 'contact' or 'communication'. The particle 이 (i) marks it as the subject of the verb. It's used because 연락 ends in a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 없었어
type: Verb
breakdown: 없다 + -었- + -어
meaning: there wasn't
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말) past tense form of 없다 (eopda - to not exist). The -었- infix indicates the past tense.

=== [35:23] 왕궁을 지켜 ===
meaning: Protect the royal palace.
tone: Informal, playful, declarative.
--- word ---
term: 왕궁을
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 왕궁 + ~을
meaning: The royal palace
notes: The particle ~을 marks the preceding noun (왕궁, royal palace) as the direct object of the verb. It's used after a noun ending in a consonant; ~를 is used after a vowel.

meaning: (I'll) protect
notes: This is the informal, non-past tense conjugation (해체, haeche). In this context, it's a playful declaration of intent, but it could also function as a command ('Protect!') depending on the situation.

=== [35:25] 미안한데 결혼 금지려 ===
meaning: Sorry, but marriage is forbidden.
tone: Informal, joking, declarative.
--- word ---
term: 미안한데
type: Adjective + connective ending
breakdown: 미안하다 + ~(으)ㄴ데
meaning: I'm sorry, but...
notes: The ~(으)ㄴ데 ending is a versatile connector. Here, it introduces a contrasting statement, but it can also be used to provide background information or soften a request. It's extremely common in conversation.
--- word ---
term: 결혼
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Marriage
notes: A standalone noun. The verb 'to get married' is 결혼하다 (gyeolhonhada).
--- word ---
term: 금지려
type: Noun + copula (colloquial)
breakdown: 금지 + ~이야 (colloquial variation)
meaning: is forbidden
notes: This is a very unique, colloquial utterance, likely a playful variation of 금지야 (geumji-ya), which means 'it is a ban'. Such variations are common in casual speech and live streams, showing the speaker's personal style.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V + ~(으)ㄴ/는데
meaning: A connective ending used to provide background, show contrast, or introduce a main clause.
example: 날씨가 좋은데 공원에 갈까요? (The weather is nice, so shall we go to the park?)
level: Intermediate

=== [35:31] 아 근데 전쟁은 진짜 끔찍한 것 같아 ===
meaning: Ah, but war seems truly terrible.
tone: Informal, serious, reflective.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common interjection used to express realization or to introduce a new, often spontaneous, thought.
--- word ---
term: 근데
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그런데
meaning: But / By the way
notes: 근데 is the spoken contraction of 그런데. It is one of the most common words in conversational Korean, used to mean 'but' or to change the subject.
--- word ---
term: 전쟁은
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 전쟁 + ~은
meaning: As for war
notes: The topic particle ~은/는 sets the topic of the sentence. Here, it also creates a slight contrast between the previous lighthearted topic and the new, serious topic of war.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Really / Truly
notes: A very common word in casual speech, used to add emphasis. It's generally considered more informal than 정말 (jeongmal).
--- word ---
term: 끔찍한
type: Adjective (modifier form)
breakdown: 끔찍하다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: Terrible
notes: Korean adjectives must be conjugated to modify nouns. The ~ㄴ ending attaches to the adjective stem (끔찍하~) to modify the following noun (것).
--- word ---
term: 것
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: Thing / Fact
notes: 것 is a dependent noun that requires a modifier. The phrase 'Adjective + ~ㄴ/은 것' means 'a ... thing' or 'the fact that it is ...'.
--- word ---
term: 같아
type: Adjective (conjugated)
breakdown: 같다 + ~아
meaning: it seems
notes: The pattern ~ㄴ/은 것 같다 is used to express an opinion, guess, or impression in a slightly softened, less direct way. It's equivalent to 'I think...' or 'It seems like...'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A + ~(으)ㄴ 것 같다
meaning: Expresses a guess or opinion about a current state or quality. 'It seems / looks like (adjective)'.
example: 이 음식이 매운 것 같아요. (This food seems spicy.)
level: Beginner

=== [35:40] 상상이 안 돼 ===
meaning: I can't imagine it.
tone: Informal, serious, emphatic.
--- word ---
term: 상상이
type: Noun + particle
breakdown: 상상 + ~이
meaning: Imagination
notes: The particle ~이 marks 상상 (imagination) as the subject of the verb 되다. The pattern 'Noun + ~이/가 되다/안 되다' is very common.
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Not
notes: 안 is a negative adverb placed directly before a verb or adjective. It's the most common way to form short negative sentences in spoken Korean. The longer form is ~지 않다.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 되다 + ~어
meaning: it works / becomes
notes: 돼 is a contraction of 되어. The phrase '상상이 안 돼' literally means 'imagination doesn't happen/work', which is an idiomatic way to say 'I can't imagine it'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + ~이/가 안 되다
meaning: An idiomatic expression meaning that the action of the noun is not possible, not working, or not allowed.
example: 와이파이가 안 돼요. (The Wi-Fi isn't working.)
level: Beginner

=== [35:46] 그치? ===
meaning: Right?
tone: Informal, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 그치?
type: Expression
breakdown: 그렇지?
meaning: Right?
notes: A very common contraction of 그렇지? (geureochi?). It is used as a tag question to confirm information or seek agreement from the listener, similar to 'isn't it?' or 'you know?'.

=== [35:54] 지금 행복하게 모든 걸 누리면서 살아야 돼 ===
meaning: We have to live happily now, enjoying everything.
tone: Informal, philosophical, encouraging.
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: 지금 refers to the present moment, 'right now'. It differs from 이제 (ije), which implies a change from the past, meaning 'now that...' or 'from now on'.
--- word ---
term: 행복하게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 행복하다 + ~게
meaning: Happily
notes: The ~게 ending attaches to an adjective stem to turn it into an adverb, describing how an action is performed. It answers the question 'how?'.
--- word ---
term: 모든
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: All / Every
notes: 모든 is a pre-noun determiner that modifies the noun that follows it. It means 'all' or 'every'. For example, 모든 사람 (all people).
--- word ---
term: 걸
type: Noun + particle (contracted)
breakdown: 것 + ~을
meaning: the thing(s)
notes: 걸 is a common spoken contraction of 것을. 것 means 'thing' and ~을 is the object particle. '모든 걸' means 'everything'.
--- word ---
term: 누리면서
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 누리다 + ~(으)면서
meaning: While enjoying
notes: The ~(으)면서 pattern connects two verbs, indicating that the actions are performed simultaneously by the same subject. 누리다 means 'to enjoy' or 'to be blessed with'.
--- word ---
term: 살아야
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 살다 + ~아야 되다
meaning: have to live
notes: This is the first part of the ~아야/어야 되다 grammar pattern, which expresses obligation or necessity ('must', 'have to'). The final 되다/하다 is often separated.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 되다 + ~어
meaning: must
notes: This is the informal conjugation of 되다, completing the '살아야 되다' (sal-aya doeda) pattern. The full phrase means 'we have to live'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)면서
meaning: Indicates two actions happening at the same time by the same subject. 'While doing V...'
example: 영화를 보면서 팝콘을 먹었어요. (I ate popcorn while watching a movie.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아야/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity. 'Must do V' or 'Have to do V'.
example: 숙제를 해야 돼요. (I have to do my homework.)
level: Beginner

=== [35:59] 알았지? 항상 늘 소중히 ===
meaning: Got it? Always, always cherish it.
tone: Informal, commanding yet affectionate, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 알았지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -았- + -지?
meaning: Got it?
notes: The ending -지? is an informal way to confirm something the speaker believes the listener already knows or agrees with, similar to adding '..., right?' in English. It creates a soft, familiar tone and is a contraction of -지요?.
--- word ---
term: 항상
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: always
notes: 항상 (hangsang) and 늘 (neul) both mean 'always' and are often used interchangeably. Using them together, as done here, is a common way to add emphasis, like saying 'always and forever'.
--- word ---
term: 늘
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: always
notes: Similar to 항상 (hangsang). While both mean 'always,' 늘 (neul) can sometimes imply a more continuous or habitual state, whereas 항상 can mean 'on every occasion.' In casual speech, this distinction is often blurred.
--- word ---
term: 소중히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 소중하다 + -히
meaning: preciously
notes: The suffix -히 (-hi) is attached to some adjective stems to turn them into adverbs, like adding '-ly' in English (e.g., 조용하다 'to be quiet' → 조용히 'quietly'). The sentence trails off here, implying a verb like 'keep' or 'cherish'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + -지(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to seek agreement or confirm information that the speaker assumes the listener also knows. It's like adding '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (Oneul nalssi joji?) - The weather's nice today, right?
level: Beginner

=== [36:02] 알겠어 모르겠어 ===
meaning: Do you get it or not?
tone: Informal, playful, demanding an answer.
--- word ---
term: 알겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -겠- + -어
meaning: Do you get it?
notes: The suffix -겠- (-get-) here doesn't indicate future tense but rather conjecture or understanding. 알겠어요? (algesseoyo?) is a very common phrase for 'Do you understand?'. This is its informal (반말) version.
--- word ---
term: 모르겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 + -겠- + -어
meaning: Don't you get it?
notes: This is the direct opposite of 알겠어 (algesseo). Stating a verb and its opposite back-to-back is a common colloquial way to pose a forceful yes-or-no question, like 'Are you going or not?' (갈 거야 말 거야?).

=== [36:03] 알겠어 모르겠어 ===
meaning: Do you get it or not?
tone: Informal, insistent, repetitive for comedic effect.
--- word ---
term: 알겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -겠- + -어
meaning: Do you get it?
notes: Repetition in Korean, especially in casual speech, is frequently used for emphasis, to show insistence, or for rhythmic/comedic effect, as is the case here.
--- word ---
term: 모르겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 + -겠- + -어
meaning: Don't you get it?
notes: The pairing of a positive verb (알다 - to know) and its negative counterpart (모르다 - to not know) creates a forceful question demanding a clear answer.

=== [36:28] 난 오열했지 오열을 했어 ===
meaning: Me? I sobbed. I wailed.
tone: Informal, emphatic, dramatic.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -는
meaning: As for me, I
notes: 난 (nan) is the common spoken contraction of 나는 (naneun). The topic particle -는 (-neun) is used here to emphasize 'me' in response to the fan's question, creating a slight contrast.
--- word ---
term: 오열했지
type: Verb
breakdown: 오열하다 + -했- + -지
meaning: I sobbed, of course
notes: 오열하다 (oyeolhada) is a strong verb meaning 'to wail' or 'sob loudly'. The -지 (-ji) ending here is declarative, stating something as an obvious fact, implying 'Of course I did!'
--- word ---
term: 오열을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 오열 + -을
meaning: wailing
notes: 오열 (oyeol) is the noun form. Many Korean verbs are formed by adding 하다 (hada, to do) to a noun. Repeating the action as 'Noun + 하다' after already stating the verb is a common way to add emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 했어
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + -었- + -어
meaning: did
notes: The phrase 오열을 했어 (oyeoreul haesseo) means the exact same thing as 오열했어 (oyeolhaesseo). Saying both back-to-back is purely for dramatic emphasis.

=== [36:40] 나도 진짜 탈수 올 뻔했어 ===

tone: Informal, hyperbolic, humorous.
--- word ---
term: 나도
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -도
meaning: Me too / I also
notes: The particle -도 (-do) means 'also' or 'too.' It attaches to nouns and pronouns and replaces subject/object particles like -가/이 or -을/를.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really, for real
notes: 진짜 (jinjja) is a very common word in casual speech, used to add emphasis. It is similar to 정말 (jeongmal) but often feels more colloquial.
--- word ---
term: 탈수

breakdown: 
meaning: dehydration
notes: The natural Korean expression for 'to get dehydrated' is 탈수가 오다 (talsuga oda), which literally means 'dehydration comes'.
--- word ---
term: 올

breakdown: 오다 + -ㄹ
meaning: that would come
notes: This is the future modifier form of 오다 (oda, to come). It modifies the following grammatical construction 뻔하다 (ppeonhada).
--- word ---
term: 뻔했어

breakdown: 뻔하다 + -었- + -어
meaning: almost did

meaning: Expresses that an event or action almost happened, but did not. It is always used in the past tense form, -(으)ㄹ 뻔했다.
example: 기차를 놓칠 뻔했어요. (Gichareul nochil ppeonhaesseoyo.) - I almost missed the train.

=== [36:43] 나의 눈물의 시작은 강아지부터야 ===
meaning: The start of my tears was from the dog.
tone: Informal, narrative, explaining a cause.
--- word ---
term: 나의
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -의
meaning: my
notes: 나의 (naui) is the possessive 'my'. In casual conversation, this is almost always contracted to 내 (nae). Using the full form can sound slightly more formal, literary, or emphatic.
--- word ---
term: 눈물의
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 눈물 + -의
meaning: of tears
notes: The particle -의 (-ui) indicates possession or description. Here, it means 'the start of the tears'. The pronunciation of -의 often changes to '에' (e) when used as a possessive particle.
--- word ---
term: 시작은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 시작 + -은
meaning: the start (is)
notes: 시작 (sijak) means 'start' or 'beginning'. The topic particle -은 (-eun) marks 'the start of my tears' as the main topic of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 강아지부터야
type: Noun + Particle + Copula
breakdown: 강아지 + -부터 + -이야
meaning: it's from the dog
notes: The particle -부터 (-buteo) means 'from' and can indicate a starting point in time, space, or, as in this case, cause. The ending -이야 (-iya) is the informal, non-polite form of the verb 이다 (ida, to be).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + -부터
meaning: A particle meaning 'from' or 'starting from.' It can indicate a starting point in time (월요일부터 - from Monday), space (여기부터 - from here), or cause/origin (너부터 - from you).
example: 수업은 9시부터 시작해요. (Sueobeun 9si-buteo sijakaeyo.) - The class starts from 9 o'clock.
level: Beginner

=== [37:04] 눈물 한 방울 툭 떨어지면서 ===
meaning: As a single teardrop fell, plop.
tone: Descriptive, narrative, incomplete sentence.
--- word ---
term: 눈물
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: tear(s)
notes: A compound noun from 눈 (eye) and 물 (water).
--- word ---
term: 한
type: Determiner
breakdown: 하나
meaning: one
notes: The modifier form of the native Korean number 하나 (one), used before a noun or counter. The numbers 하나, 둘, 셋, 넷 change to 한, 두, 세, 네 when modifying nouns.
--- word ---
term: 방울
type: Counter
breakdown: 
meaning: drop
notes: A counter word for drops of liquid. Used with native Korean numbers, e.g., '물 한 방울' (one drop of water).
--- word ---
term: 툭
type: Adverb (Ideophone)
breakdown: 
meaning: plop, with a light thud
notes: An ideophone (의성어/의태어) that mimics the sound or manner of something lightly dropping, breaking, or being touched. It adds a vivid, sensory detail to the description.
--- word ---
term: 떨어지면서
type: Verb
breakdown: 떨어지다 + -면서
meaning: while falling
notes: The verb 떨어지다 means 'to fall' or 'to drop'. The -(으)면서 ending indicates that two actions are happening at the same time. Here, the sentence trails off, but it implies something else happened as the tear fell.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)면서
meaning: Indicates that the actions in the main and subordinate clauses occur simultaneously. The subject of both verbs must be the same. Translates to 'while V-ing'.
example: 음악을 들으면서 숙제해요. (I do homework while listening to music.)
level: Beginner

=== [37:07] 성 밖으로 내쫓아가지고 강아지 이렇게 막 ===
meaning: They chased the dog out of the castle, and the dog was like, you know...
tone: Narrative, descriptive, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 성
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: castle
notes: This is a Sino-Korean word (城). It's used in words like '성벽' (castle wall).
--- word ---
term: 밖으로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 밖 + -으로
meaning: to the outside
notes: 밖 means 'outside'. The particle -(으)로 indicates direction, meaning 'to' or 'towards'. So, '성 밖으로' means 'to the outside of the castle'.
--- word ---
term: 내쫓아가지고
type: Verb
breakdown: 내쫓다 + -아 가지고
meaning: after chasing out, and...
notes: 내쫓다 means 'to kick out' or 'to drive away'. The -아/어 가지고 ending is a colloquial connector that can indicate sequence ('and then') or reason ('because'). Here, it shows the sequence of events.
--- word ---
term: 강아지
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: puppy, dog
notes: Refers to a puppy or a small dog. It's often used more broadly for any dog in an affectionate way, much like 'doggy' in English.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 + -게
meaning: like this
notes: Formed from the adjective 이렇다 (to be like this) and the adverbial suffix -게. It's often used with a gesture to demonstrate something.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, recklessly
notes: A very common colloquial adverb with many nuances. It can mean 'recklessly' or 'carelessly', but here it functions more as a filler word to add emphasis or convey a sense of 'you know' or 'like', indicating the speaker is searching for words.

=== [37:09] 이러고 있을 때 ===
meaning: When it was like this...
tone: Narrative, continuing the previous thought.
--- word ---
term: 이러고
type: Verb
breakdown: 이러다 + -고
meaning: doing this and...
notes: A contraction of 이렇게 하고 (doing like this). 이러다 is a verb meaning 'to do this/like this'. It's part of the V-고 있다 pattern here.
--- word ---
term: 있을
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 있다 + -을
meaning: being
notes: The future/prospective modifier form of 있다 (to be, to exist). It modifies the noun 때 (time).
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun (dependent)
breakdown: 
meaning: when, at the time
notes: A dependent noun that means 'time' or 'moment'. It follows a verb in its modifier form (e.g., V-(으)ㄹ 때, V-(으)ㄴ 때, V-는 때) to mean 'when...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -고 있다
meaning: Expresses a continuing action or state (present progressive). Translates to 'is V-ing'.
example: 지금 밥을 먹고 있어요. (I am eating right now.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Used to indicate the time when an action or state occurs. Translates to 'when...'.
example: 심심할 때 책을 읽어요. (When I'm bored, I read a book.)
level: Beginner

=== [37:11] 이제 줄줄줄 계속 울고 ===
meaning: Then I just kept crying and crying...
tone: Narrative, descriptive, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, then
notes: While it literally means 'now', in narrative contexts like this, it often functions as 'from that point on' or 'then', marking a transition in the story. It's used differently from 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now, at this very moment'.
--- word ---
term: 줄줄줄
type: Adverb (Ideophone)
breakdown: 
meaning: streaming, trickling
notes: An ideophone (의태어) that describes the manner of liquid flowing continuously in a stream. Repeating the word (줄줄 -> 줄줄줄) adds emphasis, vividly painting a picture of tears streaming down her face.
--- word ---
term: 계속
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: continuously
notes: Indicates that an action is ongoing without stopping. Used here with 울고 to emphasize the continuous crying.
--- word ---
term: 울고
type: Verb
breakdown: 울다 + -고
meaning: crying and...
notes: The -고 ending is a versatile connector. It can link actions in sequence ('and then'), list parallel actions, or, as used here at the end of a sentence, create a trailing-off effect, implying the story continues.

=== [37:14] 그러다가 이제 완전 그냥 거의 막 ===
meaning: And then, at that point, it was just totally, like, almost...
tone: Casual, trailing off, using many filler words.
--- word ---
term: 그러다가
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 그렇다 + -다가
meaning: and then, while doing that
notes: The -다가 ending indicates that an action is interrupted by another. 그러다가 (from 그렇다, 'to be so') is a common discourse marker meaning 'and then', 'after that', or 'in the middle of that'.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, then
notes: Functions as a temporal marker, indicating a shift in the narrative timeline to 'at that point'.
--- word ---
term: 완전
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: completely, totally
notes: Originally a noun meaning 'perfection' or 'completeness', it's now very commonly used in colloquial speech as an adverb meaning 'totally' or 'really', similar to '대박' (daebak) or '진짜' (jinjja).
--- word ---
term: 그냥
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, simply
notes: A very frequent adverb in conversation. It can mean 'just' or 'for no particular reason'. Here, it acts as a filler to soften the statement and make it sound more spontaneous.
--- word ---
term: 거의
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: almost
notes: Used to mean 'almost' or 'nearly'. The speaker piles on several adverbs (완전, 그냥, 거의, 막) as she trails off, which is common in natural, unplanned speech.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, like
notes: A colloquial adverb with many uses. Here, it's a filler word that adds a sense of immediacy or emphasis, similar to saying 'like' or 'you know' in English while thinking of what to say next.

=== [37:18] 탈수 올 정도로 오열을 한 거는 ===
meaning: The time I sobbed to the point of dehydration...
tone: Casual, narrative, emphatic. Sets up the specific moment that caused her to cry.
--- word ---
term: 탈수
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: dehydration
notes: A Sino-Korean word (脫水). 탈 (tal) means 'to escape' or 'remove', and 수 (su) means 'water'. It's a medical or formal term, but used here for dramatic effect.
--- word ---
term: 올
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: that comes
notes: This is the future/prospective modifying form of 오다 (to come). It modifies the noun 정도 (degree/extent). So, 탈수 올 정도 means 'the extent to which dehydration would come'.
--- word ---
term: 정도로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 정도 + ~(으)로
meaning: to the point of / to the extent of
notes: A common pattern used to express the degree of an action or state. ~(으)로 is a particle indicating means or manner. It's often paired with a modifying verb, as in ~ㄹ/을 정도로.
--- word ---
term: 오열을
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 오열 + ~을
meaning: sobbing
notes: 오열 (oyeol) is a strong word for 'sobbing' or 'wailing loudly'. It's often paired with the verb 하다 (to do), as in 오열을 하다 (to sob). ~을 is the object particle.
--- word ---
term: 한
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: that did
notes: The past tense modifying form of 하다 (to do). It modifies the noun 거 (thing/fact). So, 오열을 한 거 means 'the fact that I sobbed'.
--- word ---
term: 거는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 것 + ~은
meaning: the thing is...
notes: 거는 is a colloquial contraction of 것은. 것 (geot) means 'thing' or 'fact', and ~은 (eun) is the topic particle. Using ~는/은 at the end of a clause like this often means the sentence is unfinished, with the main point to follow.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 정도로
meaning: Indicates the degree or extent of an action, meaning 'to the point where...' or 'so much that...'.
example: 배가 터질 정도로 먹었어요. (I ate to the point where my stomach could burst.)
level: Intermediate

=== [37:20] 이제 오디세우스가 딱 왔을 때 ===
meaning: When Odysseus finally arrived...
tone: Casual, narrative. Setting the scene for the emotional climax.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, at that point
notes: While it means 'now', 이제 (ije) is often used in storytelling to mark a transition to the next event or a key moment, like 'and then' or 'at that point'.
--- word ---
term: 오디세우스가
type: Proper Noun + Particle
breakdown: 오디세우스 + ~가
meaning: Odysseus (as the subject)
notes: The name 'Odysseus' with the subject particle ~가 (ga), which marks the noun as the one performing the action. ~가 is used after a vowel, while ~이 (i) is used after a consonant.
--- word ---
term: 딱
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: exactly, just at that moment
notes: 딱 (ttak) is a very common adverb that adds a sense of precision, rightness, or perfect timing. It can mean 'exactly', 'perfectly', or 'suddenly'.
--- word ---
term: 왔을
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + ~았~ + ~을
meaning: when (he) came
notes: This is the past tense modifying form of 오다 (to come), used before a noun of time like 때 (ttae). The structure is Verb Stem + ~았/었~ (past tense) + ~을 (modifier).
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: when, at the time of
notes: A dependent noun that means 'time' or 'moment'. It almost always follows a verb in its modifying form (e.g., V~ㄹ/을 때 for future/present, V~(으)ㄴ 때 for past).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: A fundamental pattern used to mean 'when' or 'at the time of' an action or state.
example: 공부할 때 음악을 들어요. (I listen to music when I study.)
level: Beginner

=== [37:35] 그니까 주인 만나있다고 버틴 거 ===

--- word ---
term: 그니까
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그러니까
meaning: so, I mean, that is to say
notes: A very common colloquial contraction of 그러니까 (geureonikka). It's used to elaborate, clarify, or state a reason or conclusion, similar to 'so...' or 'I mean...' in English.
--- word ---
term: 주인
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: owner
notes: Refers to the owner of a pet or an object.
--- word ---
term: 만나있다고
type: Verb phrase
breakdown: 만나다 + ~고 있다 + ~고
meaning: saying it was waiting to meet
notes: This form is grammatically unusual. The speaker's likely intent was 만나려고 (mannaryeogo, 'in order to meet') or 만나겠다고 (mannagetdago, 'saying it would meet'). In speech, this kind of variation can happen. It conveys the idea that the dog's entire state of being (~고 있다) was for the purpose of meeting its owner.
--- word ---
term: 버틴
type: Verb
breakdown: 버티다 + ~ㄴ
meaning: that endured / held on

--- word ---
term: 거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 것
meaning: the fact that / the thing
notes: A contraction of 것 (geot). Here it nominalizes the entire preceding clause '주인 만나있다고 버틴' (the fact that it held on to meet its owner), turning it into the topic of her emotional reaction.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~ㄴ/은 거
meaning: A common way to nominalize a past-tense verb clause, turning it into a noun phrase meaning 'the act of V-ing' or 'the fact that one V-ed'.
example: 어제 영화 본 거 재미있었어. (The movie I watched yesterday was fun.)

=== [37:42] 지금 똥꼬도 그러지 않을까? ===

--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, right now
notes: 지금 (jigeum) refers to the present moment, 'right now'. It's often compared to 이제 (ije), which means 'now' but implies a change from a previous state. For example, '지금 가요' (I'm going now) vs. '이제 가요' (Okay, I'll go now, after not going before).

term: 똥꼬도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 똥꼬 + 도
meaning: Ddong-ggo too
notes: 똥꼬 (Ddong-ggo) is her dog's name, which literally means 'butthole' in Korean. It's used as a funny and endearing pet name. The particle 도 (do) means 'too' or 'also'.
--- word ---
term: 그러지
type: Verb stem
breakdown: 그렇다 + ~지
meaning: being like that
notes: This is the stem of the verb 그렇다 (geureota), meaning 'to be so' or 'to be like that'. It's part of the larger grammatical pattern ~지 않을까? (ji aneulkka?).
--- word ---
term: 않을까?
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 않다 + ~(으)ㄹ까?
meaning: I wonder if not...?
notes: This ending combines the negative auxiliary verb 않다 (anta) with the speculative ending ~(으)ㄹ까? (-(eu)lkka?). Together, ~지 않을까? forms a question expressing supposition, like 'I wonder if...' or 'Don't you think...?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~지 않을까?
meaning: Used to express a guess or supposition, often in the form of a gentle question. It translates to 'I wonder if...' or 'Don't you think...?'.
example: 비가 오지 않을까? (I wonder if it will rain?)

=== [37:55] 똥꼬도 그러고 있는 거 아닌가 모르겠어 ===
meaning: I'm not sure if Ddong-ggo is doing that too.
tone: Casual, speculative, musing out loud.
--- word ---
term: 똥꼬도
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 똥꼬 + 도
meaning: Ddong-ggo too
notes: The particle 도 (do) is attached to a noun to add the meaning of 'also' or 'too'.
--- word ---
term: 그러고
type: Verb
breakdown: 그러다 + ~고
meaning: doing that and...
notes: 그러다 (geureoda) is a contraction of 그렇게 하다 (geureoke hada), meaning 'to do so' or 'to act like that'. It's combined with the ~고 (~go) ending, which connects it to the next verb, 있다 (itda).
--- word ---
term: 있는
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 + ~는
meaning: that is...
notes: This is part of the ~고 있다 (~go itda) pattern, indicating a continuous action (present progressive). The ~는 (~neun) ending turns the verb phrase into an adjective that modifies the following noun, 거 (geo).
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 것 (contraction)
meaning: the thing / the fact that
notes: 거 (geo) is the colloquial contraction of the pronoun 것 (geot), which means 'thing' or 'fact'. It's extremely common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 아닌가
type: Verb
breakdown: 아니다 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: is it not...?
notes: This combines 아니다 (anida), 'to not be', with the questioning ending ~(으)ㄴ가 (-(eu)nga), used to express doubt or wonder. '거 아닌가' means 'I wonder if it's not the case that...'.
--- word ---
term: 모르겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 모르다 + ~겠어
meaning: I don't know
notes: The informal ending for 모르겠다 (moreugetda). While 모르다 (moreuda) means 'to not know', adding ~겠~ (~get~) often softens the statement to 'I'm not sure' or 'I guess I don't know'. The ~어 (~eo) ending makes it informal (반말).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + ~고 있다
meaning: Indicates a present progressive action, equivalent to 'is/are ~ing' in English.
example: 밥을 먹고 있어요. (I am eating a meal.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: ~는 거 아닌가 모르겠다
meaning: A common colloquial phrase used to express uncertainty or speculation. It literally means 'I don't know if it's not the case that...', but functions like 'I wonder if...' or 'I'm not sure if...'.
example: 그 사람이 오고 있는 거 아닌가 모르겠어. (I'm not sure if that person is coming.)
level: Intermediate

=== [37:59] 보여줄게 오늘 자 똥꼬야 ===
meaning: I'll show you. It's today's Ddong-ggo.
tone: Casual, friendly, interactive with the audience.
--- word ---
term: 보여줄게
type: Verb
breakdown: 보여주다 + ~(으)ㄹ게
meaning: I'll show you
notes: 보여주다 (boyeojuda) is a compound verb from 보다 (boda, to see) and 주다 (juda, to give), meaning 'to show'. The ending ~(으)ㄹ게 (-(eu)lge) is used in informal speech to express the speaker's intention or promise to do something for the listener.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: today
notes: A basic noun for 'today'.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: Suffix
breakdown: 
meaning: from (a certain date)
notes: A colloquial suffix attached to a date or time to mean 'as of' or 'from'. '오늘 자' means 'from today' or 'today's'.
--- word ---
term: 똥꼬야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 똥꼬 + ~야
meaning: it's Ddong-ggo
notes: This is the informal, non-polite copula (이다, to be). ~야 (~ya) is used after a noun ending in a vowel. The full phrase means 'It's the Ddong-ggo from today'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb Stem + ~(으)ㄹ게(요)
meaning: An informal ending used to state the speaker's intention or promise to do an action, often for the listener's benefit. It conveys a softer, more personal nuance than ~(으)ㄹ 거예요.
example: 내가 할게. (I'll do it.)
level: Beginner

=== [38:04] 오늘 자 똥꼬야 ===
meaning: It's today's Ddong-ggo.
tone: Casual, repetitive for emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: today
notes: A fundamental vocabulary word for learners.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: Suffix
breakdown: 
meaning: from (a certain date)
notes: The suffix ~자 (~ja) specifies that the item being discussed is from that particular date. It's common in contexts like '오늘 자 신문' (today's newspaper).
--- word ---

meaning: it's Ddong-ggo

=== [38:14] 오늘 자 똥꼬야 ===

tone: Casual, presenting a photo.
--- word ---
term: 오늘
type: Noun

meaning: today

type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: picture

--- word ---
term: 똥꼬야
type: Proper noun + copula
breakdown: 똥꼬 + -이야
meaning: it's Ddong-ggo
notes: 똥꼬 (Ddong-ggo) is her dog's name. -야 (ya) is the informal, non-polite ending for the verb 'to be' (이다, ida) when the preceding noun ends in a vowel. The full form is -이야 (iya), but it's often contracted to -야 (ya) in fast speech. '이건 똥꼬야' (igeon Ddong-ggoya) - 'This is Ddong-ggo'.

=== [38:27] 너무 즐거워 보이지? ===

--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, very, too
notes: 너무 (neomu) is a very common adverb for emphasis. While it traditionally meant 'too much' in a negative sense, in modern colloquial Korean it's frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative contexts, similar to 'so' in English.
--- word ---
term: 즐거워
type: Adjective
breakdown: 즐겁다
meaning: happy, joyful

meaning: looks, right?

--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective-아/어 보이다
meaning: Used to express how something or someone looks or seems. It means 'to look/seem/appear (adjective)'.

=== [38:30] 그니까 ===

breakdown: 
meaning: during, while
notes: A noun that means 'a period of time'. It's often used after a verb ending in -는 to mean 'while (verb)-ing'. For example, '기다리는 동안' (gidarineun dongan) - 'while waiting'.
--- word ---
term: 똥꼬

meaning: Ddong-ggo

pattern: V-는 동안
meaning: Indicates the duration of time during which an action or state occurs. It translates to 'while (verb)-ing' or 'during the time that...'.

=== [39:00] 할로윈이라고 ===

=== [39:05] 이 아련한 눈빛 어쩔 거야 ===
meaning: What am I supposed to do with this wistful gaze?
tone: Informal, admiring, rhetorical question.
--- word ---
term: 이
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: this
notes: A basic determiner used before a noun to specify something close to the speaker. It corresponds to 'this' in English. The other basic determiners are `그` (that, near the listener) and `저` (that, far from both).
--- word ---
term: 아련한
type: Adjective
breakdown: 아련하다 (to be faint/dim/wistful) + -ㄴ (adjective modifier ending)
meaning: wistful / sentimental
notes: The `-(으)ㄴ` ending transforms a descriptive verb (adjective) into a form that can modify a noun. `아련하다` describes a feeling that is faint, distant, sad, and beautiful, often associated with memories or a dreamy look.
--- word ---
term: 눈빛
type: Noun
breakdown: 눈 (eye) + 빛 (light)
meaning: gaze / the look in one's eyes
notes: A compound noun that literally means 'eye-light'. It refers to the expression or emotion conveyed through one's eyes, not just the act of looking.
--- word ---
term: 어쩔
type: Verb (colloquial)
breakdown: Contraction of 어찌할
meaning: what to do about...
notes: This is a very common colloquial contraction of `어찌할` (from `어찌하다`, to do what/how). It's almost always used in phrases like `어쩔 거야?` (What are you gonna do?) or the slang `어쩔 티비` (Whatever).
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: Contraction of 것 + 이야
meaning: (is) the thing
notes: `거야` is the informal, non-polite contraction of `것이야` (`것` meaning 'thing' + `이다` meaning 'to be'). The full phrase `어쩔 거야?` is a rhetorical way of expressing being overwhelmed by something, in this case, the cuteness of the gaze.

=== [39:14] 난 이 눈빛 제일 좋아 ===
meaning: I like this gaze the most.
tone: Informal, declarative, expressing preference.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 (I) + 는 (topic particle)
meaning: I / as for me
notes: A common contraction in casual speech and writing. `나는` is the full form. Using the topic particle `는` emphasizes 'I' as the topic of the sentence, often implying a contrast (e.g., 'As for me, *I* like this one').
--- word ---
term: 이
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: this
notes: Used before a noun to indicate something physically or conceptually close to the speaker.
--- word ---
term: 눈빛
type: Noun
breakdown: 눈 (eye) + 빛 (light)
meaning: gaze
notes: Refers to the expression in someone's eyes. It’s a more poetic or descriptive term than just 'eyes' (`눈`).
--- word ---
term: 제일
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: the most / number one
notes: `제일` is used to form the superlative ('-est' or 'most'). It's often used interchangeably with `가장` in everyday speech, though `제일` can feel slightly more colloquial. Example: `제일 빨라요` (It's the fastest).
--- word ---
term: 좋아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 좋다 (to be good) conjugated in informal present tense
meaning: like / is good
notes: In Korean, 'to like' is expressed with the adjective `좋다` where the thing being liked is the subject of the sentence (e.g., `[This gaze] is good [to me]`). This is different from the verb `좋아하다` (to like), which takes a direct object (e.g., `나는 [이 눈빛을] 좋아해`). Both are correct, but the adjectival form is very common.

=== [39:19] 너무 귀엽지? ===
meaning: So cute, right?
tone: Informal, affectionate, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so / very / too
notes: While textbooks often teach `너무` as 'too' (implying excess), in modern colloquial Korean, it's very frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so' in a positive sense, just like in this sentence.

meaning: Used to confirm information the speaker already assumes to be true, similar to '..., right?' or '..., isn't it?' in English. The informal version is `-지?` and the formal is `-지요?`.

=== [39:27] 귀여워 보고 싶어 ===

term: 귀여워
type: Adjective
breakdown: 귀엽다 (to be cute) conjugated in informal present tense
meaning: cute
notes: This is the informal, non-polite present tense conjugation of `귀엽다`. The `ㅂ` irregular rule applies here: when followed by a vowel, the `ㅂ` changes to `우`, which then combines with `~어` to become `워`.

pattern: Verb Stem + -고 싶다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's desire or wish to do an action. It translates to 'want to (verb)'.
example: 영화를 보고 싶어요. (I want to watch a movie.)
