=== [39:29] 똥꼬 이번에 미용했을 때 ===
meaning: When Ddong-ggo got groomed this time...
tone: Informal, narrative, setting up a story.
--- word ---
term: 똥꼬
type: Proper Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Ddong-ggo
notes: This is the name of Hwasa's dog. The name literally means 'butthole' in Korean and is used as a term of endearment, which fans find funny and charming.
--- word ---
term: 이번에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 이번 (this time) + -에 (time particle)
meaning: this time
notes: The particle `에` is used to mark a point in time. `이번` means 'this current time/occasion', so `이번에` means 'on this occasion' or 'this time'.
--- word ---
term: 미용했을
type: Verb
breakdown: 미용하다 (to do beauty treatment/groom) + -했- (past tense) + -을 (modifier)
meaning: when (he) was groomed
notes: This is part of the `-(으)ㄹ 때` (when) grammar pattern. The verb `미용하다` is put into the past tense (`미용했다`) and then combined with the prospective modifier `-(으)ㄹ` to indicate a past event. `미용했을 때` means 'at the time when he was groomed'.
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: time / when
notes: A noun meaning 'time' or 'moment'. It is very frequently used with a preceding verb clause ending in `-(으)ㄹ` to mean 'when (something happens/happened)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Indicates the time when an action or state occurs. It translates to 'when...'. For past events, the past tense infix `~았/었~` is often inserted before `-(으)ㄹ 때`.
example: 어렸을 때 키가 작았어요. (When I was young, I was short.)
level: Beginner

tone: Informal, seeking agreement, conversational.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: so, too, very
notes: While traditionally meaning 'too much' in a negative sense, in modern colloquial Korean, it's widely used to mean 'very' or 'so' in both positive and negative contexts, much like 'so cute' or 'so hard'.
--- word ---
term: 귀엽지
type: Adjective + Ending
breakdown: 귀엽다 + -지
meaning: cute, right?
notes: The -지 ending is a tag question used to confirm something the speaker believes the listener also knows or agrees with. It's like adding ', right?' or ', isn't it?' in English. This is the informal (반말, banmal) form; the polite form is 귀엽죠 (gwiyeopjyo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-지(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to seek confirmation or agreement on a shared understanding. It softens the statement and makes it more conversational.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (oneul nalssi jochi?) - The weather's nice today, right?
level: Beginner

meaning: looks/must be delicious

=== [40:22] 내가 공원에서 항상 먹는 아이스크림 ===
meaning: The ice cream I always eat at the park.
tone: Informal, descriptive, trailing thought.
--- word ---
term: 내가
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + -가
meaning: I (as the subject)
notes: 내가 is the informal way to say 'I' as the subject. The formal equivalent is 제가 (jega). Note that the informal pronoun 나 changes to 내 when the subject particle -가 is attached.
--- word ---
term: 공원에서
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 공원 + -에서
meaning: at the park
notes: The particle -에서 marks the location where an action takes place. It's different from -에, which usually marks a destination or a static location. Since 'eating' is an action, -에서 is used.
--- word ---
term: 항상
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: always
notes: A common adverb of frequency. Similar in meaning to 언제나 (eonjena).
--- word ---
term: 먹는
type: Verb (adjective form)
breakdown: 먹다 + -는
meaning: that (I) eat
notes: This is a relative clause modifying the noun 'ice cream'. The -는 ending attaches to an action verb stem to turn it into a modifier that describes a noun in the present tense.
--- word ---
term: 아이스크림
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: ice cream
notes: This is the noun being modified by the entire preceding clause '내가 공원에서 항상 먹는'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-는 Noun (Present Tense Relative Clause)
meaning: A structure used to modify a noun with an action verb, creating a descriptive phrase like 'the [Noun] that [someone] [Verbs]'.
example: 제가 읽는 책 (jega ingneun chaek) - the book that I am reading.
level: Intermediate

=== [40:24] 난 피스타치오 맛을 좋아하거든 ===

meaning: pistachio

meaning: I like, you see
notes: The -거든(요) ending provides a reason or background information for a previous or upcoming statement. It has a nuance of 'since you asked' or 'let me tell you'. It's very conversational. The -요 would be added for politeness (좋아하거든요).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-거든(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to provide a reason or background information that the listener may not be aware of. It implies there's more to the story or that this is the reason for something else.

=== [40:44] 미공개 셀카를 보여달라고? ===

breakdown: 미(未) + 공개(公開)

--- grammar ---

meaning: A pattern for quoting a request where the action benefits the speaker. It means 'to ask (someone) to do V for me'.

=== [40:59] 봐볼게 ===
meaning: I'll take a look.
tone: Informal, decisive, responsive.
--- word ---
term: 봐볼게
type: verb
breakdown: 보다 + -아 보다 + -ㄹ게
meaning: I will try looking
notes: This combines two patterns. 'V-아/어 보다' means 'to try doing V'. 'V-(으)ㄹ게' is an informal ending expressing the speaker's intention or promise to do something. The full form is '보아 볼게', which is often spoken and written as '봐 볼게' or '봐볼게'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 보다
meaning: Adds the nuance of 'trying' or 'experiencing' an action. It makes the action sound less definite and more like an attempt.
example: 이 김치를 먹어 봐. (Try this kimchi.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ게
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to state the speaker's intention or will, often in response to the listener or situation. It has a softer nuance than '-(으)ㄹ 거야'.
example: 내가 전화할게. (I'll call.)
level: Beginner

breakdown: 싶다 + -은데
meaning: you want to..., but/and...

=== [41:06] 어떤 스타일 보고 싶은데 ===
meaning: What kind of style do you want to see?
tone: Informal, questioning, engaging with fans.
--- word ---
term: 어떤
type: Adjective
breakdown: 
meaning: what kind of, which
notes: Used before a noun to ask about its nature or to specify one out of several options. It's similar to 'which' or 'what kind of' in English.
--- word ---
term: 스타일
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: style
notes: A loanword from the English word 'style'. It is commonly used in Korean for fashion, hair, music, etc.

type: Verb

meaning: see and...
notes: This is the verb 보다 (boda, 'to see') connected to the next part of the grammar pattern, ~고 싶다 (go sipda, 'to want to').

meaning: Expresses the speaker's desire to do something; 'to want to (verb)'.

meaning: A sentence ending that provides background context for the following clause or question. It can also be used to soften a statement or question, making it less direct.
example: 날씨가 좋은데 공원에 갈까요? (The weather is nice, so shall we go to the park?)

meaning: of doing a fitting
notes: 피팅하다 (pitinghada) is the verb 'to do a fitting'. The ~(으)ㄹ (eul) ending turns the verb into a modifier that describes a future or potential action for the following noun.
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: time, when
notes: Used after a verb modifier (like 피팅할), the pattern 'V-ㄹ/을 때' means 'when (someone) does V'.

type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사진 + ~들 + ~을
meaning: the photos

type: Verb
breakdown: 보여주다 + ~(으)ㄹ까?
meaning: should I show?
notes: 보여주다 (boyeojuda) means 'to show'. The ending ~(으)ㄹ까? (lkka?) is used in informal speech to make a suggestion or ask for the listener's opinion, translating to 'Shall I...?' or 'Should we...?'. The polite form is ~(으)ㄹ까요? (lkkayo?).

meaning: Indicates the time when an action or state occurs. It translates to 'when...'.

--- grammar ---
pattern: V-ㄹ/을까(요)?
meaning: Used to make a suggestion or ask for the listener's opinion about a course of action. Translates to 'Shall I/we...?' or 'Do you think...?'.
example: 같이 저녁 먹을까? (Shall we have dinner together?)
level: Beginner

=== [41:43] 피팅 사진들은 이제 보통 안 보여주잖아 ===

--- word ---
term: 피팅
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: fitting
notes: A loanword from the English 'fitting'. In the context of K-pop, it refers to trying on outfits for performances, photoshoots, or events.
--- word ---
term: 사진들은
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 사진 + ~들 + ~은
meaning: as for fitting photos
notes: The topic particle ~은/는 (eun/neun) is used here to single out 'fitting photos' as the topic of the sentence, implying a contrast with other types of photos.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: While 이제 (ije) literally means 'now', in conversation it's often used as a filler to transition to a new point or introduce a general statement, similar to 'well' or 'so'.
--- word ---
term: 보통
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: usually, normally
notes: A common adverb used to describe what is typical or general. It comes from the Hanja 普通 (botong).
--- word ---
term: 안
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: not
notes: The most common way to negate a verb in Korean. It is placed directly before the verb. It is a shorter form of 아니 (ani).
--- word ---
term: 보여주잖아
type: Verb
breakdown: 보여주다 + ~잖아
meaning: don't show, you know
notes: 보여주다 (boyeojuda) means 'to show'. The ~잖아 (janha) ending is used to state a fact that the speaker assumes the listener already knows, creating a sense of shared understanding. It's like saying '..., you know?' or '..., right?'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V-잖아(요)
meaning: A sentence ending used to confirm or remind the listener of a fact that both parties should be aware of. It creates a friendly, familiar tone.
example: 오늘 금요일이잖아. (It's Friday today, you know.)
level: Intermediate

=== [41:48] 자 한번 볼게요 ===
meaning: Okay, I'll take a look.
tone: Casual, transitional. Announcing her next action.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Well, Okay, Now
notes: A very common filler word used to start a sentence or transition to a new topic, similar to saying "Alright then..." or "Okay..." in English. It helps to get the listener's attention before speaking.
--- word ---
term: 한번
type: Adverb
breakdown: 한 (one) + 번 (counter for times)
meaning: once, one time
notes: While it literally means "one time," 한번 is often used idiomatically to mean "to give something a try" or "to do something briefly." For example, '한번 해 봐' (hanbeon hae bwa) means "Just try it." Here, it softens the action of looking, implying a quick or casual glance.
--- word ---
term: 볼게요
type: Verb
breakdown: 보다 (to see/look) + -(으)ㄹ게요
meaning: I will look
notes: The '-(으)ㄹ게요' ending expresses the speaker's intention or will, often in relation to the listener. It's a softer, more responsive way to state a future action than '-(으)ㄹ 거예요'. It's used in informal speech and implies the decision is being made at the moment of speaking.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + -(으)ㄹ게요
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or promise to do something, often considering the listener's reaction. Used in informal settings.
example: 제가 전화할게요. (Jega jeonhwahalgeyo. - I'll call you.)
level: Beginner

=== [42:03] 어디였냐 ===

=== [42:12] 그래도 한 번씩 딱 입어보고 가져가거든 ===

tone: Casual, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 그래도
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그러하여도
meaning: still, nevertheless, even so
notes: A common conjunction used to introduce a statement that contrasts with or persists despite the previous one. It's a contraction of 그러하여도 (geureohayeodo).
--- word ---
term: 한
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: one
notes: The native Korean number for 'one' used before a counter word like 번 (beon, time/instance).
--- word ---
term: 번씩
type: Adverbial phrase
breakdown: 번 + ~씩
meaning: once each, one at a time
notes: 번 (beon) is a counter for times or occurrences. The particle ~씩 (~ssik) means 'each' or 'apiece,' indicating distribution. Here, it means she tries on each outfit once.
--- word ---
term: 딱
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: exactly, just, perfectly
notes: A mimetic adverb (의태어, uitaeeo) that adds emphasis. Here, it implies the action of trying on is done precisely and decisively, not half-heartedly.
--- word ---
term: 입어보고
type: Verb
breakdown: 입다 + ~어 보다 + ~고
meaning: try wearing and then...
notes: This combines 입다 (ipda, to wear) with the pattern ~어/아 보다 (~eo/a boda), which means 'to try doing something.' The ~고 (~go) ending connects this action to the next one (가져가다, gajyeogada).
--- word ---
term: 가져가거든
type: Verb
breakdown: 가져가다 + ~거든
meaning: I take them, you see
notes: 가져가다 (gajyeogada) means 'to take' or 'to bring.' The ending ~거든 (~geodeun) is used in casual speech to provide a reason or background information for something already known or mentioned. It has a nuance of '...you see,' or 'because...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~어/아 보다
meaning: Indicates trying or attempting an action. It adds a sense of experimentation or experience.
example: 이 김치를 먹어 보세요. (i gimchireul meogeo boseyo.) - Please try this kimchi.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~거든(요)
meaning: A casual sentence ending used to explain a reason or provide background information that the listener might not know. It implies there's more to the story.
example: 어제 못 갔어요. 바빴거든요. (eoje mot gasseoyo. bappatgeodeunyo.) - I couldn't go yesterday. Because I was busy, you see.
level: Intermediate

=== [42:20] 그니까 피팅할 때는 붓기가 쏙 빠져있어가지고 ===
meaning: So, when I do the fitting, the swelling has gone down completely, so...
tone: Casual, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 그니까
type: Adverb
breakdown: 그러니까
meaning: so, that's why
notes: A very common colloquial contraction of 그러니까 (geureonikka). It's used to connect a cause to a result, similar to 'so' or 'therefore' in English, but in a very casual way.
--- word ---
term: 피팅할
type: Verb phrase
breakdown: 피팅하다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: when doing a fitting
notes: This is the future/modifying form of the verb 피팅하다 (pitinghada, to do a fitting), which modifies the following noun 때 (ttae, time).
--- word ---
term: 때는
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 때 + ~는
meaning: as for the time when...
notes: 때 (ttae) means 'time' or 'when.' The particle ~는 (~neun) is a topic marker, highlighting 'the time of the fitting' as the specific context for her statement.
--- word ---
term: 붓기가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 붓기 + ~가
meaning: the swelling
notes: 붓기 (butgi) means 'swelling' or 'puffiness.' The particle ~가 (~ga) is the subject marker, indicating that the swelling is what performs the action of 'going down'.
--- word ---
term: 쏙
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: completely, entirely
notes: A mimetic adverb (의태어, uitaeeo) that describes something being removed or fitting in perfectly. Here, it emphasizes that the swelling has gone down completely, leaving her looking slim.
--- word ---
term: 빠져있어가지고
type: Verb
breakdown: 빠지다 + ~어 있다 + ~어 가지고
meaning: because it is in a state of having gone down
notes: A complex form. 빠지다 (ppajida) means 'to be removed/fall out.' ~어 있다 (~eo itda) indicates a continued state. So, 빠져있다 (ppajyeoitda) means 'to be gone.' The ending ~어 가지고 (~eo gajigo) is a colloquial way to say 'because' or 'and so,' similar to ~어서 (~eoseo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Indicates the time 'when' an action takes place.
example: 공부할 때 음악을 들어요. (gongbuhal ttae eumageul deureoyo.) - I listen to music when I study.
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: A colloquial grammar pattern used to indicate a reason or cause, similar to ~아/어서. It's very common in spoken Korean.
example: 비가 와가지고 집에 있었어. (biga wagajigo jibe isseosseo.) - It was raining, so I stayed home.
level: Intermediate

=== [42:30] 공연할 때마다 다리랑 막 퉁퉁 부어 ===
meaning: Every time I perform, my legs and stuff get all swollen.

--- word ---
term: 공연할
type: Verb phrase
breakdown: 공연하다 + ~ㄹ
meaning: when performing
notes: The future/modifying form of 공연하다 (gongyeonhada, to perform), which modifies the noun 때 (ttae, time).
--- word ---
term: 때마다
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 때 + ~마다
meaning: every time
notes: The particle ~마다 (~mada) means 'every' or 'each.' Combined with 때 (ttae, time), it means 'every time' or 'whenever'.
--- word ---
term: 다리랑
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 다리 + ~(이)랑
meaning: legs and (other things)
notes: The particle ~(이)랑 (~(i)rang) is a colloquial equivalent of 와/과 (wa/gwa) and 하고 (hago), meaning 'and' or 'with.' It's primarily used in speech.
--- word ---
term: 막
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: just, all over, recklessly
notes: A very common colloquial adverb used for emphasis. It doesn't have a precise meaning but adds a nuance of the action happening intensely, uncontrollably, or 'just because.' Here, it emphasizes the severity of the swelling.
--- word ---
term: 퉁퉁
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: puffily, swollenly
notes: An onomatopoeic/mimetic word (의성어/의태어) that vividly describes the state of being very swollen or puffy.
--- word ---
term: 부어
type: Verb
breakdown: 붓다 + ~어
meaning: swells
notes: The informal, present tense conjugation of 붓다 (butda, to swell). This is a 'ㅅ' irregular verb, so the 'ㅅ' is dropped when followed by a vowel, changing 붓어 (buseo) to 부어 (bueo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: N + ~마다
meaning: Means 'every' or 'each' of the preceding noun.
example: 사람마다 생각이 달라요. (sarammada saenggagi dallayo.) - Every person has a different opinion.

=== [42:33] 이제 짧은 바지를 못 입겠어 ===
meaning: Now I can't wear short pants.
tone: Casual, resigned, slightly frustrated.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now, from now on
notes: Refers to the present moment, often implying a change from the past. It's similar to 지금 (jigeum, right now), but 이제 (ije) often carries a sense of transition into a new state.
--- word ---
term: 짧은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 짧다 + ~은
meaning: short
notes: The adjective form of 짧다 (jjalda, to be short), used to modify the noun 바지 (baji, pants).
--- word ---
term: 바지를
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 바지 + ~를
meaning: pants
notes: 바지 (baji) means 'pants.' The particle ~를 (~reul) marks it as the object of the verb 입다 (ipda, to wear).
--- word ---
term: 못
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: cannot
notes: This adverb is used for negation, specifically indicating a lack of ability or an external factor preventing the action. It is placed directly before the verb. Contrast with 안 (an), which indicates a lack of will.
--- word ---
term: 입겠어
type: Verb
breakdown: 입다 + ~겠~ + ~어
meaning: I can't wear / I guess I can't wear
notes: The infix ~겠~ (~get~) can express intention or will, but here it conveys a strong feeling, realization, or conjecture. It's like saying, 'I've reached the point where I just can't wear them anymore.' It adds an emotional, resigned nuance.
--- grammar ---
pattern: 못 + Verb
meaning: Expresses inability to do something ('cannot'). It's different from 안 + Verb, which expresses unwillingness ('do not').
example: 수영을 못 해요. (suyeongeul mot haeyo.) - I can't swim.
level: Beginner

meaning: A connective ending that means 'so' or 'because,' linking a cause (Clause 1) to a result (Clause 2).
example: 피곤해서 일찍 잤어요. (pigonhaeseo iljjik jasseoyo.) - I was tired, so I went to bed early.
level: Beginner

=== [42:47] 이거는 이번에 흠뻑 쉬었대 ===

meaning: As for this...
notes: `이거` is the colloquial form of `이것` (this thing). The topic particle `~는` is attached to introduce the photo she is talking about. In speech, this is often shortened even further to `이건`.
--- word ---
term: 이번에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 이번 + 에
meaning: This time
notes: `이번` means 'this current time/occasion.' The particle `~에` marks a point in time. Common related phrases are `다음에` (next time) and `저번에` (the last time).
--- word ---
term: 흠뻑
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Thoroughly, drenched

--- word ---

breakdown: 쉬다 + ~었~ + ~대
meaning: They say (someone) rested
notes: This is a reported speech form, a contraction of `쉬었다고 해`. However, it is almost certainly a slip of the tongue for `흠뻑쇼였대` (It was the Heumppeok Show), referring to a famous summer water concert in Korea.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~대
meaning: A casual reported speech ending, short for `~다고 해`. It's used to relay information that you heard from someone else.
example: 친구가 오늘 바쁘대. (My friend said they're busy today.)

=== [42:49] 입은 ===

--- word ---
term: 입은

breakdown: 입다 + ~은
meaning: (that was) worn
notes: This is the past adnominal (or adjective) form of the verb `입다` (to wear). It modifies an implied noun, such as `옷` (clothes). The full phrase would be `입은 옷` (the clothes that I wore).

=== [43:21] 분산돼있어가지고 ===
meaning: Because they're all scattered...
tone: Informal, explanatory, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 분산돼있어가지고
type: Verb
breakdown: 분산되다 + ~어 있다 + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: Because it is scattered...
notes: A long, colloquial verb form. `분산되다` (to be scattered) + `~어 있다` (to be in a state of) + `~아/어 가지고` (because). This combination indicates the reason for the next action is the continuing state of the photos being scattered.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~어 있다
meaning: Indicates a continuing state that results from a completed action. It's often translated as 'is/are ~ed.'
example: 창문이 열려 있어요. (The window is open.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A + ~아/어 가지고
meaning: A common spoken grammar pattern meaning 'because' or 'so.' It connects a cause (first clause) to a result (second clause) and is used interchangeably with `~아/어서` in conversation.
example: 늦잠 자가지고 지각했어요. (I overslept, so I was late.)
level: Intermediate

=== [43:23] 어디있어? ===
meaning: Where is it?
tone: Informal, questioning, talking to herself.
--- word ---
term: 어디있어?
type: Interrogative phrase
breakdown: 어디 + 있다 + ~어?
meaning: Where is it?
notes: A standard casual question. `어디` means 'where' and `있다` means 'to be/exist.' The `~어?` ending is informal (반말). In natural speech, the location particle `에` is often omitted from `어디에 있어?`.

=== [43:41] 되게 많은데 ===
meaning: There are so many, but...
tone: Informal, observational, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 되게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Very, really
notes: A colloquial adverb used to emphasize an adjective or another adverb, similar to `아주` or `정말`. It's very common in everyday conversation.
--- word ---
term: 많은데
type: Adjective
breakdown: 많다 + ~은데
meaning: there are a lot, but...
notes: The `~는데` ending provides background information ('There are a lot of them...') while implying a contrast or subsequent thought ('...but I can't find them'). It's a versatile connector used to soften statements or introduce a situation.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V + ~(으)ㄴ/는데
meaning: A connective ending used to provide background context for the next clause, show a soft contrast, or express surprise.
example: 비가 오는데, 우산 있어요? (It's raining, do you have an umbrella?)
level: Beginner

=== [43:42] 어디갔니? ===

meaning: Where did it go?
notes: A casual question. The `~니?` ending is an informal question form often used with people of the same age or younger, or when talking to oneself. It can feel slightly softer or more intimate than the standard `~어/아?` ending.

=== [43:49] 키 늘린 거 아니다 ===
meaning: I didn't stretch my height (in the photo).
tone: Informal, defensive, clarifying.
--- word ---
term: 키
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: height
notes: Refers to a person's physical stature. A common compliment in Korea is '키가 크다' (kiga keuda), meaning 'You are tall.'
--- word ---
term: 늘린
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 늘리다 + -(으)ㄴ
meaning: that was stretched/lengthened
notes: This is the past-tense adjectival form of '늘리다' (to increase, stretch, lengthen). It modifies the following noun, '거'. It's common for people to use photo editing apps to make their legs look longer, which is what she is denying.
--- word ---
term: 거
type: Noun (pronoun)
breakdown: 것 (contraction)
meaning: thing
notes: '거' is the colloquial, spoken contraction of '것' (geot), which means 'thing' or 'fact'. It's extremely common in everyday conversation. '늘린 거' means 'the thing that was stretched'.
--- word ---
term: 아니다
type: Verb (negative copula)
breakdown: 
meaning: to not be
notes: This is the negative equivalent of '이다' (to be). It's used to say 'Noun A is not Noun B'. The sentence structure is 'Noun + 아니다'. Here, it negates the entire preceding phrase '키 늘린 거'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb-(으)ㄴ 것/거
meaning: Used to nominalize a past-tense verb phrase, turning it into 'the thing that was verbed' or 'the act of having verbed'.
example: 어제 먹은 거 (eoje meogeun geo) - The thing I ate yesterday.
level: Beginner

=== [43:57] 길게 찍힌 건가? ===

tone: Informal, speculative, self-questioning.
--- word ---
term: 길게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 길다 + -게
meaning: longly, in a long way

--- word ---
term: 건가?
type: Noun + Ending
breakdown: 거 + -(ㄴ)가?
meaning: Is it that...?
notes: '-(ㄴ/은/는)가?' is a question ending that expresses doubt, curiosity, or a self-directed question. It feels softer than a direct '-(아/어)요?' question. '건가' is a contraction of '것인가'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Adjective Stem + -게
meaning: A common way to form an adverb from an adjective. It means 'in a ... way' or '...-ly'.
example: 예쁘다 (to be pretty) -> 예쁘게 (prettily).
level: Beginner

breakdown: 것 + (이)라서
meaning: because it is...
notes: This is a contraction of 것이라서 (geosiraseo). 거 is the spoken form of 것 (geot, 'thing'). The particle ~(이)라서 (iraseo) means 'because it is (noun)', used here to give a reason. The sentence trails off, but this sets up the reason for her next thought.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + (이)라서
meaning: Used to state that a noun is the reason for something. It attaches to nouns.
example: 학생이라서 돈이 없어요. (haksaeng-iraseo don-i eopseoyo) - Because I'm a student, I don't have money.
level: Beginner

=== [45:10] 신기하다 뭔가 ===
meaning: It's fascinating, somehow.
tone: Reflective, musing.
--- word ---
term: 신기하다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 
meaning: to be fascinating/novel
notes: Used to describe something that is new, interesting, amazing, or cool. It implies a sense of wonder. This is the dictionary form, used here as a statement in informal speech (반말, banmal).
--- word ---
term: 뭔가
type: Pronoun/Adverb
breakdown: 무엇 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: something / somehow
notes: A combination of 무엇 (mueot, 'what') and the ending ~ㄴ가, which adds a sense of vagueness or questioning. It's used when you can't pinpoint exactly why you feel a certain way, similar to 'for some reason' or 'somehow'.

=== [45:11] 나도 공개하는 게 ===
meaning: For me to be revealing them, too...
tone: Self-reflective, elaborating.
--- word ---
term: 나도
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + ~도
meaning: me too / I also
notes: 나 (na) is the informal word for 'I'. The particle ~도 (do) means 'too' or 'also'. She's adding her own perspective to the previous statement.
--- word ---
term: 공개하는
type: Verb (Adjective Form)
breakdown: 공개하다 + ~는
meaning: revealing
notes: The verb 공개하다 (gongaehada) means 'to make public'. The ~는 ending creates a modifier for the following noun, '게'.
--- word ---
term: 게
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 것 + ~이
meaning: the act of... (as subject)
notes: 게 (ge) is a very common spoken contraction of 것이 (geosi), which is the noun 것 (geot, 'thing/fact') plus the subject particle ~이 (i). The phrase 공개하는 게 means 'the act of revealing (them)'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + ~는 게
meaning: A common spoken pattern that nominalizes a verb (turns it into a noun phrase) and marks it as the subject or topic. It's a contraction of V + ~는 것이.
example: 매일 운동하는 게 중요해요. (maeil undonghaneun ge jung-yohaeyo) - Exercising every day is important.
level: Beginner

=== [45:12] 이건 어때? ===
meaning: How about this one?
tone: Inquisitive, engaging.
--- word ---
term: 이건
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 이것 + ~은
meaning: this one (as for this)
notes: A contraction of 이것은 (igeoseun). 이것 (igeot) means 'this thing', and ~은/는 is the topic marking particle. It's used to introduce 'this' as the topic of the question.
--- word ---
term: 어때?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 어떻다 + ~아/어?
meaning: How is it?
notes: The informal question form of 어떻다 (eotteota, 'to be how'). It's a very common and useful phrase for asking for an opinion, like 'What do you think?' or 'How about it?'. The polite form is 어때요? (eottaeyo?).

type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common exclamation used to express a wide range of emotions like surprise, frustration, realization, or pain. Its meaning is heavily dependent on intonation.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Really / For real
notes: Very common in spoken Korean. Can be used as an adverb ('진짜 예뻐요' - It's really pretty) or as a standalone exclamation ('진짜?' - Really?). It's informal; in formal situations, 정말 (jeongmal) is often preferred.

breakdown: 
meaning: Really / So

meaning: (is) scruffy / grimy

meaning: (is) funny

=== [45:23] 본론으로 넘어가볼게요 ===
meaning: I'll get back to the main topic.
tone: Informal but polite, declarative.
--- word ---
term: 본론으로
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 본론 + -으로
meaning: To the main point
notes: 본론 (bollon) means 'main subject' or 'the point.' The particle -으로 (euro) indicates direction. The phrase 본론으로 들어가다 (bollon-euro deureogada) is a common idiom meaning 'to get to the point'.
--- word ---
term: 넘어가볼게요
type: Verb
breakdown: 넘어가다 + -아 보다 + -(으)ㄹ게요
meaning: I will try to move on (to it)
notes: A combination of 넘어가다 (to move on), -아/어 보다 (to try doing), and -(으)ㄹ게요 (I will...). The -(으)ㄹ게요 ending expresses the speaker's intention in a way that is mindful of the listener, making it sound soft and conversational. The -요 makes it polite.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-아/어 보다
meaning: Expresses trying or experiencing an action.
example: 이 김치를 먹어 보세요. (Try eating this kimchi.)
level: Beginner
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)ㄹ게요
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or decision, often in response to the listener. It is polite and conversational.
example: 제가 문을 닫을게요. (I'll close the door.)
level: Beginner

=== [45:25] 아 난 저 아련한 그 똥꼬의 눈빛이 너무 웃겨 ===

notes: An exclamation expressing her amusement and fondness as she looks at the photo.

meaning: I / As for me
notes: A common contraction of 나 (na, 'I' in informal language) and the topic particle 는 (neun). Used frequently in casual speech.
--- word ---
term: 저
type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: That
notes: Used to refer to something that is not near the speaker. Here, it points to the 'look' in the photo she is discussing.
--- word ---
term: 아련한
type: Adjective
breakdown: 아련하다 + -ㄴ
meaning: Wistful / Faint
notes: The adjective form of 아련하다, used to modify the noun 눈빛 (nunbit). It describes a feeling that is faint, distant, sad, or nostalgic. It's a very poetic and common word in lyrics and descriptions.

type: Determiner
breakdown: 
meaning: That / The
notes: Refers to something already mentioned or known to both speaker and listener. Here, it refers to the specific dog in the photo they are looking at.

breakdown: 똥꼬 + -의
meaning: Ddong-ggo's

breakdown: 눈빛 + -이
meaning: The look in (his) eyes
notes: 눈빛 (nunbit) is a compound word from 눈 (nun, 'eye') and 빛 (bit, 'light'). It refers to the expression or look in someone's eyes. The particle -이 (i) marks it as the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So / Very
notes: In modern colloquial speech, it's very frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so' in a positive sense, like '너무 좋아요' (I like it so much).
--- word ---
term: 웃겨
type: Verb
breakdown: 웃기다 + -어
meaning: (is) funny
notes: The informal, non-polite (반말, banmal) conjugation of 웃기다 (to be funny).

=== [45:26] 뭔지 알지? ===
meaning: You know what I mean, right?
tone: Informal, engaging, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 뭔지
type: Pronoun + Verb Ending
breakdown: 뭐 + -ㄴ지
meaning: What it is
notes: A very common contraction of 무엇인지 (mueosinji). The -ㄴ/은/는지 grammar pattern is used to embed a question or an unknown piece of information within a larger sentence.
--- word ---
term: 알지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -지?
meaning: You know, right?
notes: The -지? (-ji?) ending is used in informal speech to confirm something the speaker believes the listener already knows or agrees with. It's similar to adding '..., right?' or '..., don't you?' in English and creates a friendly, inclusive tone.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Clause + -지(요)?
meaning: Used to seek confirmation or agreement from the listener about a fact the speaker assumes is shared knowledge.
example: 오늘 날씨 좋지? (The weather's nice today, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [45:27] 아 이거는 그때다 ===
meaning: Ah, this is from that time.
tone: Informal, reminiscent, realizing something.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah
notes: An exclamation of realization, as if she just remembered when the photo was taken.
--- word ---
term: 이거는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 이것 + 는
meaning: This thing / As for this
notes: 이거 (igeo) is the colloquial, spoken form of 이것 (igeot), which means 'this thing.' It's often shortened further to 이건 (igeon) in speech. The -는 (neun) is the topic marking particle.

term: 그때다
type: Noun + Verb
breakdown: 그때 + -이다
meaning: is that time
notes: 그때 (geuttae) means 'that time' or 'back then.' The verb -이다 (ida, 'to be') is conjugated in the plain form (-다), which is a type of informal speech (반말, banmal). It's often used when making a simple declaration to a close friend or talking to oneself.

=== [45:28] 이거 ===
meaning: This one.
tone: Informal, repetitive, pointing/showing something.
--- word ---
term: 이거
type: Demonstrative Pronoun
breakdown: 이것
meaning: This thing, this one
notes: This is the colloquial contraction of '이것' (this thing). '이것' is a combination of '이' (this) and '것' (thing). In spoken Korean, the contracted form '이거' is almost always used instead of the full written form '이것'. The object particle '를' is often omitted after '이거' in casual speech.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Demonstrative + 것 Contractions
meaning: In spoken Korean, demonstrative pronouns ending in '것' (thing) are commonly contracted. 이것 (this thing) becomes 이거, 그것 (that thing) becomes 그거, and 저것 (that thing over there) becomes 저거. These contracted forms are standard in informal conversation.
example: 이거 주세요. (Please give me this.)
level: Beginner

type: Adverb

=== [46:10] 홍콩 때였나? ===
meaning: Was it from the Hong Kong event?
tone: Informal, self-questioning, trying to recall.
--- word ---
term: 홍콩
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Hong Kong
notes: Refers to a past event or performance that took place in Hong Kong.
--- word ---
term: 때였나?
type: Noun + Copula + Verb Ending
breakdown: 때 + 이다 + ~었~ + ~나?
meaning: Was it the time...?
notes: The ending ~나(요)? is used for self-reflection or when asking a question to oneself. It conveys a sense of wondering or trying to recall something. It's softer than other question endings.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 때
meaning: Indicates the time or occasion of the noun. 'At the time of (Noun)' or 'when (Noun)'.
example: 학생 때 (haksaeng ttae) - 'When I was a student.'
level: Beginner

pattern: Past Stem + ~었/았나?
meaning: A casual, introspective question ending used to wonder about a past event. 'I wonder if...' or 'Was it...?'
example: 그 사람이 왔나? (geu saram-i wanna?) - 'I wonder if that person came.'
level: Intermediate

breakdown: 짧다 + ~았~ + ~다
meaning: was short
notes: The plain form declarative ending (~다) is used here in casual speech (반말). It creates a tone of stating a fact or having a sudden realization, as if talking to oneself.
--- word ---
term: 이때
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이 + 때
meaning: At this time / Back then
notes: A compound word from 이 (this) and 때 (time). Placing it at the end of the sentence is common in spoken Korean for emphasis or as an afterthought.

=== [46:12] 이때 그거 하는데 자다가 나온 거야 ===
meaning: At this time, I was doing that (the fitting), and I had just gotten up from sleeping.
tone: Casual, explanatory, storytelling.
--- word ---
term: 이때
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이 (this) + 때 (time)
meaning: At this time
notes: Refers to a specific point in time being shown or discussed, in this case, the moment the photo was taken. It's formed by combining 이 (this) and 때 (time/moment).
--- word ---
term: 그거
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 그것 (that thing)
meaning: That thing
notes: A common colloquial contraction of 그것 (that thing). In speech, 그거 is used far more often than the full form. Here, it refers to the photo fitting she's talking about.
--- word ---
term: 하는데
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + -는데
meaning: was doing it, and...
notes: The -는데 ending provides background information for the following clause. It connects the action of 'doing that' (the fitting) to the state she was in ('having just woken up'). It's a very versatile connector in Korean.
--- word ---
term: 자다가
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 자다 (to sleep) + -다가
meaning: while sleeping / from sleeping
notes: The grammar pattern V-다가 indicates that an action was interrupted by another. It implies she stopped sleeping in order to 'come out' for the fitting.
--- word ---
term: 나온
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 나오다 (to come out) + -ㄴ (past tense modifier)
meaning: (that) came out
notes: This is the past adjectival form of 나오다 (to come out). It modifies the noun '거' (thing/situation) that follows.
--- word ---
term: 거야
type: Noun + Copula
breakdown: 것 (thing) + -이야 (informal copula)
meaning: it is the case that...
notes: A very common informal sentence ender, contracted from 것 + 이야. The pattern -(으)ㄴ 거야 is used to state or explain a fact in a casual, conversational way. This is part of Hwasa's 반말 (banmal), or informal speech style.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-다가
meaning: Indicates an interruption or shift of action. The action in the first clause is stopped, and a new action begins.
example: 집에 가다가 친구를 만났어. (I was on my way home, and then I met a friend.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: -(으)ㄴ 거야
meaning: An informal sentence ending used to explain a situation or state a fact, often with a nuance of emphasis or realization.
example: 어제 밤새서 피곤한 거야. (It's because I stayed up all night yesterday that I'm tired.)
level: Beginner

=== [46:30] 그래서 뒤통수가 다 눌려있어 ===
meaning: So the back of my head is all pressed down.
tone: Casual, descriptive, slightly amused.
--- word ---
term: 그래서
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: So, therefore
notes: A fundamental conjunction that shows a cause-and-effect relationship. The cause is that she just woke up (previous sentence), and the effect is that her hair is flat.
--- word ---
term: 뒤통수가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 뒤통수 (back of the head) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: The back of the head
notes: 뒤통수 literally means 'back of the head'. The particle -가 marks it as the subject of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 다
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: All, completely
notes: An adverb used to emphasize that the entire back of her head was affected, not just one part.
--- word ---
term: 눌려있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 누르다 (to press) -> 눌리다 (to be pressed) + -어 있다 (to be in a state of)
meaning: is pressed down
notes: This combines the passive verb 눌리다 (to be pressed) with the grammar pattern -어/아 있다, which describes a continuing state. It means her hair is currently in a state of being pressed down as a result of sleeping on it.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-어/아 있다
meaning: Expresses a continuing state that resulted from a completed action. It is often used with passive verbs.
example: 창문이 닫혀 있어요. (The window is closed / in a state of being closed.)
level: Intermediate

=== [46:31] 너무 귀엽지 ===
meaning: So cute, right?
tone: Playful, seeking agreement from fans.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: So, very
notes: In modern colloquial Korean, 너무 is frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so' in positive contexts, although its original meaning was 'too (excessively)'. This positive usage is extremely common in daily conversation.
--- word ---
term: 귀엽지
type: Adjective
breakdown: 귀엽다 (to be cute) + -지 (tag question ending)
meaning: cute, right?
notes: The -지 ending is used to seek confirmation for something the speaker believes the listener will agree with. It creates a friendly, conversational tone, similar to adding 'right?' or 'isn't it?' in English.
--- grammar ---
pattern: A/V-지(요)?
meaning: A sentence ending used to seek confirmation or agreement. The informal version is -지 and the polite version is -지요 (often shortened to -죠).
example: 이 영화 재미있지? (This movie is fun, right?)
level: Beginner

=== [46:32] 나 원래는 이렇게 짧은 치마에 이렇게 입으려고 했었다 ===
meaning: Originally, I had planned to wear it like this with a short skirt.
tone: Explanatory, sharing a behind-the-scenes thought.
--- word ---
term: 나
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: I, me
notes: The informal pronoun for 'I'. Hwasa uses this throughout the live stream, which reflects her casual and close relationship with her fans. The formal equivalent is 저 (jeo).
--- word ---
term: 원래는
type: Adverb + Particle
breakdown: 원래 (originally) + -는 (topic particle)
meaning: Originally...
notes: 원래 means 'originally'. The topic particle -는 adds a nuance of contrast, implying that what she originally planned is different from what actually happened.
--- word ---
term: 이렇게
type: Adverb
breakdown: 이렇다 (to be like this) + -게 (adverbial suffix)
meaning: Like this
notes: An adverb meaning 'in this way' or 'like this'. She is likely referring to the photo she is showing her fans.
--- word ---
term: 짧은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 짧다 (to be short) + -은 (modifier ending)
meaning: short
notes: The adjectival form of 짧다 (to be short), used to modify the noun 치마 (skirt).
--- word ---
term: 치마에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 치마 (skirt) + -에 (particle)
meaning: with a skirt
notes: The particle -에 here indicates the item of clothing that is part of the coordinated outfit. It can be translated as 'with a skirt' or 'paired with a skirt'.
--- word ---
term: 입으려고
type: Verb ending
breakdown: 입다 (to wear) + -으려고 (intending to)
meaning: intending to wear
notes: This is part of the -(으)려고 하다 grammar pattern, which expresses an intention or plan to do something.
--- word ---
term: 했었다
type: Verb
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + -었었- (past perfect tense)
meaning: had done / had planned
notes: The double past tense marker -았/었었- indicates a past action that was completed and is no longer relevant to the present. It emphasizes that she *had* this plan in the past, but it didn't happen or the situation has changed.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-(으)려고 하다
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or plan to do something. 'I intend to...' or 'I'm going to...'.
example: 한국어를 배우려고 해요. (I intend to learn Korean.)
level: Intermediate
--- grammar ---
pattern: V-았/었었다
meaning: The past perfect tense. It describes something that happened and finished in the past, emphasizing its disconnection from the present.
example: 작년에 학생이었어요. (I was a student last year.) vs. 작년에 학생이었었어요. (I was a student last year, but I'm definitely not now.)
level: Intermediate

=== [46:33] 짧은 치마가 좋아 긴 치마가 좋아? ===
meaning: Do you like short skirts or long skirts?
tone: Interactive, asking fans for their opinion.
--- word ---
term: 짧은
type: Adjective
breakdown: 짧다 (to be short) + -은 (modifier ending)
meaning: short
notes: The adjectival form of 짧다 (to be short), modifying 치마 (skirt).
--- word ---
term: 치마가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 치마 (skirt) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: a skirt
notes: The subject particle -가 marks 'skirt' as the subject of the adjective 좋다 (to be good).
--- word ---
term: 좋아
type: Adjective
breakdown: 좋다 (to be good/liked) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: (do you) like
notes: To express 'to like X' in Korean, you can say 'X이/가 좋다' (X is good). This is a common point of confusion for learners who expect to use the verb 좋아하다 (to like) with the object particle 을/를.
--- word ---
term: 긴
type: Adjective
breakdown: 길다 (to be long) + -ㄴ (modifier ending)
meaning: long
notes: The adjectival form of 길다 (to be long). This is an irregular conjugation where the final ㄹ of the stem is dropped before adding -ㄴ.
--- word ---
term: 치마가
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 치마 (skirt) + -가 (subject particle)
meaning: a skirt
notes: The subject particle -가 marks 'long skirt' as the subject of the second part of the question.
--- word ---
term: 좋아?
type: Adjective
breakdown: 좋다 (to be good/liked) + -아 (informal ending)
meaning: (do you) like?
notes: In informal Korean (반말), a question is often formed simply by using a rising intonation at the end of a declarative sentence, without any change in grammar.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + 이/가 좋다
meaning: A common structure to express 'to like (Noun)'. 좋다 is an adjective meaning 'to be good', so the liked item becomes the subject. It is interchangeable with 'Noun + 을/를 좋아하다'.
example: 저는 강아지가 좋아요. (I like puppies.)
level: Beginner

=== [46:34] 그지 진짜 자다 일어나고 있어 ===
meaning: Right? I really look like I just woke up.
tone: Agreeing with fans, self-deprecating humor.
--- word ---
term: 그지
type: Contraction
breakdown: 그렇지 (that's right, isn't it?)
meaning: Right? / I know, right?
notes: A very common colloquial contraction of 그렇지. It's used to agree with someone or to seek agreement. Hwasa is likely responding to fan comments agreeing with her.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Really, for real
notes: A versatile word used constantly in spoken Korean for emphasis, similar to 'really' or 'literally' in English.
--- word ---
term: 자다
type: Verb
breakdown: 자다 (to sleep)
meaning: sleep
notes: The first part of the phrase '자다 일어나다' (to wake up from sleep).
--- word ---
term: 일어나고
type: Verb
breakdown: 일어나다 (to get up) + -고 (and)
meaning: get up and...
notes: The -고 ending connects two verbs sequentially. The set phrase 자다 일어나다 means 'to have just woken up'.
--- word ---
term: 있어
type: Verb
breakdown: 있다 (to be) + -어 (informal ending)
meaning: am
notes: While V-고 있다 usually means '-ing' (present progressive), in this specific idiomatic context, '자다 일어나고 있어' describes her current appearance or state as someone who has just woken up.

=== [46:35] 피팅할 때 맨날 귀찮아가지고 ===
meaning: Because I'm always too lazy during fittings...
tone: Casual, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 피팅할
type: Noun + Verb
breakdown: 피팅 (fitting) + 하다 (to do) + -ㄹ (modifier)
meaning: doing a fitting
notes: 피팅 is a loanword from English 'fitting'. The structure V-ㄹ/을 때 means 'when (verb)'.
--- word ---
term: 때
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: when
notes: A noun meaning 'time' or 'moment'. It is frequently combined with a verb in the -ㄹ/을 modifier form to mean 'at the time of doing X'.
--- word ---
term: 맨날
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Always, every day
notes: A colloquial adverb for 'always'. It's used more frequently in casual speech than its synonym 매일 (maeil).
--- word ---
term: 귀찮아가지고
type: Adjective + Connector
breakdown: 귀찮다 (to be a bother) + -아/어 가지고
meaning: because it's a bother
notes: The -아/어 가지고 ending is a colloquial connector meaning 'because' or 'so'. It's very common in spoken Korean. The sentence trails off, implying a result like '...I don't bother getting ready'.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V/A-아/어 가지고
meaning: A spoken grammar pattern indicating reason or cause, similar to -아/어서. It connects a cause to a result.
example: 피곤해가지고 일찍 잤어. (I was tired, so I went to bed early.)
level: Intermediate

=== [46:36] 피팅서진이 ===
meaning: The fitting photos...
tone: Trailing off, introducing a topic.
--- word ---
term: 피팅서진이
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 피팅 사진 (fitting photo) + -이 (subject particle)
meaning: The fitting photos (are)...
notes: This is a fast, colloquial pronunciation of 피팅 사진이 (piting sajini). The noun 사진 (sajin, 'photo') ends in a consonant, so the subject particle -이 is attached. The sentence is incomplete, as she is likely about to say more about the photos.

=== [46:37] 어딘가 ===
meaning: Somewhere...
tone: Informal, trailing, searching.
--- word ---
term: 어딘가
type: Adverb
breakdown: 어디 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: somewhere
notes: This word is formed by combining the question word 어디 (where) with the particle ~ㄴ가, which adds vagueness or uncertainty. It literally means 'somewhere' or 'someplace.' It can also be used to mean 'somehow' or 'in some respect' when modifying an adjective (e.g., 어딘가 이상해 - 'it's strange somehow'). In conversation, as seen here, it's often used as a filler word when someone is thinking or looking for something, similar to muttering 'Where is it...' or 'Somewhere...' in English.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Interrogative + ~ㄴ가/은가
meaning: Adds vagueness to a question word (who, what, where, when), turning it into an indefinite pronoun (someone, something, somewhere, sometime).
example: 누구 (who) -> 누군가 (someone); 무엇 (what) -> 무언가 (something).
level: Intermediate

=== [47:10] 어딘가 ===
meaning: Somewhere...
tone: Informal, trailing, searching.
--- word ---
term: 어딘가
type: Adverb
breakdown: 어디 + -ㄴ가
meaning: Somewhere
notes: This word combines the question word 어디 (where) with the ending -ㄴ가 to mean 'somewhere' or 'someplace'. This pattern is used to create indefinite pronouns and adverbs, indicating an unspecified person, place, or thing. Other examples include: 누군가 (someone) from 누구 (who), and 무언가 (something) from 무엇 (what).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Interrogative + -(ㄴ)가
meaning: Attaches to question words (like 어디, 누구, 무엇, 언제) to create indefinite pronouns or adverbs, adding a sense of vagueness or uncertainty.
example: 언젠가 다시 만날 수 있을 거야. (We'll be able to meet again someday.)
level: Intermediate

This pattern is used to create indefinite pronouns and adverbs, indicating an unspecified person, place, or thing. Other examples include: 누군가 (someone) from 누구 (who), and 무언가 (something) from 무엇 (what).
--- grammar ---
pattern: Interrogative + -(ㄴ)가
meaning: Attaches to question words (like 어디, 누구, 무엇, 언제) to create indefinite pronouns or adverbs, adding a sense of vagueness or uncertainty.
example: 언젠가 다시 만날 수 있을 거야. (We'll be able to meet again someday.)

breakdown: 어디 + -ㄴ가
meaning: somewhere
notes: This is a combination of the question word 어디 (where) and the suffix -ㄴ가, which adds a sense of vagueness or uncertainty. This pattern is used to create indefinite pronouns and adverbs: 언제 (when) -> 언젠가 (sometime/someday), 누구 (who) -> 누군가 (someone), 무엇 (what) -> 무언가 (something). --- grammar ---

example: 언젠가 다시 만나자. (Let's meet again someday.)

=== [47:52] 어딘가 ===
meaning: Somewhere...
tone: Informal, musing, searching.
--- word ---
term: 어딘가
type: Indefinite pronoun/Adverb
breakdown: 어디 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: somewhere, someplace
notes: This word combines the interrogative pronoun 어디 (where) with the suffix ~ㄴ가. This suffix attaches to question words (like 누구 'who', 뭐 'what', 언제 'when') to create indefinite pronouns, turning a question into a vague 'some-'. For example: 누군가 (someone), 무언가 (something), 언젠가 (sometime). It's often used when the speaker is unsure of the exact location or is speaking generally, as if thinking aloud.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Interrogative + ~(ㄴ)가
meaning: A pattern used to form indefinite pronouns and adverbs. It attaches to question words to mean 'some-'. For example, 'where' (어디) becomes 'somewhere' (어딘가) and 'who' (누구) becomes 'someone' (누군가).
example: 어딘가에서 맛있는 냄새가 나요. (There's a delicious smell coming from somewhere.)
level: Intermediate

--- word ---
term: 어딘가
type: Indefinite pronoun
breakdown: 어디 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: somewhere
notes: Formed by adding the ending ~ㄴ가 to the question word 어디 (where). This pattern creates indefinite pronouns: 뭔가 (something), 누군가 (someone), 언젠가 (sometime). It's used when referring to an unspecified or unknown place. The repetition suggests the speaker is thinking aloud or trying to recall something.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Interrogative + ~(ㄴ)가
meaning: Attaches to question words (like 어디 where, 누구 who, 뭐 what) to create indefinite pronouns that mean 'some-'. It indicates an unspecified person, place, thing, or time.
example: 가방에서 뭔가 떨어졌어요. (Something fell from the bag.)

meaning: Attaches to question words (like 어디 where, 누구 who, 뭐 what) to create indefinite pronouns that mean 'some-'. It indicates an unspecified person, place, thing, or time.
example: 가방에서 뭔가 떨어졌어요. (Something fell from the bag.)
level: Beginner

=== [48:17] 어딘가 ===
meaning: Somewhere...
tone: Informal, thoughtful, trailing.
--- word ---
term: 어딘가
type: Indefinite pronoun
breakdown: 어디 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: somewhere
notes: Formed by adding the ending ~ㄴ가 to the question word 어디 (where). This pattern creates indefinite pronouns: 뭔가 (something), 누군가 (someone), 언젠가 (sometime). It's used when referring to an unspecified or unknown place. The repetition suggests the speaker is thinking aloud or trying to recall something.

=== [48:43] 어딘가 ===
meaning: Somewhere...
tone: Informal, trailing, searching.
--- word ---
term: 어딘가
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 어디 + ~ㄴ가
meaning: somewhere, some place
notes: A combination of the interrogative pronoun 어디 (where) and the particle ~ㄴ가, which adds vagueness or uncertainty. It changes 'where?' to 'somewhere'. It can also be used to pose a rhetorical question to oneself, like 'Where could it be?'. Hwasa repeats it as if searching for something, similar to saying 'Where is it... where is it...' in English.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun/Pronoun + ~(이)ㄴ가
meaning: Adds vagueness or uncertainty to a noun/pronoun, turning a question word into an indefinite one. It can also express a self-directed question.
example: 이게 누구의 것인가? (I wonder whose this is?)
level: Intermediate

=== [48:57] 워터밤 때다 나 워터밤이래 행복쇼 ===
meaning: It's from Waterbomb. No, I mean Waterbomb? It's the Haengbok Show.
tone: Casual, self-correcting, reminiscent.
--- word ---
term: 워터밤
type: Noun (Proper)
breakdown: 
meaning: Waterbomb
notes: This is the Korean transliteration of 'Waterbomb,' a popular summer music festival in South Korea known for its water-themed performances.
--- word ---
term: 때다
type: Noun + Verb
breakdown: 때 (time) + 이다 (to be) -> 때이다 -> 때다
meaning: It's the time of...
notes: A colloquial contraction of 때이다 (ttae-ida). The noun 때 (ttae) means 'time' or 'when,' so this phrase means 'it's from the time of Waterbomb' or 'this is from the Waterbomb event.' The informal copula 이다 (ida) is used.
--- word ---
term: 나
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: Me / I
notes: The informal pronoun for 'I.' Here it's used as a conversational filler to pause and recenter her thoughts before she corrects herself, similar to saying 'Wait, me?' or 'Oh, hold on.'
--- word ---
term: 워터밤이래
type: Noun + Quoted speech ending
breakdown: 워터밤 + 이라고 해 -> 워터밤이래
meaning: Did I say Waterbomb?
notes: A colloquial contraction of 워터밤이라고 해 (woteobamirago hae). The ~(이)라고 하다 pattern is for quoting. Here, she's quoting her own previous thought or statement as a way of questioning and correcting it. It can also mean 'they say it's...'
--- word ---
term: 행복쇼
type: Noun (Proper)
breakdown: 행복 (happiness) + 쇼 (show)
meaning: Haengbok Show (Happiness Show)
notes: This is likely the name of a concert or event she performed at. 행복 (haengbok) means 'happiness.'

=== [49:01] 완전 글래머 할 때야 ===
meaning: That was when I was being super glamorous.
tone: Casual, proud, reminiscent.
--- word ---
term: 완전
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Completely, totally
notes: In colloquial speech, 완전 (wanjeon) is frequently used like 'totally,' 'super,' or 'so' to emphasize an adjective or state. While its formal meaning is 'completely,' its casual usage is much broader.
--- word ---
term: 글래머
type: Noun (Loanword)
breakdown: 
meaning: Glamour / glamorous
notes: This is Konglish (Korean-style English) from 'glamour.' In Korean, it often specifically refers to a person with a curvy, voluptuous figure, but here Hwasa uses it more broadly to describe her overall glamorous vibe at the time.
--- word ---
term: 할
type: Verb (modifier form)
breakdown: 하다 (to do/be) + ~ㄹ (future/prospective modifier)
meaning: that I was doing/being
notes: This is the future adnominal (noun-modifying) form of 하다 (hada). The pattern ~ㄹ/을 때 usually means 'when (subject) will do...', but it can also be used retrospectively to refer to a past event or state, as seen here.
--- word ---
term: 때야
type: Noun + Verb
breakdown: 때 (time) + 야 (informal 'to be')
meaning: it's the time when...
notes: A combination of the noun 때 (ttae, 'time') and the informal, non-polite ending 야 (ya), a form of the copula 이다 (ida, 'to be'). The full phrase ~ㄹ/을 때야 means 'It's the time when...' or 'That was when...'
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + ~(으)ㄹ 때
meaning: Indicates the time when an action or state occurs. Translates to 'when...'
example: 학생일 때 공부를 열심히 했어요. (Haksaeng-il ttae gongbu-reul yeolsimhi haesseoyo.) - I studied hard when I was a student.
level: Beginner

=== [49:11] 이건 싱가폴 때인데 ===
meaning: This one is from Singapore, and...
tone: Casual, explanatory, trailing off.
--- word ---
term: 이건
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 이것 (this thing) + 은 (topic particle)
meaning: This one / As for this
notes: 이건 (igeon) is a common contraction of 이것은 (igeoseun). It introduces 'this [photo]' as the topic of the sentence.
--- word ---
term: 싱가폴
type: Noun (Proper)
breakdown: 
meaning: Singapore
notes: A common colloquial pronunciation and spelling for 'Singapore.' The standard written form is 싱가포르 (singgaporeu).
--- word ---
term: 때인데
type: Noun + Verb ending
breakdown: 때 (time) + 이다 (to be) + ~ㄴ데 (background/contrast ending)
meaning: it was the time of..., but/and...
notes: The ~ㄴ데/은데/인데 ending is very versatile. It can provide background information ('...and...'), show contrast ('...but...'), or gently introduce a following thought. Here, it leaves the sentence unfinished, making the speech sound natural and conversational.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Noun + ~인데 / Verb/Adj + ~ㄴ/은/는데
meaning: A connective ending that provides background context for the following clause. Can mean 'and,' 'but,' or 'so,' depending on the context.
example: 비가 오는데, 우산 있어요? (Biga oneunde, usan isseoyo?) - It's raining, so do you have an umbrella?
level: Intermediate

breakdown: 예전 (old days/past) + 이다 (to be)
meaning: It is the old days

--- word ---
term: 예전
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: The old days, the past
notes: Repeating a word at the end of a sentence is a common conversational habit in Korean used for emphasis. It's like saying, 'This is really old. Ancient.'

=== [49:31] 언니 미안해 지금 나갈게 ===
meaning: Unnie, I'm sorry. I'll get off now.
tone: Apologetic, compliant, informal.
--- word ---
term: 언니
type: Noun
breakdown: 
meaning: Older sister (for a female speaker)
notes: 언니 (unnie) is what a female calls an older female. It can refer to a biological sister, an older female friend, or a senior female colleague. Here, she is likely addressing a female staff member or manager.
--- word ---
term: 미안해
type: Adjective (conjugated)
breakdown: 미안하다 (to be sorry) -> 미안해
meaning: I'm sorry
notes: The informal, non-polite way to say 'sorry' (반말, banmal), used with people you are close to. The polite equivalent is 미안해요 (mianhaeyo) or, more formally, 죄송합니다 (joesonghamnida).
--- word ---
term: 지금
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Now
notes: 지금 (jigeum) means 'now,' referring to this very moment. It is often compared to 이제 (ije), which also means 'now' but often implies a change from the past ('from now on').
--- word ---
term: 나갈게
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 나가다 (to go out/leave) + ~ㄹ게 (ending for expressing intention)
meaning: I will go out / I'll leave
notes: The ~ㄹ/을게 ending expresses the speaker's intention or promise in informal speech. It has a soft nuance, implying the decision is made with the listener in mind. Here, it means 'I'll get off [the live stream] now.'
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb + ~(으)ㄹ게
meaning: 'I will...' Expresses the speaker's intention or promise to do something, often in response to the listener. Used in informal speech (반말).
example: 내가 전화할게. (Naega jeonhwahalge.) - I'll call you.
level: Beginner

=== [49:33] 다음에 또 할게 ===
meaning: I'll do this again next time.
tone: Reassuring, promising, informal.
--- word ---
term: 다음에
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 다음 (next) + 에 (time/location particle)
meaning: Next time
notes: 다음 (da-eum) means 'next,' and the particle 에 (e) marks a point in time. 다음에 is a very common phrase meaning 'next time.'
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Again
notes: 또 (tto) is an adverb that means 'again' or 'also,' indicating the repetition of an action.
--- word ---
term: 할게
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 하다 (to do) + ~ㄹ게 (ending for expressing intention)
meaning: I will do
notes: Using the `~ㄹ게` (~lge) ending, Hwasa is making an informal promise to her fans that she will do another live stream (the implied action of 하다) in the future.

=== [49:42] 또 갈게 알았지? ===
meaning: I'll go now, okay?
tone: Concluding, informal, seeking agreement.
--- word ---
term: 또
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: Again / Then
notes: While 또 (tto) means 'again,' in the context of ending a conversation or departing, it can act as a filler word similar to 'Well then...' or 'So...'. It softens the statement of departure.
--- word ---
term: 갈게
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 가다 (to go) + ~ㄹ게 (ending for expressing intention)
meaning: I'll go / I'm going
notes: A common, informal way to say goodbye. The `~ㄹ게` (~lge) ending softens the announcement of departure, implying 'I'll get going now.' It's friendlier than just saying 가 (ga, 'go!').
--- word ---
term: 알았지?
type: Verb (conjugated)
breakdown: 알다 (to know) + ~았~ (past tense) + ~지? (tag question ending)
meaning: You got it, right? / Okay?
notes: The `~지?` (~ji?) ending is a tag question used to confirm something the speaker assumes the listener knows or agrees with. It's very conversational and makes the tone inclusive, like saying '..., right?' or '..., okay?'
--- grammar ---
pattern: Verb/Adjective + ~지(요)?
meaning: A tag question ending used to seek confirmation or agreement on something the speaker believes is mutually known or obvious.
example: 날씨 좋지? (Nalssi joji?) - The weather's nice, isn't it?
level: Beginner

=== [49:44] 재밌다 안녕 ===
meaning: This was fun. Bye.
tone: Informal, conclusive, friendly.
--- word ---
term: 재밌다
type: Adjective
breakdown: 재미있다
meaning: is fun
notes: This is a common contraction of 재미있다 (jaemi-itda). In spoken Korean, the contracted form 재밌다 (jaemitda) is used far more frequently than the full form. It combines 재미 (jaemi), meaning 'fun' or 'interest', and 있다 (itda), meaning 'to exist' or 'to have'.
--- word ---
term: 안녕
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Bye
notes: 안녕 (annyeong) is a versatile and informal greeting used for both 'hello' and 'goodbye' among close friends or people of the same age or younger. It's the 'banmal' (informal language) equivalent of 안녕하세요 (annyeonghaseyo) and 안녕히 가세요/계세요 (annyeonghi gaseyo/geseyo).

=== [49:46] 자 이제 나는 갈게 ===
meaning: Okay, now, I'm gonna go.
tone: Informal, transitional, signaling departure.
--- word ---
term: 자
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Okay / Well / Now
notes: 자 (ja) is a filler word used to draw attention, signal a transition, or start a new action, similar to 'Okay, now...' or 'Right then...' in English. It's very common in spoken Korean.
--- word ---
term: 이제
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: now
notes: 이제 (ije) means 'now', but it often implies a change from a previous state. It's used when a new situation is beginning. It differs from 지금 (jigeum), which means 'right now' or 'at this moment'. Here, it signals the end of the live stream and the start of her departure.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + 는
meaning: I / as for me
notes: 나 (na) is the informal pronoun for 'I', used with friends or those younger than you. 는 (neun) is the topic particle, indicating that 'I' is the topic of the sentence. In casual speech, this is often contracted to 난 (nan).
--- word ---
term: 갈게
type: Verb
breakdown: 가다 + -ㄹ게
meaning: I will go
notes: The -(으)ㄹ게 (-lge) ending is used to express the speaker's intention or promise to do something. It's used only in the first person ('I' or 'we') and in informal 'banmal' speech. It has a nuance of informing the listener of one's action, making it softer than a simple statement.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -(으)ㄹ게
meaning: Expresses the speaker's intention or promise to the listener. Used in informal speech with first-person subjects.
example: 내가 할게 (naega halge) - I'll do it.
level: Beginner

=== [49:51] 아 알지 나 공항에서 난 바로 넘어가야 돼 ===
meaning: Ah, you know, right? At the airport, I have to transfer right away.
tone: Informal, explanatory, slightly urgent.
--- word ---
term: 아
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Ah / Oh
notes: A common interjection used to express realization, surprise, or understanding.
--- word ---
term: 알지
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -지
meaning: you know, right?
notes: The -지(요) (-ji(yo)) ending is used to confirm information that the speaker believes the listener already knows. It creates a sense of shared understanding. In 'banmal' (informal speech), it's just -지? (ji?).
--- word ---
term: 나
type: Pronoun
breakdown: 
meaning: I
notes: The informal first-person pronoun. Here it's used without a particle, which is common in casual conversation.
--- word ---
term: 공항에서
type: Noun + Particle
breakdown: 공항 + -에서
meaning: at the airport
notes: The particle -에서 (-eseo) marks the location where an action takes place. It's different from -에 (-e), which typically marks a destination or a static location.
--- word ---
term: 난
type: Pronoun + Particle (contracted)
breakdown: 나는
meaning: I
notes: 난 (nan) is the spoken contraction of 나는 (naneun), which is 'I' + the topic particle. Contractions like this are extremely common in everyday conversation.
--- word ---
term: 바로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: right away / immediately
notes: 바로 (baro) emphasizes immediacy. It can also mean 'directly' or 'the very one'.
--- word ---
term: 넘어가야
type: Verb
breakdown: 넘어가다 + -아야
meaning: have to transfer
notes: 넘어가다 (neomeogada) literally means 'to cross over'. In an airport context, it means to transfer or go through to the next gate/flight. This is part of the '-아야/어야 되다/하다' grammar pattern for obligation.
--- word ---
term: 돼
type: Verb
breakdown: 되다
meaning: must / have to
notes: This is the conjugated ending for the grammar pattern -아야/어야 되다 (aya/eoya doeda), meaning 'must' or 'have to'. 돼 (dwae) is a contraction of 되어 (doeeo).
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -아야/어야 되다/하다
meaning: Expresses obligation or necessity, equivalent to 'must' or 'have to' in English.
example: 숙제를 해야 돼요 (sukjereul haeya dwaeyo) - I have to do my homework.
level: Beginner

=== [49:54] 그러니까 절대 멀리 오지 말고 ===
meaning: So please don't come all the way out here.
tone: Informal, cautionary, considerate.
--- word ---
term: 그러니까
type: Conjunction
breakdown: 
meaning: So / Therefore
notes: 그러니까 (geureonikka) is used to provide a reason for a following command, suggestion, or statement. It connects a cause (what was said before) to a result (this sentence). It's stronger than 그래서 (geuraeseo) and often used when giving instructions.
--- word ---
term: 절대
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: never / absolutely
notes: 절대 (jeoldae) is a strong adverb that almost always accompanies a negative verb. It emphasizes that something should absolutely not be done. Using it with a positive verb is grammatically possible but very rare.
--- word ---
term: 멀리
type: Adverb
breakdown: 멀다 + -리
meaning: far
notes: 멀리 (meolli) is the adverbial form of the adjective 멀다 (meolda), 'to be far'. The -리 (-ri) suffix is one way to turn certain adjectives into adverbs.
--- word ---
term: 오지
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + -지
meaning: don't come
notes: This is the verb stem 오다 (oda), 'to come', combined with -지 (-ji), which is part of the negative command form -지 말다 (-ji malda).
--- word ---
term: 말고
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 말다 + -고
meaning: don't do... and...
notes: This is the negative command verb 말다 (malda) connected with -고 (-go). The pattern 'A-지 말고 B' means 'Don't do A, and do B instead'. Here, the sentence trails off, but the implied meaning is 'Don't come, and (just stay home/rest).'
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -지 말다
meaning: A negative command, used to tell someone not to do something. It's the equivalent of 'Don't...'.
example: 걱정하지 마세요 (geokjeonghaji maseyo) - Don't worry.
level: Beginner

=== [49:56] 나는 진짜 바로 넘어가야 되니까 ===
meaning: Because I really have to transfer right away.
tone: Informal, emphatic, explanatory.
--- word ---
term: 나는
type: Pronoun + Particle
breakdown: 나 + 는
meaning: I / as for me
notes: 나 (na) is the informal 'I'. 는 (neun) is the topic particle. Using the full form instead of the contracted 난 (nan) can add slight emphasis.
--- word ---
term: 진짜
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: really / truly
notes: 진짜 (jinjja) is a very common word in colloquial Korean, used to add emphasis, similar to 'really' or 'for real'. It can also be used as a noun meaning 'the real thing'.
--- word ---
term: 바로
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: right away
notes: 바로 (baro) emphasizes that the action must happen immediately, without delay.
--- word ---
term: 넘어가야
type: Verb
breakdown: 넘어가다 + -아야
meaning: have to transfer
notes: Part of the '-아야/어야 되다/하다' pattern expressing obligation. 넘어가다 (neomeogada) means 'to cross over' or 'transfer'.
--- word ---
term: 되니까
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 되다 + -(으)니까
meaning: because I have to
notes: The -(으)니까 (-eunikka) ending indicates a reason or cause. It's often used when the main clause (which came before this sentence) is a command or suggestion. It explains the reason for her telling fans not to come to the airport.
--- grammar ---
pattern: Clause 1 + -(으)니까 + Clause 2
meaning: Indicates that Clause 1 is the reason or basis for Clause 2. Often used when Clause 2 is a command, suggestion, or proposal.
example: 비가 오니까 우산을 가져가 (biga onikka usaneul gajyeoga) - Because it's raining, take an umbrella.
level: Beginner

=== [49:59] 너무 괜히 고생해서 오지 말고 ===
meaning: Don't go through all the trouble to come for no reason.
tone: Informal, considerate, pleading.
--- word ---
term: 너무
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: too / so / very
notes: 너무 (neomu) is an adverb of degree. While it traditionally meant 'too (much)', in modern colloquial Korean it's frequently used to mean 'very' or 'so', similar to 아주 (aju) or 정말 (jeongmal).
--- word ---
term: 괜히
type: Adverb
breakdown: 
meaning: needlessly / in vain
notes: 괜히 (gwaenhi) implies that an action is pointless, unnecessary, or will lead to a negative or regrettable outcome. Here, it suggests that if fans come, their effort will be wasted because she can't meet them anyway.
--- word ---
term: 고생해서
type: Verb
breakdown: 고생하다 + -아/어서
meaning: by going through trouble
notes: 고생하다 (gosaenghada) means 'to have a hard time' or 'to go through hardship'. The -아/어서 (-a/eoseo) ending here indicates the manner in which the following action is performed: 'don't come *by way of* suffering'.
--- word ---
term: 오지
type: Verb
breakdown: 오다 + -지
meaning: don't come
notes: The verb stem 오다 (oda), 'to come', as part of the negative command form -지 말다 (-ji malda).
--- word ---
term: 말고
type: Verb Ending
breakdown: 말다 + -고
meaning: don't... and...
notes: The sentence trails off again, reinforcing the gentle but firm request. The speaker is showing concern for her fans' well-being, a common aspect of the idol-fan relationship.

=== [50:01] 알았지? ===
meaning: Got it?
tone: Informal, seeking confirmation, affectionate.
--- word ---
term: 알았지?
type: Verb
breakdown: 알다 + -았- + -지?
meaning: Got it? / Understood?
notes: A very common phrase to confirm if the listener has understood and agreed to what was said. It's composed of 알다 (alda, 'to know'), the past tense infix -았- (-at-), and the confirming question ending -지? (-ji?). It's informal and friendly.
--- grammar ---
pattern: V + -았/었지?
meaning: Used to confirm a past action or a state that the speaker assumes the listener is aware of or has just become aware of. It translates to 'You did X, right?' or 'You understood, right?'.
example: 어제 영화 봤지? (eoje yeonghwa bwatji?) - You saw the movie yesterday, right?
level: Intermediate

=== [50:03] 안녕 잘자고 ===
meaning: Bye, sleep well.
tone: Informal, final, warm.
--- word ---
term: 안녕
type: Interjection
breakdown: 
meaning: Bye
notes: The informal 'banmal' (informal language) farewell used among close friends.
--- word ---
term: 잘자고
type: Verb Phrase
breakdown: 잘 + 자다 + -고
meaning: sleep well and...
notes: 잘 (jal) means 'well'. 자다 (jada) means 'to sleep'. The connective ending -고 (-go) is used here to make the farewell sound softer and less abrupt, as if trailing off. The full phrase is often just 잘 자 (jal ja), but adding -고 is common in casual speech.

=== [50:05] 굿나잇 ===
meaning: Good night.
tone: Informal, closing, affectionate.
--- word ---
term: 굿나잇
type: Interjection / Loanword
breakdown: 
meaning: Good night
notes: This is the Korean transliteration of the English phrase 'Good night.' It's commonly used in casual, modern speech, especially online or among friends. The native Korean equivalents are '안녕히 주무세요' (annyeonghi jumuseyo) in formal situations or '잘 자' (jal ja) in informal ones.

=== [50:13] 바이바이 ===
meaning: Bye bye.
tone: Informal, final closing, cute.
--- word ---
term: 바이바이
type: Interjection / Loanword
breakdown: 
meaning: Bye bye
notes: A casual and friendly way to say goodbye, transliterated from the English 'Bye bye.' It often sounds softer or cuter than the standard informal Korean '안녕' (annyeong). It's very common in everyday conversation, especially when ending a phone call or a live stream like this.
